Keywords: ReaDirect, automatic speech recognition, oral reading, Grade 1 learners, DepEd ARAL Program

DEFINITION OF TERMS 
This section presents the key terms used in the study to provide clarity and consistency in understanding the concepts, processes, and technologies involved in the system. The researchers defined the operational and technical terms according to how they are applied in the development and evaluation of the AI-based oral reading coach. 
Operational Terms 
The operational terms refer to the roles, processes, and reading-related concepts as applied in the study. 
Admin 
Refers to the system user responsible for managing the overall platform, including user accounts, system settings, and data monitoring. 

Adaptive Feedback 
Refers to system-generated responses that adjust based on the learner’s performance by providing corrective guidance, reinforcement, and support. 

ARAL Program 
Refers to a government initiative designed to address learning gaps through structured literacy interventions. 

Learner/User 
Refers to the primary user of the system, specifically Grade 1 learners who use the AI reading coach to practice and improve their reading skills. 

Oral Reading Fluency 
Refers to the ability of a learner to read text accurately, quickly, and with proper expression. 

Parent User 
Refers to a secondary user who monitors the learner’s progress and supports reading practice at home. 

Phonological Awareness 
Refers to the ability to recognize and manipulate sounds in spoken words. 

Pronunciation Accuracy 
Refers to the degree to which a learner correctly produces the expected sounds of words. 

Reading Comprehension 
Refers to the ability to understand and interpret the meaning of a text. 

Teacher User 
Struggling Readers 
Scaffolded English Oral-Reading Support 
Refers to educators who use the system to monitor learner progress, analyze performance data, and provide additional instructional support. 
Refers collectively to Grade 1 learners classified as Low Emerging, High Emerging, or Developing based on official Filipino CRLA results. The term indicates the school’s overall reading intervention need, while ReaDirect participants were limited to Developing readers. 
Refers to ReaDirect’s structured support for Grade 1 Developing readers through guided activities, voice assistance, corrective feedback, repeated practice, module placement, and teacher monitoring. It helps learners progress in English reading based on performance and supplements, rather than replaces, Filipino foundational reading instruction. 

Technical Terms 
The technical terms refer to specialized concepts, methods, and technologies used in the development and operation of the system. 
Artificial Intelligence 
Refers tos the simulation of human intelligence in machines that are programmed to perform tasks such as learning, reasoning, and decision-making. 

Automatic Speech Recognition 
Refers to a technology that converts spoken language into text by processing audio input and is used in the system to evaluate learners’ oral reading. 

Beam Search Decoding 
Refers to an algorithm used in speech recognition to determine the most probable sequence of words or phonemes from multiple possible outputs. 

Isolated-Letter Classifier 
Refers to the supervised audio-classification component that predicts an A–Z label and confidence values for isolated-letter responses. 

Grapheme-to-Phoneme (G2P) 
Refers to a process that converts written text into its corresponding phonetic representation. 

Goodness of Pronunciation (GOP) 
Refers to a scoring method used to evaluate how closely a learner’s pronunciation matches the expected phoneme output. 

Levenshtein Distance 
Refers to a metric used to measure the difference between two sequences by counting the number of edits required to transform one sequence into another. 

Phoneme 
Refers to the smallest unit of sound in a language that distinguishes one word from another. 

Progressive Web Application 
Refers to a web-based application that functions like a mobile app and supports offline or low-connectivity usage. 

Voice Activity Detection 
Refers to a method used to detect the presence of speech within an audio signal. 

Word Error Rate (WER) 
Refers to a metric used to measure reading accuracy by comparing spoken words to the expected text and identifying errors. 

Words Correct Per Minute (WCPM) 
Refers to a measure of reading fluency based on the number of correctly read words within one minute. 

16 kHz Audio 
Refers to audio resampled to 16,000 samples per second. In the system, this format is used to make learner recordings compatible with the Whisper speech-recognition pipeline. 

Audio Preprocessing 
Refers to the preparation of learner audio before speech recognition, including audio loading, mono conversion, 16 kHz resampling, duration checking, silence detection, volume checking, and clipping inspection. 

Audio Quality Metadata 
Refers to technical information generated from a learner recording, such as duration, loudness, silence ratio, clipping ratio, speech segments, retry flags, and uncertainty indicators. 

Audio Quality Validation 
Refers to the process of checking whether a learner recording is usable before scoring, so that silence, low volume, clipped audio, or very short recordings are not treated as pronunciation errors. 

Argmax 
Refers to the operation that selects the highest-scoring output symbol from the model logits at a given time step during decoding. 

Contextual Embedding 
Refers to a learned speech representation whose meaning is influenced by nearby sounds and surrounding audio information. 

Corrected Transcript 
Refers to the transcript produced after evidence-based expected-centric correction when the raw ASR output is safely related to the expected answer. 

Classifier Confidence 
Refers to the probability or confidence value assigned to the isolated-letter classifier’s predicted A–Z label. 

Displayed Transcript 
Refers to the transcript selected for learner-facing display after the system chooses whether to show the raw transcript or the safely corrected transcript. 

Embedding 
Refers to a learned numerical representation of speech information that allows the model to process audio patterns in a computational form. 

Expected Phonemes 
Refers to the target phoneme sequence for the expected answer, generated through the custom letter map, CMU Pronouncing Dictionary, or grapheme-to-phoneme conversion. 

Expected-Centric Correction 
Refers to a correction method that uses the known expected answer and supporting evidence before accepting a corrected transcript. 

Audio Feature Extractor 
Refers to the component that converts a standardized audio waveform into numerical features used by the isolated-letter classifier or supporting pronunciation analysis. 

Greedy Decoding 
Refers to a decoding strategy that selects the highest-scoring token at each time step without exploring alternative output paths. 

Inference 
Refers to the process of running a trained model on a new learner audio recording to produce outputs such as transcripts, phoneme evidence, or metadata. 

Input Tensor 
Refers to the normalized numerical audio values passed into a speech-processing model for inference. 

Logit 
Refers to a raw unnormalized model score before probability conversion, showing the model’s preference for a possible output symbol. 

Logits Matrix 
Refers to the table of raw model scores across all time steps and vocabulary symbols or phoneme symbols during ASR decoding. 

Observed Phonemes 
Refers to the phoneme sequence decoded from the learner’s audio by the phoneme-support model. 

Phoneme Alignment 
Refers to the process of matching expected and observed phoneme sequences to identify matches, substitutions, deletions, and insertions. 

Phoneme Evidence Scores 
Refers to sound-level scores produced by the supporting pronunciation-analysis component before normalization and comparison. 

Phoneme Evidence 
Refers to sound-level information generated from expected and observed phoneme sequences to support pronunciation comparison and safe transcript correction. 

Raw Transcript 
Refers to the direct text output generated by Whisper before expected-centric correction is applied. 

Sentence Alignment 
Refers to the process of comparing the expected sentence with the learner transcript to identify correct words, substitutions, deletions, insertions, and missing words. 

Time Step 
Refers to one position in the model output sequence that corresponds to a processed portion of the audio signal rather than a full word. 

Transcript Correction 
Refers to the process of checking and adjusting ASR output using expected answers, known confusions, letter aliases, text similarity, phoneme evidence, and safety rules. 

Encoder-Decoder Transformer 
Refers to the Whisper architecture that converts audio features into contextual representations and generates transcript tokens for longer responses. 

Output Token 
Refers to a text unit generated by the ASR model, such as a word piece, punctuation mark, or special control token. 

Whisper Processor 
Refers to the preprocessing and decoding component used to prepare audio input and convert Whisper outputs into readable transcript text. 

Word Alignment 
Refers to the mapping between expected words and recognized ASR words during sentence or passage reading evaluation. 

CHAPTER I 
INTRODUCTION 
Literacy is a fundamental skill that serves as the foundation of a child’s academic development, particularly during the early years of education. In the Philippine basic education system, Grade 1 is a critical stage where learners are expected to develop essential reading competencies such as phonological awareness, letter–sound recognition, word decoding, and proper pronunciation. These foundational skills are necessary for learners to progress successfully throughout Key Stage 1 and beyond. 
Reading proficiency among early grade learners remains a persistent concern in basic education, as many students continue to experience difficulty in acquiring foundational reading skills. This concern is reflected in literacy assessments such as the Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment (CRLA) and the Philippine Informal Reading Inventory (Phil-IRI), where learners may be identified as requiring additional support or placed under lower reading profiles. In the study of Cañete and Eswan (2025), a significant number of Grade 1 learners experienced reading difficulties, with many requiring structured instructional supports, showing that reading challenges remain an unresolved issue that requires continuous support and intervention. 
To address these learning gaps, the Philippine government implemented Republic Act No. 12028, also known as the Academic Recovery and Accessible Learning (ARAL) Program, with DepEd Order No. 18, Series of 2025 providing structured guidelines for literacy intervention. The program emphasizes a progressive approach to reading development, guiding learners from basic to more complex reading skills. 
Despite these initiatives, challenges remain in the actual implementation of reading interventions. Teachers often handle large classes and multiple responsibilities, making it difficult to provide individualized attention and sufficient guided practice in pronunciation, fluency, and comprehension. 
Advancements in educational technology have introduced new ways to support reading development. The ICOMPREHEND system developed by Perci et al. (2024) demonstrates how artificial intelligence can enhance reading through automated question generation and recognition technologies. By integrating models such as the T5 Transformer and ResNet50V2, the system generates contextually relevant comprehension questions while adapting to learners’ cognitive and emotional states, resulting in improved engagement and learning performance. 
This highlights the growing potential of AI-driven systems in delivering adaptive feedback and personalized learning experiences. While such systems focus on comprehension, similar approaches can be applied to early literacy by supporting pronunciation, reading accuracy, and fluency through real-time feedback and guided practice. 
In line with these developments, this study proposes the development of an AI-based speech recognition-assisted oral reading coach. The system is designed to provide scaffolded English oral reading support for Grade 1 learners who have enough foundational readiness to participate in guided English reading activities. In this study, the intended learners are those profiled as Developing readers through ReaDirect's English CRLA-style reading profile, which is adapted from the structure of CRLA categories for English reading support and is not presented as an official Grade 1 CRLA English instrument. Through the integration of speech recognition and artificial intelligence, the study aims to support learners' reading accuracy, fluency, pronunciation, and comprehension through real-time error detection, phoneme-level analysis, and adaptive feedback mechanisms. 
Background of the Study 
Reading difficulties among learners remain a persistent issue in the Philippine education system, particularly in the early grades where foundational literacy skills are expected to be developed. As part of the preliminary study, the researchers coordinated with the DepEd Santa Cruz Sub-Office to further understand the reading situation of elementary learners and identify a suitable school for the proposed pilot implementation. Based on the available comparative assessment data, the DepEd Santa Cruz Sub-Office endorsed Duhat Elementary School as the most suitable research locale because it demonstrated the greatest documented need for reading intervention among the schools considered. 
The available Grade 1 assessment data showed that 89.10% of the assessed learners in Duhat Elementary School were classified as struggling readers, representing the highest documented proportion among the schools included in the comparison. For research-locale selection, the term struggling readers was used collectively to describe learners whose reading profiles indicated a need for reading intervention. The term was used only as a school-level indicator of literacy need and was not treated as a separate official CRLA classification. The comparative data therefore supported the endorsement of Duhat Elementary School as an appropriate setting for the proposed study. 
Following the endorsement, the researchers conducted an interview with the principal of Duhat Elementary School regarding the literacy levels of the learners. Based on the interview, the principal stated that many students continue to struggle in reading, as reflected in their Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment (CRLA) and Philippine Informal Reading Inventory (Phil-IRI) results. These learners experience difficulties in basic literacy skills such as phonological awareness, word decoding, vocabulary, and reading fluency. Because Grade 1 learners are still beginning English reading, the researchers recognized that learners in the earliest reading profiles may first require stronger Filipino foundational reading support before participating in English oral reading activities. The researchers also asked whether a digital artificial intelligence-based system could help improve the efficiency of reading assessment, monitoring, and support, to which the principal agreed. 
In addition, the researchers conducted an interview with the Curriculum Implementation Division Office Chief to further understand the literacy concerns and possible direction of the proposed system. The chief suggested that the study should focus on Grade 1 learners, as this level serves as a critical foundation for developing reading skills needed in the succeeding grade levels. The researchers also asked whether the ARAL Program could be utilized as part of the implementation context of the proposed system, and the chief agreed. These preliminary interviews strengthened the need for a digital reading support system while also clarifying that ReaDirect should function as a supplementary English reading support tool for learners with sufficient foundational readiness, rather than a replacement for Filipino foundational reading remediation. 
Although the school-level data demonstrated a high proportion of struggling readers, the proposed English oral reading intervention was not intended for all learners under this broader group. Learners in the earliest reading profiles may first require more intensive Filipino foundational reading support before participating in guided English oral reading activities. For this reason, the present study narrows its intervention scope to Grade 1 learners profiled as Developing readers through the ReaDirect English CRLA-style reading profile. These learners still require scaffolded support but possess sufficient foundational readiness to participate in guided English pronunciation, word recognition, sentence reading, oral reading fluency, and comprehension activities. Thus, the proportion of struggling readers established the school-level need for reading intervention, while the availability of Developing readers established Duhat Elementary School’s suitability for the specific scope of ReaDirect. 
To address these concerns, schools have implemented various reading interventions focusing on essential literacy components such as oral language, phonics, vocabulary, fluency, and comprehension. Programs aligned with national initiatives, such as the ARAL Program, aim to provide structured and progressive reading support. However, these approaches often rely heavily on teacher-led instruction, which may not fully address the diverse needs of learners, especially in large classroom settings where individualized attention is limited. For this reason, the present study narrows its intervention scope to Grade 1 learners profiled as Developing readers, since they still need scaffolded support but have enough readiness to participate in guided English oral reading tasks. 
With the advancement of technology, innovative approaches to reading intervention have emerged. The ICOMPREHEND system developed by Perci et al. (2024) demonstrates how artificial intelligence can be utilized to enhance reading comprehension through automated question generation and recognition technologies. By incorporating models such as the T5 Transformer and ResNet50V2, the system was able to generate meaningful comprehension questions and adapt to learners’ cognitive and emotional states, leading to improved engagement and learning outcomes. 
Similarly, the use of Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) has shown effectiveness in supporting oral reading development. ASR-based systems are capable of analyzing learners’ speech, detecting pronunciation errors, and providing immediate feedback. This allows learners to engage in repeated practice while enabling teachers to monitor progress more efficiently. Such capabilities highlight the potential of technology in delivering individualized learning experiences and addressing limitations in traditional classroom instruction. 
These developments emphasize the importance of integrating artificial intelligence into early literacy support. By combining structured reading programs with intelligent systems, it becomes possible to provide adaptive, data-driven support that enhances reading accuracy and fluency for learners who are ready for guided English oral reading practice. 
Given these circumstances, the present study focuses on the design and development of an AI-based speech recognition-assisted oral reading coach aligned with the ARAL Program context. The system aims to address existing gaps in English reading support for Grade 1 Developing readers by offering real-time feedback, personalized learning support, and progressive skill development, ultimately contributing to improved English oral reading practice among Grade 1 learners. 
Through ReaDirect, the researchers aim to support teachers by providing a digital tool that can assist in assessing learner speech, monitoring English reading progress, and recording support-related data more efficiently. The system is not intended to replace teacher instruction, but to serve as a supplementary reading support tool that can guide learners through repeated practice and corrective feedback. In this way, the study responds to the need for a practical, technology-assisted support system within the context of the DepEd ARAL Program. 
Research Problem 
Grade 1 learners under the DepEd ARAL Program may experience difficulties in foundational reading skills such as pronunciation accuracy, word recognition, oral reading fluency, and reading comprehension. However, the Grade 1 CRLA used in the school context is administered in Filipino, and learners in the Low Emerging and High Emerging profiles may first need stronger Filipino foundational reading support before participating in English-based oral reading activities. To keep the study aligned with its English reading support scope, this study focuses on Grade 1 learners profiled as Developing readers through a ReaDirect English CRLA-style reading profile. ReaDirect does not claim to be an official Grade 1 CRLA English tool; instead, it adapts the structure of CRLA reading profiles to guide English oral reading support, feedback, module placement, and teacher-guided progress monitoring. 
Specifically, this study seeks to answer the following questions: 
How can a ReaDirect English CRLA-style oral reading profile module be designed to support Grade 1 learners classified as Developing readers in English reading activities? How can an AI-based speech recognition component be developed to capture and process learners' spoken English reading responses using Automatic Speech Recognition, audio quality validation, and phoneme-level analysis? How can a rule-based scoring and reading-profile process be implemented to identify Developing readers and support English oral reading progress monitoring without claiming to directly implement an official Grade 1 CRLA English instrument? How can ReaDirect provide immediate, child-friendly feedback and guided English reading practice to help Developing readers improve pronunciation, word recognition, fluency, and comprehension without replacing teacher instruction? How can a teacher dashboard be developed to present learner English reading results, Developing-reader profile indicators, common pronunciation or reading difficulties, and progress records for monitoring and support planning? How can the usability, acceptability, and functional performance of ReaDirect be evaluated as a supplementary English reading support tool for Grade 1 learners under the ARAL Program context? Research Objectives 
This study aims to design, develop, and evaluate ReaDirect, an AI-based speech recognition-assisted oral reading coach intended to provide scaffolded English reading support for Grade 1 learners profiled as Developing readers through a ReaDirect English CRLA-style reading profile, using learner speech analysis, corrective feedback, guided practice, and teacher-guided progress monitoring. 
To design a ReaDirect English CRLA-style oral reading profile module for Grade 1 learners classified as Developing readers, focusing on English pronunciation, word recognition, oral reading fluency, and comprehension readiness. To develop an AI-based speech recognition component that captures and processes learners' spoken English reading responses using Automatic Speech Recognition, audio quality validation, and phoneme-level analysis. To implement a rule-based scoring and reading-profile process that supports the identification and monitoring of Developing readers without treating the tool as a direct official Grade 1 CRLA English instrument. To provide immediate, child-friendly feedback and guided English reading activities that help Developing readers practice difficult sounds, words, and sentences without replacing teacher instruction. To develop a teacher dashboard that presents English reading assessment results, Developing-reader profile indicators, common pronunciation or reading difficulties, and progress records for monitoring and support planning. To evaluate the usability, acceptability, and functional performance of ReaDirect as a supplementary English reading support tool for Grade 1 learners within the ARAL Program context. Research Framework 
This section presents the concepts, guidelines, and issues from related studies. The researchers also present the theoretical framework that serves as the foundation for the conceptual framework of ReaDirect as an English oral reading support system for Grade 1 Developing readers. 
Theoretical Framework 

Figure 1. Intelligent Agent Theory from Comşa et al. (2011) 
The study is anchored on Intelligent Agent Theory, which explains how an intelligent system perceives information from its environment, processes it using a defined rule set, and performs appropriate actions based on the given conditions. In this framework, the agent receives input through perception, applies rules and learning mechanisms, and produces an action that affects the environment. This theory supports the proposed system because ReaDirect functions as an intelligent English reading support system that receives learner responses, analyzes oral reading performance, and provides assessment results, feedback, and scaffolded support based on the learner's reading profile. Comşa et al. (2011) explained that an intelligent agent uses perceived conditions to select proper rules and transfer the result back to the environment through action. 
This theoretical foundation is further supported by studies on Artificial Intelligence in Education, particularly intelligent tutoring systems. Létourneau et al. (2025) described intelligent tutoring systems as AI-equipped learning environments that can detect, understand, and adapt to learner progress by monitoring performance, identifying difficulties and errors, and adjusting learning content according to the learner's needs. This is aligned with ReaDirect because the proposed system is designed not only to present reading tasks, but also to assist in identifying English oral reading difficulties, supporting individualized guided practice, and guiding Developing readers toward appropriate learning activities. Through this, the system applies the intelligent agent concept in an educational setting where learner performance becomes the input, system analysis becomes the decision process, and feedback or support placement becomes the resulting action. 
The framework also considers technology-supported reading intervention as a basis for the instructional component of the system. Nurmahanani (2023) emphasized that reading difficulties require intervention programs that address several reading components, including phonemic awareness, vocabulary mastery, reading fluency, and reading comprehension. This supports ReaDirect's scaffolded module approach because the proposed system provides structured digital English reading activities that can help Developing readers practice specific literacy skills while allowing teachers to monitor their progress more efficiently. 
In relation to the assessment component, the study is also supported by automatic speech recognition-based oral reading assessment. Van der Velde et al. (2025) found that ASR-based oral reading fluency assessment can generate observable and reliable reading measures, including word decoding and passage reading scores. Their study emphasized that oral reading performance may be assessed through accuracy, speed, and automaticity, which are important indicators of reading fluency. This supports the use of speech recognition in ReaDirect, where learner speech is captured, transcribed, and analyzed to assist in evaluating English oral reading performance. However, since ASR systems still require optimization and error analysis, the proposed system is positioned as a reading support and decision-assistance tool rather than a complete replacement for teacher judgment. 
Overall, the theoretical framework shows that ReaDirect operates as an intelligent agent-based English reading support system. The learner's oral reading response serves as the input from the environment; the system processes this input through speech recognition, scoring rules, and learner-performance analysis; and the system produces actions such as feedback, English reading profile indicators, support placement, and teacher monitoring. Through the integration of Intelligent Agent Theory, intelligent tutoring systems, technology-supported reading intervention, and ASR-based reading assessment, the proposed system is theoretically grounded as a digital tool that supports early English reading practice, learner feedback, and teacher-assisted monitoring. Therefore, the theoretical framework strengthens the basis of ReaDirect as a system that receives learner input, processes reading performance, and responds through scaffolded support while keeping the teacher's role central in guiding early reading development. 
This connection shows that ReaDirect follows the intelligent agent process by transforming learner speech input into meaningful reading support actions that can be reviewed and guided by the teacher. 
Conceptual Framework 


Figure 2. Conceptual Framework 
The conceptual framework of the study illustrates the overall structure and interaction of the components within the proposed AI-assisted oral reading coaching system. As shown in the framework, the input begins with learner’s voice recording and expected English text input, such as letter, word, phrase, or short sentence. These inputs are processed through the ReaDirect ASR Transcript Generation Engine, which includes audio preprocessing, audio analysis, expected text preparation, context preparation, transcript processing, and final transcript selection. The output is a reviewable transcript result that may include raw, normalized, corrected, and aligned transcript forms, which support learner feedback, teacher monitoring, and scaffolded English oral reading practice for Grade 1 learners classified as Developing readers. 
Scope and Limitations of the Study 
This study focuses on the design and development of an AI-based speech recognition-assisted oral reading coach for Grade 1 learners classified as Developing readers through a ReaDirect English CRLA-style reading profile. The target group is limited to Developing readers because they represent the middle reading profile: they have moved beyond the earliest emerging stage but still need scaffolded support in English pronunciation, word recognition, oral reading fluency, and comprehension. 
The study does not cover Low Emerging and High Emerging learners as direct participants for English reading intervention because these learners may first require stronger Filipino foundational reading support before English oral reading tasks. Transitioning and At Grade Level learners are also not the primary focus because they require less scaffolded support than Developing readers. 
The CRLA-related method used in this study is not treated as a direct copy of the official DepEd Grade 1 CRLA or as an official Grade 1 CRLA English version. Rather, it is a ReaDirect English adaptation that uses the idea of reading profiles to guide placement and monitoring within the system. The study includes the evaluation of the system using selected Grade 1 learners, teachers, and school stakeholders, and it gathers user feedback to determine the system's usability, acceptability, and functional performance as a supplementary learning tool. 
The system integrates Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), isolated-letter audio classification, and phoneme-level analysis to evaluate oral reading performance and generate metrics such as Word Error Rate (WER) and Words Correct Per Minute (WCPM). The study is limited to English-based reading tasks involving letters, words, simple sentences, short passages, and comprehension items aligned with ARAL-informed materials. It does not cover Filipino reading remediation, advanced comprehension, writing, or critical reading. 
Additionally, background noise, variations in children's speech, accent differences, short utterances, and limitations in available speech datasets may affect the system's performance. The study also has a short evaluation period and serves as a supplementary learning support tool rather than a replacement for teacher instruction or teacher judgment. 
Significance of the Study 
This study aims to generate theoretical, practical, and technological contributions through the development and evaluation of ReaDirect, an AI-based speech recognition–assisted oral reading coach designed to support Grade 1 Developing readers. The study is significant because it addresses the need for a school-based English oral reading support tool that can assist learners in pronunciation practice, diagnostic reading activities, module-based support, final reassessment, and teacher-guided progress monitoring. The results of this research may serve as a useful reference for educators, school personnel, researchers, and future developers interested in AI-assisted early literacy support. Specifically, the study is expected to benefit the following: 
Grade 1 Developing Readers . This study may benefit Grade 1 learners classified as Developing readers by providing scaffolded English oral reading support through guided pronunciation, word recognition, sentence reading, passage reading, and comprehension activities. Since these learners still require support but already have enough readiness to participate in guided English reading tasks, ReaDirect can help them practice reading in a structured and interactive way. The system provides immediate feedback, repeated practice opportunities, and child-friendly guidance through the learner interface and virtual reading agents, helping learners become more engaged in English reading activities. 
Classroom Teachers . This study may assist classroom teachers by providing a digital tool for organizing learner reading records, diagnostic results, module progress, reassessment scores, and transcript review. ReaDirect does not replace teacher instruction, but it supports teachers by reducing the manual burden of checking repeated oral reading attempts and by presenting learner performance data in a more organized manner. Through the teacher dashboard, teachers can monitor learner progress, review common reading difficulties, and use the system-generated records as support for planning follow-up instruction. 
Reading Coordinators and School Administrators . This study may help reading coordinators and school administrators by demonstrating how a school-based AI-assisted reading support system can organize learner performance data, reading classifications, module progress, and reassessment outcomes. The system can provide a structured view of how Developing readers respond to English oral reading support activities. This may help school personnel identify possible areas for improvement in reading support implementation, teacher monitoring, and learner progress documentation. 
DepEd ARAL Program Implementers. This study may provide a localized example of how technology can support early-grade reading intervention activities in a school setting. ReaDirect is not presented as a replacement for official DepEd assessment tools or teacher-led intervention, but as a supplementary English reading support system inspired by structured reading assessment and intervention principles. The study may help inform future discussions on how AI-assisted tools, teacher dashboards, and digital reading records can support learners who need scaffolded English reading practice. 
Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence Education Research. This study contributes to computing research by applying automatic speech recognition, Whisper-based speech processing, expected-centric transcript correction, phoneme evidence, rule-based scoring, and learner-feedback mechanisms in an early-grade reading support system. The study demonstrates how AI techniques can be integrated into a functional educational technology prototype that supports learner assessment, feedback, progress tracking, and system-based reading workflows. It may also serve as a reference for future research on child-centered ASR, AI-assisted literacy tools, and explainable scoring mechanisms in educational systems. 
Future Developers and Researchers . This study may benefit future developers and researchers who plan to improve or extend AI-assisted reading support systems. The design of ReaDirect can serve as a reference for developing systems that combine learner audio recording, ASR processing, transcript correction, reading-task scoring, teacher monitoring, and child-friendly feedback. Future researchers may use this study as a basis for improving the ASR model, expanding the learner sample, conducting longer implementation periods, comparing different learner groups, or validating the system in other schools. 
Duhat Elementary School . This study may benefit Duhat Elementary School by providing an initial school-based implementation of ReaDirect with Grade 1 Developing readers. The system may support the school’s reading-related activities by giving learners additional English oral reading practice and by helping teachers observe learner progress through digital records. The results of the study may also help the school identify practical considerations for using AI-assisted educational technology in early-grade reading support. 
CHAPTER II 
REVIEW OF RELATED LITERATURE AND STUDIES 
This chapter presents the related literature and related studies that support the development of ReaDirect, an AI-based speech recognition-assisted oral reading coach for Grade 1 learners. The chapter is organized into two major parts. The Related Literature (RRL) discusses concepts, technologies, frameworks, and supporting references relevant to artificial intelligence, automatic speech recognition, transcript evaluation, audio preprocessing, feedback design, and existing literacy systems. The Related Studies (RRS) presents empirical works, system evaluations, experiments, surveys, and research findings related to reading improvement, pronunciation assessment, child speech recognition, and educational technology support. 
The Related Literature section includes conceptual, technical, and review-based sources that explain the foundation of the present study. These sources provide background on artificial intelligence in language education, automatic speech recognition, text similarity, voice activity detection, scoring approaches, child-friendly feedback, and existing speech-supported literacy systems. 
Artificial Intelligence in Language Education 
Recent studies have highlighted the growing role of artificial intelligence (AI) in language education. Zhu and Wang (2025) conducted a systematic review of 125 research articles, identifying key AI tools such as automated writing evaluation systems, chatbots, machine translation, and automatic speech recognition, which are mainly used in writing and speaking instruction. They concluded that AI technologies are increasingly essential in language education, though future research should focus on diversity, equity, inclusion, and long-term impacts. Dennis (2024) examined AI-powered speech recognition for improving English pronunciation and speaking skills among EFL learners and found significant improvements in students' abilities, with positive feedback on the technology’s flexibility and motivational benefits. Similarly, Létourneau et al. (2025) reviewed AI-driven tutoring systems in K-12 education, showing positive effects on learning outcomes, but also called for more evidence through larger and longer-term studies. These findings suggest that while AI holds great potential for enhancing language learning, further research is needed to fully assess its long-term impact. 
Speech Technologies and Automatic Speech Recognition 
Recent studies show that speech technologies like speech-to-text, text-to-speech (TTS), and automatic speech recognition (ASR) are becoming more important for helping with pronunciation and reading assessment. Pavan Kumar et al. (2023) explored speech-to-text and text-to-speech using deep learning, laying the groundwork for systems that turn spoken words into text and generate speech to support learning and accessibility. Their work is technically detailed but mainly explains the technology rather than how well it works for reading assessment. On the other hand, Kouidri (2023) focused on education and looked at how TTS and ASR affect the pronunciation of English regular past “-ed” among A1 EFL learners, using a pretest-posttest control-group design. The results showed that students who used TTS and ASR improved in phonological awareness, perception, and production. This suggests these tools can help with pronunciation practice, self-assessment, and keeping learners engaged. In a similar way, Bachiri et al. (2024) added AI-based speech recognition to Morocco’s Teaching at the Right Level reading assessment program. They found that students assessed with AI speech recognition made more progress in reading than those tested with traditional paper methods. Together, these studies support using ASR in reading and pronunciation systems because it allows for automated assessment, instant feedback, and more engaging, learner-centered practice. Still, the research points out that ASR-based assessment needs careful validation to make sure it is accurate, fair, and reliable, especially for young or beginner readers. 
Prabhavalkar et al. (2023) further described end-to-end speech recognition as a process in which spoken audio is converted into text through computational models that learn the relationship between acoustic input and output sequences. This supports the present study because ReaDirect relies on ASR to transform learner recordings into readable transcripts before the system applies expected-answer matching, scoring, and feedback. However, the study mainly discussed ASR architectures and recognition performance, while the present study applies ASR as part of a child-centered oral reading assessment and intervention flow. 
Text Similarity and Transcript Evaluation 
Text similarity methods are essential for systems that compare learner responses or automatic speech recognition (ASR) transcriptions with reference answers. For example, Po (2020) demonstrated that identifying similar sentences is valuable in search engines, language processing, machine translation, and document organization. Po implemented the Levenshtein Distance algorithm, which quantifies sentence similarity by analyzing differences in spelling or meaning between words. This approach is particularly advantageous in educational contexts, where student responses may include minor spelling errors or transcription inaccuracies. Similarly, Berger, Waterman, and Yu (2021) examined the application of Levenshtein Distance for comparing sequences, such as strings of letters or words, and observed that although their research focused on biological data, the underlying principles are relevant to ASR transcript comparison. Since ASR converts speech into sequences of words or characters, these techniques support the identification of discrepancies. Janardhana Rao et al. (2024) assessed Levenshtein Distance alongside other similarity measures, including Sequence Matcher, Fuzzy-Wuzzy, and spaCy, for multilingual text comparison, concluding that Levenshtein Distance achieved the highest accuracy. Collectively, these studies suggest that using Levenshtein Distance and related algorithms in ASR-based reading assessments improves the detection of near-correct responses, even when minor spelling or transcription errors are present. In the present study, these techniques are anticipated to support fairer scoring by recognizing near-correct answers and minor mistakes rather than penalizing only exact mismatches. 
Li et al. (2024) also emphasized the importance of post-ASR error correction, explaining that ASR outputs may contain substitutions, insertions, or deletions when speech conditions differ from the data used to train the system. Their work supports ReaDirect’s expected-centric correction layer because learner responses should not be judged only through raw transcripts; instead, likely ASR errors must be checked before scores are assigned. The difference is that Li et al. focused on cross modal ASR correction, while the present study applies correction to early-grade reading tasks with teacher oversight and child-friendly feedback. 
Audio Preprocessing, Voice Activity Detection, and Noise Reduction 
Voice Activity Detection (VAD) plays a significant role in ASR-based systems because it identifies the speech portions of an audio signal while filtering out silence, pauses, and background noise. Recent studies have emphasized VAD as an essential preprocessing component for speech-related tasks, particularly Automatic Speech Recognition. Dinkel et al. (2021) proposed a teacher-student training approach to improve VAD in noisy real-world conditions, while Xu et al. (2021) developed a lightweight online VAD framework suitable for real-time speech processing. Other studies have improved VAD through deep neural networks, neural architecture search, low-SNR optimization, and lightweight real-time models. For instance, Rho et al. (2022) applied neural architecture search to VAD, Zhu et al. (2023) proposed a robust lightweight VAD algorithm for low signal-to-noise environments, and Jia et al. (2024) introduced MagicNet for real-time VAD with reduced model cost. Recent works also show the importance of personalized and ASR-integrated VAD, as Ding et al. (2022) optimized personal VAD for on-device speech recognition, Bovbjerg et al. (2024) used self-supervised pretraining for robust personalized VAD, and Lin et al. (2026) fused VAD with target-speaker ASR. These studies support the integration of VAD in the present ASR-based reading assessment system because it can help determine when learners should speak, detect valid speech segments, reduce failed audio submissions, handle short or silent recordings, and improve the reliability of transcript generation before scoring learner responses. 
Language Transfer and Literacy Support 
The researchers examined the impact of mother tongue transfer on Chinese students’ English reading and writing by reviewing existing theories and empirical studies on language transfer. The findings revealed that first language skills significantly influence second language literacy, with strong phonological and syntactic abilities in the mother tongue positively contributing to English reading comprehension and word recognition. The study also found that similarities between the first and second language result in positive transfer, while differences may lead to errors in writing and grammar. Overall, the results indicate that the mother tongue serves as a foundation for second language development, where strong L1 proficiency enhances L2 literacy, while weak L1 skills may limit effective learning of English. (The Impacts of Mother Tongue Transfer on Chinese Students’ English Reading and Writing, Xie, 2023) 
Teacher Workload and Technology Adoption 
The researchers conducted a systematic research synthesis on teacher workload, work intensification, and time poverty across school settings. Existing studies on workload and intensification were reviewed to identify common patterns and to understand how added duties and administrative tasks affect teachers’ capacity to perform core teaching roles and implement new educational programs. Results showed that intense workload pressures and time constraints reduced teachers’ attention to teaching-related activities and hindered the adoption of new strategies and technologies, underlining the negative impact of overload on innovation in classrooms. (Workload, work intensification and time poverty for teachers and school leaders: a systematic research synthesis, S. Creagh, G. Thompson, N. Mockler, M. Stacey, & A. Hogan, 2023) 
Speech-to-Text / ASR in Reading Assessment 
Stoian et al. (2020), Gyulyustan et al. (2024), and Hirai and Kovalyova (2023) focused on the use of speech-to-text and Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) in different areas of speech processing, transcription, and pronunciation assessment. Stoian et al. (2020) studied ASR pretraining for low-resource speech-to-text translation and found that it can help improve performance when only a small amount of training data is available. In a related study, Gyulyustan et al. (2024) tested the accuracy of selected speech-to-text services using different types of text and recording conditions. Their study showed that ASR accuracy may change depending on the system used, the speaking environment, background noise, and the quality of the recorded audio. Meanwhile, Hirai and Kovalyova (2023) examined how speech-to-text applications can be used to assess the pronunciation of English language learners by comparing ASR results with human ratings. Overall, these studies show that ASR can be useful in language assessment, but its accuracy still depends on several factors that must be considered when using it in an educational system. 
Yashina, Rubanik, and Chukhray (2022), Chen et al. (2023), and Ngo and Chen (2025) further support the use of ASR-related technologies in educational and speech-based assessment systems. Yashina et al. (2022) discussed the design of speech-to-text software for higher education, emphasizing that speech recognition can support learning by transforming spoken input into usable text, although recognition quality may still be affected by pronunciation clarity, background noise, and technical conditions. Chen et al. (2023) introduced BLASER, a text-free evaluation metric for speech-to-speech translation, and highlighted the limitation of evaluation methods that depend heavily on ASR output, since inaccurate transcription may affect the reliability of assessment results. Meanwhile, Ngo and Chen (2025) examined Google Read Along as an ASR-based reading tutor for young EFL learners and showed that ASR-supported reading tools can provide oral reading practice and immediate feedback. These studies are relevant to ReaDirect because they strengthen the basis for using ASR in learner audio processing, transcript evaluation, and automated reading support. However, they also show that ASR-based learning systems must be carefully validated to ensure that feedback and scoring remain accurate, fair, and appropriate for young learners. 
Automatic Speech Recognition for Pronunciation Assessment 
Pronunciation-based learning systems require a careful use of speech recognition. Cámara-Arenas et al. (2023) explained that Automatic Speech Recognition and automatic pronunciation assessment may seem similar because both deal with spoken language, but their purposes are different. ASR is concerned with identifying the words spoken by the learner, while pronunciation assessment examines whether those words were produced clearly and accurately. This difference is important because a learner’s response may still be understood by the system even if the pronunciation is not fully correct. In this case, the transcript alone may not reflect the learner’s actual speaking performance, which shows the need for correction and feedback in pronunciation assessment. 
In relation to this, Evers and Chen (2021) studied the use of ASR software in pronunciation learning. Their findings showed that ASR can support the improvement of learners’ pronunciation, particularly when the learning activities are accompanied by correction and feedback. Their findings suggest that speech recognition becomes more meaningful in language learning when it is not limited to transcription alone. Instead, it should also support learners by identifying possible pronunciation issues and providing guidance for improvement. 
Dja’far and Hamidah (2024) similarly found that AI-based speech recognition can improve English pronunciation when learners receive immediate feedback and opportunities for independent practice. Their findings show that ASR becomes more useful in language learning when it is connected to correction and guided practice rather than used only for transcription. This is relevant to ReaDirect because the system uses learner speech as assessment evidence while also providing corrective feedback to support reading and pronunciation development. 
These studies are relevant to the present study because ReaDirect also depends on learner speech as the basis for assessment. The system must not only recognize what the learner said, but also support the evaluation of pronunciation, reading accuracy, and fluency. Therefore, the reviewed studies support the development of a speech-based learning system that does not rely on transcription alone. Instead, the system should include correction and feedback features to help ensure that learner assessment becomes more accurate, fair, and meaningful for young learners. 
Levenshtein Distance for Word or Transcript Matching 
Fadel et al. (2023), Isbell, Kim, and Chen (2023), and Gutz, Maffei, and Green (2023) highlighted the use of Levenshtein distance and transcript-matching measures in improving or evaluating ASR-based systems. Fadel et al. (2023) used Levenshtein and Damerau–Levenshtein distance as post-processing methods for correcting out-of-vocabulary words in speech recognition outputs, showing that edit-distance methods can help reduce recognition errors after transcription. Isbell et al. (2023) explored ASR-based scoring for the Korean Elicited Imitation Test by comparing ASR-generated transcripts with scoring metrics, showing that automated transcript comparison can support language assessment. Gutz et al. (2023) also used Levenshtein distance between target words and ASR transcription in a study that provided ASR-based feedback to elicit clearer speech. These studies are relevant to the present study because ReaDirect also compares learner speech transcripts with expected answers before scoring. However, these works focused on OOV correction, language testing, and clear-speech feedback, while the present study applies transcript matching in a Grade 1 oral reading coach with rule-based scoring and child-friendly feedback. 
Zellou and Lahrouchi (2024), Kuhn et al. (2024), and Liao et al. (2023) further showed the importance of edit-distance and transcript-quality evaluation in ASR systems. Zellou and Lahrouchi (2024) examined cross-language ASR transfer from Arabic to Tashlhiyt and measured performance using Word Error Rate and Levenshtein distance, showing that ASR accuracy can vary based on word form and speaking style. Kuhn et al. (2024) studied the accuracy of ASR solutions and emphasized that WER is commonly based on minimum edit distance between reference and generated transcripts. Liao et al. (2023) focused on improving the readability of ASR transcriptions, showing that raw ASR output may still require post-processing before it becomes useful for readers or downstream tasks. These studies support the present study because learner transcripts should not be judged only as raw ASR outputs; they need checking, correction, and comparison against expected responses. However, these works focused on ASR evaluation, accessibility, and transcription readability, while ReaDirect uses transcript matching for oral reading assessment and learner feedback. 
Edit Distance for Transcript Comparison 
Russell et al. (2024), Park, Chen, and Hain (2024), and Kuhn, Kersken, and Zimmermann (2024) further emphasized that transcript comparison should go beyond simple ASR output checking. Russell et al. (2024) reviewed six ASR technologies for conversational speech transcription and noted that WER alone may not fully describe transcript quality or practical usability. Park et al. (2024) proposed ASR system-independent WER estimation, showing that transcription quality can be estimated by generating likely ASR hypotheses and comparing them with reference-like patterns. Kuhn et al. (2024) introduced a method beyond standard Levenshtein-based WER computation by using multiple algorithms to provide more robust error computation and granular error classification. These studies are relevant to the present study because ReaDirect needs transcript comparison not only to mark answers as correct or incorrect, but also to understand possible ASR errors before learner scoring. However, these studies focused mainly on ASR evaluation metrics, while the present study applies transcript comparison within a rule-based reading-assessment flow. 
Word Error Rate 
Choe et al. (2022), Heitz, Schneider, and Langer (2024), and Arif et al. (2025) further showed that Word Error Rate remains useful for comparing ASR systems across accents, clinical datasets, and low-resource languages. Choe et al. (2022) studied ASR errors in World Englishes and showed that recognition errors can vary depending on language-specific and speaker-related factors. Heitz et al. (2024) compared ASR systems using WER on Alzheimer’s speech recordings and examined how ASR errors affect linguistic features and automatic classification. Arif et al. (2025) benchmarked Urdu ASR models using WER and analyzed frequent error types such as insertions, deletions, and substitutions. These studies are relevant to the present study because ReaDirect also needs to interpret ASR output carefully before scoring learners, especially when speech patterns differ from standard adult speech. However, these works mainly focused on ASR benchmarking and error analysis, while the present study applies WER within a learner-centered reading assessment flow with transcript validation and child-friendly feedback. 
Character Error Rate 
Thennal D K, James, Gopinath, and Muhammed Ashraf K (2025), Park, Kang, and Hain (2024), and Park and Saz (2025) emphasized the importance of Character Error Rate in evaluating Automatic Speech Recognition systems, especially when word-level evaluation may not fully capture transcription accuracy. Thennal D K et al. (2025) argued that CER is more suitable than WER in multilingual ASR evaluation because it is less affected by word segmentation and morphological differences across languages. Park et al. (2024) proposed a CER estimation method for short utterances, showing that character-level tokenization can provide finer evaluation for shorter speech outputs. Park and Saz (2025) further applied CER estimation for semi-supervised Arabic dialect speech recognition, using CER to select better pseudo-transcribed data. These studies are relevant to the present study because ReaDirect evaluates short learner responses where a small spelling or character-level difference may affect transcript interpretation. However, these works focused mainly on ASR evaluation and estimation methods, while the present study applies CER as a supporting metric within an oral reading coach with transcript correction, expected-answer matching, rule-based scoring, and child-friendly feedback. 
Ankita and Shahnawazuddin (2024), Mussakhojayeva et al. (2023), and Bekarystankyzy et al. (2024) further demonstrated that Character Error Rate is useful in children’s ASR and multilingual low-resource ASR studies. Ankita and Shahnawazuddin (2024) developed a children’s ASR system under low-resource conditions and reported that data augmentation with suitable acoustic features produced a large relative improvement in CER. Mussakhojayeva et al. (2023) developed multilingual ASR models for Turkic languages and reported reductions in both character and word error rates when related languages were trained jointly. Bekarystankyzy et al. (2024) also applied multilingual end-to-end ASR for low-resource Turkic languages and showed that transfer learning and multilingual modeling can reduce CER in related language groups. These studies are relevant to the present study because Grade 1 learners’ speech may be short, developing, and different from standard adult speech, making character-level evaluation useful for transcript checking. However, these studies focused mainly on ASR model performance, while ReaDirect uses CER as part of a learner-centered system that combines recognition, transcript validation, scoring, and supportive feedback. 
Words Correct Per Minute 
Grabe and Yamashita (2022) discussed reading fluency, reading rate, and comprehension in second-language reading. Their work reviewed how reading fluency is understood through automaticity, accuracy, reading rate, and prosodic phrasing. The researchers explained that fluent word reading is important in early reading development, while fluent passage reading becomes strongly connected to later reading comprehension. Their discussion is useful to the present study because Words Correct Per Minute can serve as one measurable indicator of oral reading fluency, especially when learners read aloud and their speed and accuracy need to be monitored. Still, their work mainly provides a theoretical explanation of reading fluency rather than a system-based method for automated learner assessment. This leads to studies that examine how WCPM scores can be measured and compared more consistently across reading passages. 
Nese (2022) compared the growth and predictive performance of a traditional oral reading fluency measure with an experimental computerized oral reading measure. The study is relevant because it shows that computerized oral reading evaluation can use timing, correct words, and related reading indicators to support progress monitoring. For ReaDirect, this supports the use of WPM and WCPM as measurable fluency indicators, although the system still combines fluency with accuracy, transcript correction, and comprehension-related scoring. 
Kara, Kamata, Qiao, Potgieter, and Nese (2024) focused on equating oral reading fluency scores using a model-based approach. Their study addressed the problem that WCPM scores may vary depending on the reading passage used, making direct comparison difficult when learners read different texts. Using simulation methods, the researchers compared a model-based equating approach with traditional observed-score equating methods. The study is important because WCPM is often used to screen learners, monitor progress, and evaluate intervention outcomes, but its reliability depends on how fairly the scores are interpreted. In relation to the present study, this supports the need to treat WCPM as a measurable fluency indicator that must be interpreted carefully rather than as a simple raw number. While Kara et al. focused on psychometric score equating, the present system applies WCPM within a learner-centered reading platform where ASR output, scoring rules, and feedback must work together. 
Van der Velde, Molenaar, Veldkamp, Feskens, and Keuning (2024) conducted a scoping review on oral reading fluency assessment among early primary school children. The review examined how reading fluency is defined, assessed, and validated in early-grade contexts. Their work is useful because it shows that oral reading fluency is not limited to speed alone, but also includes accuracy and expressive reading. WCPM remains a common measure because it combines reading speed with the number of correctly read words, making it practical for screening and monitoring. For the present study, this strengthens the basis for including WCPM as one indicator in oral reading assessment. At the same time, the review shows that fluency assessment should not depend on one measure only, which connects to the need for other indicators such as pronunciation accuracy, comprehension, and teacher review in the proposed system. 
Audio Preprocessing 
Labied, Belangour, Banane, and Erraissi (2022) discussed audio preprocessing as one of the first and most important stages in Automatic Speech Recognition. Their work presented common preprocessing techniques used before recognition, including noise removal, voice activity detection, pre-emphasis, framing, windowing, and normalization. These techniques were explained as necessary steps for preparing raw speech signals so that the recognition system can process them more efficiently. For ReaDirect, this literature helps explain why learner recordings cannot be sent directly to the ASR model without preparation, especially because Grade 1 learners may speak softly, pause often, or record in noisy surroundings. The study mainly explains preprocessing from a general ASR perspective, so the next work broadens the discussion by reviewing how different ASR methods depend on feature extraction and audio representation. 
Malik, Malik, Mehmood, and Makhdoom (2021) reviewed Automatic Speech Recognition systems by discussing speech recognition approaches, feature extraction methods, classification models, and their effect on ASR performance. Their survey explained that speech recognition depends heavily on the quality of the acoustic features extracted from the input signal. If the audio features are unclear, noisy, or poorly represented, the recognition model may produce inaccurate transcripts. This connects to ReaDirect because the system needs reliable learner audio before transcript matching, pronunciation checking, and reading performance scoring can be applied. The survey gives a wide technical background on ASR, but it does not focus specifically on young learners or reading assessment, which makes it useful to connect with studies that examine modern deep learning techniques for speech processing. 
Mehrish, Majumder, Bharadwaj, Mihalcea, and Poria (2023) reviewed deep learning techniques used in speech processing, including speech recognition, speech enhancement, speech synthesis, and other speech-related tasks. Their review traced the development of speech processing from traditional approaches such as MFCC and HMM to modern deep learning architectures such as CNNs, RNNs, transformers, conformers, and diffusion models. The discussion is important for ReaDirect because the system depends on deep learning-based speech processing to convert learner audio into usable transcripts. Although the work provides a broad review of speech processing, it does not directly address how children’s oral reading audio should be handled in a classroom-based reading coach. This gap leads to studies that focus more closely on improving recognition under noisy or difficult audio conditions. 
Kheddar, Hemis, and Himeur (2024) examined advanced deep learning approaches in Automatic Speech Recognition, including deep transfer learning, federated learning, reinforcement learning, and transformer-based models. Their survey emphasized that ASR systems often face problems when the training and testing conditions are different, especially in noisy environments or domain-specific speech settings. This point is useful for ReaDirect because learner recordings may vary depending on microphone quality, background noise, speaking clarity, and learner behavior during oral reading. The study gives a strong foundation for understanding robust ASR, but it remains focused on broad ASR architectures rather than a specific preprocessing pipeline for reading support. The next study narrows the discussion by showing how speech enhancement can be integrated directly with speech recognition. 
Chang, Maekaku, Fujita, and Watanabe (2022) proposed IRIS, an end-to-end ASR model that integrates speech enhancement with self-supervised speech representation. Their approach used a speech enhancement module to improve noisy input before the enhanced speech was processed by the ASR component. In their experiments, the model was evaluated on noisy speech recognition tasks and achieved strong performance, showing that enhancement before recognition can improve transcription quality. This is related to ReaDirect because unclear learner recordings may affect speech-to-text output, and preprocessing can help reduce failed or inaccurate recognition. The study is more focused on robust ASR model design than educational reading assessment, so it is useful to connect it with work that also considers dereverberation and multi-channel speech processing. 
Masuyama, Chang, Cornell, Watanabe, and Ono (2023) extended audio preprocessing for ASR by integrating dereverberation, beamforming, self-supervised learning representation, and speech recognition into a single end-to-end architecture. Their work addressed noisy and reverberant speech, where audio captured from microphones may contain echoes or unwanted sound reflections that reduce recognition accuracy. The system achieved improved word error rate results by combining audio enhancement and ASR training in one framework. For ReaDirect, this supports the idea that audio quality must be considered before using transcripts for scoring learner responses. The study is highly technical and aimed at robust ASR performance, while the present system applies simpler audio validation and preprocessing steps for young learners. This prepares the discussion for studies that examine speech enhancement more directly. 
Tal, Mandel, Kreuk, and Adi (2022) compared phonetic-aware techniques for speech enhancement. Their study examined how phonetic information from pretrained ASR or self-supervised learning models can be used to improve speech enhancement systems. Rather than removing noise only for human listening quality, the researchers considered how enhanced speech could preserve phonetic information needed by recognition systems. This idea is important for ReaDirect because preprocessing should not distort the learner’s actual pronunciation. If enhancement removes or changes meaningful speech sounds, the transcript and pronunciation feedback may become unfair. Although the study focused on speech enhancement models, it connects well to ReaDirect’s need to clean learner audio while still preserving the spoken response for reading assessment. 
Eickhoff, Möller, Rosin, Twiefel, and Wermter (2023) introduced Cleancoder, a preprocessing architecture designed to improve noise robustness in pretrained ASR systems. Their approach extracted denoising capabilities from a Conformer ASR model and used them to reconstruct denoised spectrograms from noisy speech input. The researchers evaluated the preprocessor as a front-end for ASR and reported improvements in noisy conditions. In relation to ReaDirect, this study shows that preprocessing can function as a protective layer before speech recognition, especially when audio is recorded outside ideal studio conditions. The work, however, is still centered on model architecture and noise robustness, while ReaDirect applies preprocessing in a learning environment where the goal is accurate transcript generation and child-friendly feedback. 
Guo, Du, Siniscalchi, Pan, and Liu (2025) proposed a controllable Conformer for speech enhancement and recognition. Their method was designed to balance speech quality for human listening and ASR performance by using a control factor within a Conformer-based enhancement model. This is important because stronger denoising does not always guarantee better recognition if the enhancement process changes useful speech details. For ReaDirect, the study strengthens the idea that preprocessing should be careful and controlled, especially when learner pronunciation is being evaluated. The study focused on technical speech enhancement and recognition performance, while the present system uses audio preprocessing as part of a broader oral reading support flow that includes transcript correction, expected-answer matching, rule-based scoring, and teacher monitoring. 
16 kHz Audio Resampling 
Radford et al. (2023) presented Whisper, a robust speech recognition system trained through large-scale weak supervision. The model used an encoder-decoder Transformer architecture and standardized incoming audio by resampling it to 16,000 Hz before computing an 80-channel log-magnitude Mel spectrogram. Its training covered large and varied audio sources, allowing the system to generalize across languages, tasks, and recording conditions. For ReaDirect, the study demonstrates that 16 kHz resampling is not only a dataset convention but also a practical preprocessing step used in modern ASR model pipelines. ReaDirect differs from Whisper because its purpose is not broad multilingual transcription alone; it applies ASR to structured reading tasks where the transcript must be compared with expected learner responses and converted into instructional feedback. The limitation of using a general-purpose ASR model is that it may still misrecognize short child speech, letters, and beginner words, which makes post-ASR scoring and correction necessary. This leads to multilingual and low-resource speech studies that further show why input consistency matters when adapting pretrained models. 
Huang et al. (2026) introduced MSR-HuBERT, a self-supervised model designed to handle multiple sampling rates. The researchers argued that many speech self-supervised learning models assume a single sampling rate, which can create temporal-resolution mismatches when audio comes from mixed-rate sources. Their method replaced HuBERT’s single-rate down sampling front end with a multi-sampling-rate adaptive design that maps waveforms from different sampling rates into a shared temporal space without requiring resampling. Experiments across 16 kHz to 48 kHz audio showed improvements over HuBERT in speech recognition and speech reconstruction. This study is significant to ReaDirect because it explains the limitation behind standard 16 kHz resampling: resampling is useful for compatibility with existing ASR models, but it may not preserve all high-frequency information from higher-quality recordings. Since ReaDirect is built for a practical classroom system, 16 kHz resampling remains appropriate for compatibility, efficiency, and consistency, but future versions may explore sampling-rate-aware models if learner recordings come from many devices with different audio qualities. 
Word Recognition 
Some studies observed that rhyming poems allow children to discover patterns in sounds and word boundaries through playful repetitions. MacKenzie et al. (2021) discovered that rhyme ability is associated with phonological awareness in preschool classrooms, and concluded that targeted rhyme production tasks could even improve children’s segmentation skills in children ages 4 to 6. Furthermore, Šimunović et al. (2021) found rhyme production and rhyme matching to be correlated with pre reading skills that fall under the umbrella of phonological awareness, specifically in preschool classrooms as well. These two studies correlate because they both prove how children can obtain entry knowledge into phonemes through the use of rhyming words. Rhyming allows children to predict sounds and words despite not knowing detailed letter sounds. 
Rhyming aids children in their early stages of reading development because children can feel accomplished before segmenting phonemes within words. Hjetland et al. (2023) reported that children who participated in rhyming interventions showed significant improvement in vocabulary retention and sound manipulation which in return increases early reading development. McArthur et al. (2024) found that reading rhyming poetry allowed elementary students to read more fluently and with better comprehension. Although these studies show positive outcomes for rhyming interventions, they also found that children who read rhyming books but also received instruction in print showed a better ability to decode words. Therefore, rhyming allows for children to feel accomplished when guessing words, but reading books with intention allows for children to learn how to correctly decode words. 
To compensate for this drawback, studies have focused on programs that work on phonological skills while assisting children in reading development. Lervåg et al. (2022) claimed that if children performed rhyme tasks that were followed by blending tasks, then word reading improved. Torppa et al. (2023) argued that reading development can be improved through rhymes that can be related to the child’s culture. What can be taken from these studies is that rhyming teaches children phonological skills but blending and reading allows the child to transfer those skills onto real reading applications. Rhyming allows for children to learn about the playful nature of phonemes and phonological awareness skills allow for children to break down words. However, if children do not know how letters connect to phonemes they will never improve in reading. 
Many studies tend to only focus on English children or use children who only speak English. Studies should focus on using rhyming as a tool for many children that speak different languages. By doing so, teachers will be able to use children’s Home Language (HL) to assist them in developing their phonological skills. Therefore, the current study will use previous rhyming studies but incorporate them into a multilingual classroom. 
Reading fluency 
Both research studies focused on the improvements gained from fluency interventions. Gorsuch et al. (2021) found that repeated reading with prosody feedback significantly increased words correct per minute and expression scores among elementary students. Additionally, Sanchez et al. (2021) found improvements in both oral reading speed and accuracy with the help of digital fluency programs during COVID 19 on grades 2-5. These two studies relate because they have both researched how repeated exposure to texts allows children to allocate more cognitive resources to comprehension instead of decoding. 
What makes fluency particularly important to reading development is that it creates a link between word recognition and comprehension. Baker et al. (2024) showed how much students grow in fluency between grade 2 predicts their literacy skills in adulthood. Rasinski et al. (2025) also showed how modeling fluent reading can increase student motivation and prosody in a classroom with diverse learners. Although both studies demonstrated how fluency can help students become better readers, they do not account for individual differences in treatment response. For example, students come into fluency programs with different baseline skills based on socioeconomic status. Therefore, while treatment may help students improve fluency quicker, it may not lead to long-lasting improvements if not individualized. 
Luckily, other studies introducing multidimensional fluency account for both rate, accuracy, and expression while also considering comprehension. Stevens et al. (2023) reported that assisted reading technologies could improve student prosody while also increasing their speed. Little et al. (2024) claimed that students who completed oral retellings of the text were able to transfer their fluency skills to reading comprehension. Based on these studies, one could use repeated reading to help students improve reading speed, modeling to improve prosody, and technology to check for comprehension. Using technology could allow students to get feedback at a quicker rate than a teacher could provide.
