However, while many studies look into the effect of fluency interventions on English monolingual students or one aspect of rate, few studies take a cross linguistic approach to providing holistic prosody instruction to multilingual learners. This study is significant because not all languages show the same fluency patterns. Languages with more syllable-timed tendencies will need different prosody targets than what has been set in English. Therefore, we will use the studies mentioned above and expand on them by giving multilingual students multidimensional fluency interventions and charting their progress through a cross linguistic lens. This will allow us to create interpretable student profiles and make more prescriptive recommendations on how to improve targeted fluency programs for all types of learners. 
Letter-sound correspondence 
Research has found effective instruction in letter sound correspondence builds decoding ability through explicit grapheme phoneme mappings required for word reading. Ehri et al. (2022) discovered that teaching letter sound correspondences explicitly through phonics is increasing kindergarten reading accuracy by 25% compared to when these skills are taught implicitly. Additionally, Mesmer and Griffith (2021) found when taught explicitly though systematic synthetic phonics that emphasis letter sound blending outperformed students who were taught through analytic phonics methods in beginning grade 1. The commonality between these two studies is that they both support direct explicit instruction of letter sound connections as opposed to learning these skills implicitly. 
Letter sound knowledge is a critical piece of early reading because readers rely on memory of sound-letter patterns to decode new words. Castles et al. (2023) found that learning the consistent correspondences helps predicts future word reading ability. Nation et al. (2024) explained how learners utilize knowledge of irregular correspondences by first overlearning regular correspondences then using them to decode unfamiliar words. While each study provides evidence for the importance of letter sound instruction, they also mentioned that learning letter sounds transferred differently across languages of different orthographic depths. Using this information, we understand letter sound knowledge provides a foundation for reading, but proficiency will vary based on the writing system. 
The studies discussed above addressed this issue by focusing on blended phonics techniques which taught letter sounds explicitly while reinforcing them through decodable books. Fletcher et al. (2022) mentioned how combining phonics with decodable texts will improve students’ ability to remember letter sound correspondence. Compton et al. (2023) discuss how using RTI tiers allows for more individualized instruction when students do not respond to mainstream phonics teaching. Pairing these two studies together shows how explicit instruction can be used to teach letter sounds, decodable texts can allow for students to practice reading what they have learned in an applicable setting, and using RTI tiers can help give all students equal opportunity to learn these skills. 
While each of these studies revolves around the instruction of letter sound correspondences, they do not take into consideration how different writing systems could impact the learning of letter sounds. The current study will include participants reading in English, Spanish, Chinese, and Arabic to create assessment tools that can compare learning across scripts using the universal nature of graphemes. 
Early reading comprehension 
Recent studies have shown that children’s early reading comprehension increases when taught reading strategies explicitly. These strategies enable children to make meaning of a text rather than decoding the words on a page. For instance, Kim and Phillips (2022) discovered that teaching kindergarten to grade 3 ‘students’ strategies such as inference and summarizing increased comprehension test scores by 30%. Elleman and Compton (2021) also demonstrated that reading aloud coupled with vocabulary instruction and asking questions improved students’ understanding of story elements. These studies are related because they emphasize that children perform better when engaged in learning strategies than when passively listening to or reading a story. 
Direct instruction also plays a role in children’s basic reading skills. Research shows that reading comprehension integrates word reading skills, vocabulary instruction, and students’ background knowledge (BM) to help offset fourth-grade reading slump. McCoach et al. (2023) described how children’s listening exposure statistically influenced their comprehension abilities beyond word reading skills in K-3 students. The Language and Reading Research Consortium (2024) affirmed that reading activities at home assist learners to understand implicit information. Both articles support direct comprehension instruction by illustrating how various skills impact reading comprehension. However, they also note that learners require varied support. For example, English learners may benefit from instruction that scaffolds learning based on their language and culture. The review suggests that although direct instruction can help students comprehend texts better, educators should be mindful of the unique needs of various learners. 
Background knowledge also plays a vital role in reading comprehension among children. Learners are more likely to understand texts when they know something about the topic being discussed. Cabell et al. (2022) mentioned that read aloud focusing on a particular topic can help children acquire knowledge and have better comprehension. Neuman and Wright (2023) stated that when selecting texts, educators should be fair and purposeful to help bridge the gaps between students with low and many learning resources. Previous articles agree that lessons should involve rich and meaningful texts to help improve children’s reading. 
Additionally, studies on cognitive processes have shown that children should monitor their comprehension. Monitoring one’s comprehension entails checking if one understands what they are reading. Baker et al. (2024) showed that first graders who received assistance in noticing and repairing their confusion while reading learned to reflect on their level of understanding. Furthermore, research by Dignath and Veenman (2021) connected elementary self-monitoring comprehension skills to future academic performance. The article on cognitive processes furthers previous research by providing evidence that reading strategies should be taught explicitly. 
The research studies on effective teaching practices show that children benefit from interactive read aloud and classroom discussions. Hennessey et al. (2023) found that reading instruction that used high-support questioning during shared reading produced better results than low-support repeated reading in simple recall and deeper-level comprehension. Snow et al. (2022) agreed that peer discussions should be considered when teaching children because they allow learners to build comprehension collaboratively. In my opinion, the studies on teaching practices complement the other articles. Reading strategies give learners skills to understand a text, background knowledge allows them to have prior information about a topic, monitoring teaches learners to check their level of comprehension, and discussion allows learners to practice their skills. 
While previous research has helped provide valuable insight into reading comprehension, there are still gaps in the literature. Many articles concentrate on teaching students in English-speaking environments or only one reading strategy. Moreover, minimal studies look at comprehensive approaches to teaching multilingual children or students from low socioeconomic status. Student language, background, and environments play a big role in their reading comprehension. Thus, the present study will add to previous research by utilizing a reading comprehension intervention that focuses on various evidence-based strategies and providing scaffolds to promote fairness. The study will allow for clear learner profiles to be generated and offer practical recommendations that may assist other educators and policymakers when creating programs for children. 
Reading Accuracy 
A 2019 article on word decoding accuracy and speed in reading comprehension, replicated and expanded upon in 2024, explored how learners process written words correctly and how quickly they understand the words they read. The study operationalized decoding as the ability at the word level to perform activities that facilitate accessing meaning from print. With measurements of decoding speed, decoding accuracy, and comprehension performance, researchers determined how decoding accuracy and speed independently impact overall reading comprehension. Learners who read quickly tended to still struggle with reading comprehension even if they were fast, indicating that speed should not be considered an all-encompassing metric for reading success. Accuracy matters to ReaDirect because the application measures not only the speed at which learners read passages but also how accurately they are pronouncing and reading words through oral reading activities. This study contributes to the theoretical underpinnings by considering accuracy an important metric to track during learner assessment, feedback, and progress monitoring. While the study focused on decoding measures and reading comprehension, ReaDirect builds upon the concept of reading accuracy through an AI-powered reading support tool that helps teachers gauge where learners experience accuracy-related challenges during early-grade reading. 
Motnelaar et al. (2023) explored automatic oral reading accuracy assessment for reading diagnostics. The study used Dutch language automatic speech recognition technology to measure learners’ oral reading output against the text they were expected to read correctly. Scorings were evaluated to see if a fully automated approach to measuring oral reading accuracy agreed with human ratings. Results indicated fair to substantial agreement between ASR scoring and human accuracy ratings, allowing researchers to conclude speech technology could help facilitate frequent checks for reading accuracy. This study is relevant to ReaDirect because it similarly uses speech recognition technology to evaluate learners’ oral reading performance. Capturing learners’ spoken reading response can aid teachers in noticing when and where students make errors that can be hard to follow through on paper across many students. Molenaar et al.’s study focused on Dutch oral reading skills, whereas the current study implements a similar methodology with Grade 1 students reading early literacy items in English. The studies’ contrast in languages exemplifies how reading-based technologies should be adapted to accurately interpret students’ pronunciation and language differences. 
Researchers in a 2024 longitudinal study on early literacy skills and first-grade performance investigated how early skills in phonological awareness and letter sound correspondences predicted students’ later reading accuracy and fluency. The study followed children from low-socioeconomic-status backgrounds who were learning to read in Turkish and measured their performance on early literacy skills and first-grade reading outcomes. Results concluded that learners who had higher awareness of sounds and knowledge of letter-sound relationships in Turkish grew to have higher accuracy and fluency in reading. Reading accuracy is essential to ReaDirect because the application measures students’ reading ability through more than just passage reading exercises. Assessing students on letter knowledge, sound production, and simple word reading can help teachers better understand how students’ accuracy with smaller reading units can impact their overall performance. This study helps establish how early intervention and support for reading accuracy can prevent future reading challenges. ReaDirect allows for support to be given immediately after reading accuracy issues are detected, while the study focused on how reading accuracy was impacted months down the line. The importance of phonological awareness on reading accuracy was further highlighted in another study. 
Researchers in a 2023 study on EFL students’ phonological awareness and its relation to reading skill found connections between students’ phoneme and syllable manipulation skills and their ability to read accurately. Students took a test measuring their ability to work with sounds and syllables in words and read words for accuracy. The study concluded that there was a relationship between phonological awareness and reading accuracy because students who performed better on phonological awareness tasks also read words more accurately. This study supports how ReaDirect can help teachers gauge students’ reading accuracy because the application listens to students read words aloud. When students miss-read words, it may not only show a lack of exposure or practice but could indicate that students are having trouble hearing, pronouncing, or recalling sounds that make up words. While the study was conducted with school-aged students in an EFL classroom, and ReaDirect targets first graders reading early literacy items, both show how reading accuracy is dependent on students being able to recognize and understand individual sounds and syllables within words. 
The 2025 fluency assessment study defined oral reading fluency as “word-decoding accuracy, an appropriate rate of reading, and acceptable prosody”. Rather than treating accuracy alone as a beginning-reader skill, the study included it alongside rate and prosody as a fundamental component of fluent reading. The researchers used machine-learning methods to determine how individual indicators of reading can be processed and analyzed. This study is significant to the present work because it supports treating fluency as more than speed. While fast reading is a valuable skill, mistakes like mispronunciations and substitutions hinder comprehension and make oral reading less reliable overall. ReaDirect operates under a similar approach by measuring accuracy in addition to common fluency-related metrics like pacing, pronunciation, and oral reading flow. However, the 2025 study used machine-learning techniques to support fluency assessment specifically, while the present study focuses on applying AI-supported oral reading analysis in the context of a child-friendly reading coach that also incorporates corrective feedback and teacher support. 
The 2025 reading comprehension study also investigated reading accuracy, but in regards to how it impacted reading comprehension across different grade levels. Their findings indicated that while the strength of text-reading accuracy as a predictor did “decline somewhat across the elementary school years”, it remained a strong predictor of reading comprehension. This suggests that while accuracy is critical during the earliest stages of reading development, it also continues to play a role in comprehension beyond beginner readers. As learners progress through school, they still require sufficient accuracy in order to comprehend words correctly and extract meaning from sentences and passages. This applied to the present study because it validates using accuracy metrics as part of ReaDirect’s oral reading evaluation. Accuracy data can help pinpoint whether learners are struggling due to decoding issues, slow pacing, poor pronunciation, or difficulties connecting words to meaning. While the study looked at how accuracy impacted reading comprehension across elementary school, ReaDirect is intended for use with Grade 1 learners. Accuracy is crucial during the first year of reading instruction because students are still developing their reading habits and behaviors. Monitoring students’ accuracy early can help address potential issues before they develop into persistent patterns. 
In Five from Five’s fluency metrics discussion, reading accuracy is incorporated into the definition of oral reading fluency as words correct per minute. Additionally, their breakdown of reading levels includes accuracy thresholds. High levels of accuracy are expected for reading passages at the independent level, while lower accuracy can indicate when reading assignments are too challenging and falling into the frustration level. These benchmarks are useful because they demonstrate how accuracy can be used to make decisions about text difficulty and reading intervention. Instead of assuming that learners who read slowly or make many mistakes are simply not trying hard enough, low accuracy could be a sign that texts are not properly matched to their ability. ReaDirect could use this information to help teachers better understand learner performance in the context of a specific reading level or task difficulty. By organizing accuracy data into structured performance profiles, the system could also help teachers distinguish between learners who may benefit from simpler texts versus those who need extra phonics practice, pronunciation support, or more oral reading practice. Five from Five offers conceptual guidance on reading fluency and assessment, while the present study develops an oral reading tool that can automate accuracy measurement and support data-driven reading intervention. 
The academic achievement study explored reading as one of several predictors of academic success among learners in key stage 2, which includes grades 4 to 6. The researchers discovered that better performance on the reading test, which included measures of accuracy, allowed them to predict learners who were achieving higher grades in school. This is relevant to the current study because it extends the value of reading accuracy to later stages of academic development. Once learners have mastered basic accuracy, it can continue to support them as they encounter more complex texts and subjects in the upper elementary grades. If students cannot decode words accurately, they will face obstacles in any other class that requires them to read instructions, explanations, or learning materials. Although ReaDirect is designed for younger learners, this study highlights the value of teaching accuracy early. By helping teachers recognize and address accuracy issues early on, it may help reduce the number of students who struggle with reading accuracy later in elementary school and beyond. 
The reviewed articles connect reading accuracy with foundational reading skills, instructional decisions, and long-term academic potential. Reading accuracy lays the groundwork for comprehension by ensuring learners correctly identify words and avoid major decoding errors. Performance monitoring can help differentiate between causes of decoding problems and inform instructional decisions about how to address them. Although each article provides valuable information about reading accuracy, many of these works were conducted outside of multilingual or low-resource contexts. Additional research is needed to explore how accuracy can be monitored digitally in low-cost systems, especially for early-grade classrooms where students may be reading in multiple languages. The present study aims to fill this gap by developing ReaDirect, an AI-powered reading coach that can assess children’s reading accuracy, track their performance over time, and provide teachers with tools to design accuracy-focused instruction. 
Word Recognition 
Within early literacy development rhyming is beneficial because its rhythmic and predictable nature helps young learners build confidence before engaging in more complex phoneme manipulation tasks. Hjetland et al. (2023) explained that the rhyming interventions are strengthen emergent reading development by promoting vocabulary growth and sound play while McArthur et al. (2024) noted that the repeated exposure to rhyming poetry improves the fluency and comprehension in elementary learners. However, these studies demonstrate the value of rhyming they also suggest that its effects become stronger when combined with print exposure since rhyme practice alone produces more limited improvements in decoding accuracy. This indicates that while rhyming develops foundational awareness it must be integrated with alphabetic instruction to achieve stronger literacy outcomes. 
In response to this concern, studies on integrated phonological programs emphasize the importance of combining rhyming activities with explicit phonics instruction to develop skills that can be transferred to reading. Lervåg et al. (2022) stated that rhyme-based activities supported by blending tasks can improve word recognition while Torppa et al. (2023) argued that culturally adapted rhymes benefit diverse learners by reflecting home language patterns. Collectively these studies suggest that rhyming phonological training and phonics work together as complementary components of literacy instruction. Rhyming promotes sound play; phonological awareness tasks strengthen segmentation skills and phonics connects sounds to printed language for more effective reading development. 
Despite these findings many existing studies primarily focus on monolingual English learners or examine rhyming as an isolated activity while fewer studies apply rhyming interventions in multilingual classrooms using inclusive instructional frameworks. This gap is significant because learners’ phonological needs differ depending on their linguistic backgrounds, particularly since rhyme awareness may vary across syllable timed and stress timed languages. Therefore, the present study builds on previous research by applying rhyming interventions within a multilingual framework to develop interpretable phonological profiles and provide prescriptive recommendations that may help educators design targeted literacy programs for diverse early learners. 
Reading Fluency 
In relation to reading development, fluency is useful because it bridges the word recognition to comprehension, especially for struggling readers who plateau without practice. Baker et al. (2024) explained that the growth in fluency from grade 2 predicts the adult literacy outcomes, while Rasinski et al. (2025) noted that the modeling fluent reading boosts motivation and prosody in diverse classrooms. However, while these studies show fluency's benefits, they also suggest that individual differences in response, as socioeconomic factors influence baseline skills. This means that although interventions accelerate fluency, they must be personalized to sustain long term gains. 
To address this concern, the studies on multidimensional fluency emphasize combining rate, accuracy, and expression with the comprehension checks. Stevens et al. (2023) stated that assisted that reading technologies improve prosody alongside with speed, while Little et al. (2024) argued that the oral retellings ensure that fluency transfers to understanding. Taken together, these studies suggest that the repeated reading, modeling, and technology can complement one another in fluency programs. Repeated practice builds speed, modeling enhances expression, and tech provides scalable feedback for comprehension. 
However, most existing studies focus mainly on English monolinguals or isolated rate measures, while fewer apply fluency research to multilingual learners using holistic prosody frameworks. This gap is important because fluency manifests differently across languages, with syllable timed structures requiring adapted prosody goals. Therefore, the present study builds on previous research by applying multidimensional fluency interventions within a cross linguistic framework to generate interpretable profiles and provide prescriptive recommendations that may assist educators in planning targeted programs for diverse readers. 
Letter-Sound Correspondence 
Within early reading development, letter sound correspondence is important because beginning readers depend on organized sound letter knowledge to decode unfamiliar words independently. Castles et al. (2023) explained that mastery of consistent correspondences predicts fluent word reading, while Nation et al. (2024) noted that irregular mappings require overlearning regular patterns first for compensatory strategies. However, while these studies show its benefits, they also suggest variability by language orthographic depth, as transparent systems yield faster gains than opaque ones. This means that although letter sound training builds core skills, it must adapt to linguistic context for maximal transfer. 
In response to this concern, the studies on blended phonics emphasize the integration of explicit letter sound instruction with a decodable-texts to strengthen practical application. Fletcher et al. (2022) stated that paired phonics with controlled reading materials will boosts retention of correspondences, while Compton et al. (2023) argued that the response to intervention tiers personalize instruction are for non-responders. Taken together these studies suggest that explicit phonics, decodable practice, and tiered support can complement one another in correspondence programs. Explicit teaching provides mappings, decodables enable practice, and tiers ensure equity in mastery. 
Despite these findings, most existing studies focus mainly on alphabetic languages such as English, while fewer apply letter sound research to logographic or mixed scripts using universal grapheme frameworks. This gap is important because correspondence needs differ by writing system, with visual phonetic integration varying across languages. Therefore, the present study builds on previous research by applying letter sound interventions within a cross-script framework to generate interpretable decoding profiles and provide prescriptive recommendations that may assist educators in planning targeted phonics programs for diverse early readers. 
Early Reading Comprehension 
In terms of basic reading skills, early reading comprehension is important because it brings together word reading, vocabulary, and what children already know. These skills help prevent the common drop in reading performance in fourth grade. McCoach et al. (2023) explained that children’s exposure to spoken language affects comprehension more than word reading alone in grades K to 3. Likewise, the Language and Reading Research Consortium (2024) noted that the reading activities at home help the children understand ideas that are not directly stated in the text. However, these studies also show that learners need different kinds of support especially on English learners who may need lessons that match their language and culture. This means that direct teaching can improve comprehension but it should also give proper support to different types of learners. 
Moreover, background knowledge is also important in reading comprehension because children understand a text better when they already know something about the topic. Cabell et al. (2022) stated that topic based read aloud help children build knowledge and improve comprehension. In the same way, Neuman and Wright (2023) explained that choosing fair and meaningful texts can help lessen learning gaps among children with fewer learning resources. These findings support earlier studies by showing that lessons with rich and meaningful content can improve early reading development. 
Research on thinking skills also shows that self-monitoring is important in reading comprehension. Self-monitoring means that children check whether they understand what they are reading. Baker et al. (2024) found that showing first graders how to notice and fix confusion while reading helped them become more aware of their understanding. Similarly, Dignath and Veenman (2021) linked early self-monitoring training to better school performance over time. This shows that reading strategies become more useful when children learn how to use them by themselves. 
To address the classroom challenges, studies on teaching practices highlight that the importance of interactive read aloud and class discussions. Hennessey et al. (2023) reported that the guided questioning during shared reading is more effective than practicing alone in improving both simple recall and deeper understanding. Snow et al. (2022) also supported the peer discussions because they help learners build understanding together. Overall, these studies suggest that reading strategies, background knowledge, self-monitoring, and discussion-based teaching work well together. Strategies give children tools, background knowledge helps them understand the topic, self-monitoring helps them become independent, and discussions help them practice what they learn. 
Despite these contributions, many existing studies still focus on English speaking settings or single reading strategies. Fewer studies use complete approaches for multilingual learners or children from low-income backgrounds. This gap is important because children’s reading comprehension can be affected by their language, culture, and learning environment. Therefore, the present study builds on previous research by using a reading comprehension intervention that combines different strategies and supports fairness in learning. It aims to create clear learner profiles and provide useful recommendations that may help educators and policymakers plan better programs for different young readers. 
AI-Assisted Language Learning and Pronunciation Studies 
Kim et al. (2023) investigated the relationship between AI-supported online language learning and learners’ self-esteem, cognitive-emotion regulation, academic enjoyment, and language success. The study used a quantitative design with 389 EFL learners in China, and the data were analyzed through confirmatory factor analysis and structural equation modeling. The results showed that AI-supported online language learning positively influenced learners’ cognitive-emotion regulation and academic enjoyment, which were associated with improved language success. The study concluded that AI-supported language learning can strengthen both emotional and academic outcomes in EFL contexts. 
Reading Comprehension and Literacy Improvement 
The researchers describe the use of an Intelligent Tutoring System (ITS) to improve reading comprehension in primary and secondary education. A systematic literature review was used to analyze existing ITS-based reading-comprehension studies, and an experimental method was employed to evaluate the proposed system in contexts where learners study in their native language. Results showed that ITS-based instruction was more effective than traditional teaching approaches, especially for students with lower reading proficiency, indicating that AI-tutoring can support struggling readers and enhance reading-comprehension development. (Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITS) to Improve Reading Comprehension: A Systematic Review, H. Atun, 2020) 
The researchers aimed to evaluate the reading performance of Grade 1 learners and identify the causes of reading difficulty to develop a remedial reading program. A descriptive-survey design was used involving 70 Grade 1 learners, 7 parents, and 3 teachers; the Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment (CRLA) and survey questionnaires were administered to assess reading performance. Results revealed that 84% of learners were “full refreshers” and needed major reading support, with key causes including inadequate reading materials, limited parental support at home, and insufficient time for reading practice. (Reading Performance of Grade 1 Learners: Basis for Remedial Program, S. Cañete & J. Eswan, 2025) 
The researchers investigated how early exposure to English (L2) affects phonological awareness in Chinese (L1) among young bilingual learners using behavioral tests and neuroimaging (fNIRS). The findings revealed that bilingual children showed enhanced phonological processing mechanisms, with evidence of L2-to-L1 transfer, where learning English contributed to improved phonological awareness in their mother tongue. Although behavioral differences between bilingual and monolingual learners were not yet significant at Grade 1, bilingual learners demonstrated greater brain activation in regions associated with phonological manipulation, indicating early cognitive advantages that support literacy development. These results suggest that learning a second language can positively influence and strengthen first language reading skills, particularly in phonological awareness. (Neural substrates of L2-L1 transfer effects on phonological awareness in young Chinese-English bilingual children, Kou et al., 2024) 
Advanced ASR and Model-Based Speech Recognition 
The researchers systematically examined the effect of Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR)-based tools on ESL/EFL pronunciation through a meta-analysis of multiple experimental studies. Data were aggregated from various research projects testing ASR-supported pronunciation practice among second-language learners, and effect sizes were computed to compare ASR-based interventions against control conditions. Results showed that ASR-based tools significantly improved pronunciation accuracy and overall speaking ability, with particularly strong effects when feedback was targeted and specific. (The effectiveness of automatic speech recognition in ESL/EFL pronunciation: A meta-analysis, T. Ngo, H. Chen, & K. Lai, 2023) 
The researchers designed and tested an oral pronunciation correction system using speech recognition and AI technologies to enhance learners’ speaking and pronunciation skills. The study followed a system-design and experimental-testing approach, implementing speech-recognition and speech-synthesis modules, along with preprocessing, feature extraction, and template-matching algorithms to compare learner speech against standard pronunciation models. Experimental results indicated high recognition accuracy - up to 97.755% for males and 95.208% for females - under controlled conditions, and the system successfully delivered real-time corrective feedback, demonstrating its suitability as an automated pronunciation-training tool. (Speech recognition sensors and artificial intelligence automatic evaluation application in English oral correction system, W. Jing, 2024) 
The researchers developed a gamified mobile app for pronunciation training to support Computer-Assisted Language Learning (CALL) using AI and speech-recognition technology. The system was specifically designed for children, including those with Speech Sound Disorder, and for non-native learners of Swedish and Finnish. A speech-recognition-based engine was embedded in a mobile game, where learners produced utterances that the models evaluated and fed back to them in a playful context. The setup worked well as both a recognizer and evaluator within the game, providing accurate feedback while maintaining learner engagement through gamified elements. (Developing an AI-Assisted Low-Resource Spoken Language Learning App for Children, Y. Getman et al., 2023) 
The researchers explored the use of Google Translate’s ASR to provide corrective and confirmative feedback for second-language learners’ pronunciation. An experimental design was used, in which Quebec Francophone speakers produced English words beginning with “th,” “h,” and various vowels embedded in both predictable and unpredictable sentence contexts; their recordings were fed into Google Translate’s ASR to assess transcription accuracy and the system’s ability to distinguish correct from incorrect pronunciations. Results showed that ASR was most accurate for correct pronunciations in predictable contexts, enabling confirmative feedback, and performed well at detecting errors in less predictable ones, highlighting its potential as a pronunciation-assessment and feedback tool in language learning. (Exploring automatic speech recognition for corrective and confirmative pronunciation feedback, P. John, C. Johnson, & W. Cardoso, 2025) 
The researchers investigated an ASR-based language-learning system named “I Can Speak” to improve English pronunciation among learners. An experimental study was conducted in which participants used the system to practice spoken English, with their utterances analyzed by an ASR engine that provided targeted, immediate feedback. Results indicated that learners showed clear improvements in pronunciation accuracy and overall speaking ability after using the system, suggesting that ASR-driven platforms can serve as reliable tools for targeted pronunciation practice and language-skills development. (“I Can Speak”: improving English pronunciation through automatic speech recognition-based language learning systems, M. Bashori et al., 2024) 
Barcovschi et al. (2023) explored the adaptation of Whisper, a large-scale supervised Automatic Speech Recognition model, for child speech recognition using MyST, PFSTAR, CMU Kids, and LibriTTS. The researchers evaluated Whisper in zero-shot and fine-tuned settings and found that fine-tuning substantially reduced Word Error Rate across child-speech datasets. The study also showed that recognition performance varies according to dataset distribution and speaker characteristics, while Whisper can generalize to speech conditions not represented in a narrow training set. This is relevant to ReaDirect because the system uses Whisper for words, sentences, and passages but still applies audio-quality validation, expected-answer comparison, and teacher-review safeguards for young learners’ speech. 
Reading Rate 
“The Neural Bases of Reading Fluency” was a comprehensive neurocognitive review published in 2024. The document looked at reading fluency through brain activity responsible for word recognition and comprehension. Brain imaging studies show fluent readers have more robust activation in regions supporting these functions. In other words, when typical readers see a word, they recognize it automatically and do not need to pause and sound it out. The article associated reading rate with these neurological functions because fluent readers can traverse text with relatively little effort. This resource supports the current study by providing a biological reason for including reading rate with other performance measures. In ReaDirect, reading rate can help teachers determine if learners decode every word they read or whether they are developing automaticity with high-frequency words and passages. The difference between these sources is their scale of research. The journal article analyzes fluency through neurocognitive scans, while the present study takes place in real-world classrooms with oral reading tasks and speech recognition-supported assessment. Both agree, however, that educators should help children read accurately and automatically without equating fluency with speed. 
Across different countries and literacy rates, reading speed does not always correspond with comprehension ability. The 2021 to 2022 GEM Report SCOPE synthesis showed how readers may struggle when reading too slowly or too quickly. If reading speed is too slow, readers may forget the beginning of a sentence before they can finish reading it. Reading too quickly may cause learners to overlook details and read without understanding the material. This understanding of reading rate applies to ReaDirect because the software should not push learners to read as quickly as possible. For example, a Grade 1 learner reading faster than their classmates is not necessarily reading fluently. If the child skips words, omits pronunciation of certain sounds, or cannot answer questions about the text, they will need further support. The present study can take this finding into account by treating reading rate as one part of a whole reading performance. By looking at accuracy, proper pronunciation, pacing, and comprehension-based questions, teachers can use ReaDirect to determine whether a learner is reading too fast, too slowly, or at an appropriate rate. 
Educational interventions like repeated reading and assisted reading have demonstrated benefits of tracking reading rate. Both types of interventions require learners to read the same or similar text multiple times. Learners may read independently or with teacher support, peer assistance, or by reading along with a fluent reader. Assuming the text slightly challenges the learner but does not contain unknown words, reading the passage repeatedly can increase confidence, pace, word recognition, and understanding. Repeated reading supports ReaDirect in that the software offers learners reading activities after assessment. Learners can practice reading level-appropriate texts that correspond with their instructional needs. Rate of speech can indicate if the practice is improving the learner’s skill or if they continue to read word-by-word without connecting words into phrases. However, repeated reading relies on teacher observation and often a pen and paper to keep time or record mistakes. ReaDirect streamlines this process by allowing teachers to organize oral reading responses and fluency metrics in a digital format. As both resources suggest, rate should increase as a learner’s accuracy improves, but they should not sacrifice correct pronunciation or comprehension to read faster. 
Reading Rate as One Indicator of Fluency Accuracy is rated second-highest in importance because literacy educators agree that reading rate should be interpreted with other reading performance measures. Some researchers, previous applications, and educational frameworks highlight how reading rate figures into overall literacy development. 
The 2023 quantitative-correlational study investigating reading fluency among linguistically diverse learners revealed relationships between oral reading fluency, English language proficiency, and reading comprehension for second-language learners in third and fourth grade. Because oral reading fluency includes reading rate, learners who were proficient in both language and fluency scored higher on comprehension. Linguistically diverse learners are common in Philippine multilingual classrooms, so reading rate may be influenced by factors outside of decoding ability. Learners might read slowly due to unfamiliar language or vocabulary, knowledge of literacy skills, or confidence with oral reading. ReaDirect is relevant to this article because Grade 1 learners may speak different languages at home and struggle to read English or Filipino texts at an expected rate. Using the system, teachers can determine if reading rate is impacted by word decoding, pronunciation uncertainty, vocabulary familiarity, or lack of exposure to print. This article focused on older linguistically diverse students, but the current study considers similar factors for younger learners who are developing foundational reading habits. 
The 2026 article discussing changes in Filipino reading proficiency of elementary and junior high school students explored how growth measures in reading rate and other fluency-related skills can provide information about literacy instruction and school-level interventions. This longitudinal study interpreted changes in student reading proficiency to understand whether educational benchmarks were bringing about change. Reading rate is useful for this study because ReaDirect allows for learner-level and teacher-level decision-making. Teachers can use longitudinal reading-rate data to determine whether learners are developing or need intervention. Although the article measured school-level proficiency shifts over multiple years, this study aims to impact learner outcomes at a more immediate scale through Grade 1 oral reading assessment and remediation. Both articles acknowledge that reading rate can be used as one measure of growth when paired with other literacy skills. 
International large-scale assessment discussions from groups like OECD and PISA often include similar dialogue about reading speed, volume, and comprehension. Higher reading proficiency is usually linked to students who read efficiently and often. International assessments are not featured in this review because they generally focus on older students and aggregate literacy performance. However, they help validate the use of reading rate as a meaningful metric in literacy. 
DepEd-initiated literacy discussions and school-based programs sometimes reference reading rate in the Philippines. Fluency is a growing area of literacy that schools can use when determining reading improvement. Rate is best used with other reading performance measures like accuracy and comprehension to understand whether the learner is reading connected text efficiently. This application aligns with how ReaDirect can help teachers track students’ oral reading and fluency progress. By coupling the reading rate with accuracy and comprehension measures, teachers can identify learners who may need fluency interventions like repeated reading, read-aloud practice, text leveling, or decoding checks. Traditional reading rate tracking may be subjective and difficult to record, but ReaDirect streamlines the process with speech recognition technology and target word identification. 
Reading rate has been understood as a key indicator of fluency overall, as it showcases the degree to which decoding processes have automated sufficiently to allow for comprehension. Reading too slowly or too quickly can disrupt comprehension, while a reasonable reading rate allows learners to maintain meaning across words, sentences, and longer passages of text. Across recent work on fluency, neurocognitive processing, multilingual learners, school-based fluency intervention, and benchmarked fluency assessment, regular monitoring of reading rate is recommended while understanding that it does not provide useful information when considered outside of accuracy, prosody, and comprehension. However, much of this work continues to focus on English-medium, high-resource, or later language-learning contexts. Limited research to date has described how reading rate ought to be conceptualized and assessed among multilingual and low-resource early-grade classrooms where students may read in different languages than the language of instruction, have varied exposure to print, or lack normative information on expected reading rates. Here, we develop ReaDirect, an AI-based oral reading coach that measures students' reading rate in real time, produces human-interpretable fluency profiles, delivers corrective feedback, and supports Grade 1 teachers to individualize rate-building practices given learner level, linguistic background, and classroom resources. 
Reading Reassessment 
Mesny et al. (2026) explored creative assessment and grading practices, including reassessment opportunities to reinforce learning following intervention. The article reviewed important factors regarding reassessment processes and opportunities; learners should not simply retake a test but instead be given a chance to learn through the assessment cycle of receiving feedback on performance, practicing the skill, and reattempting to reach proficiency. Reassessment strengthens achievement and self-regulated learning when feedback provided is explicit, immediate, and tethered to the learner’s struggle (Mesny et al., 2026). This relates to ReaDirect as the system can help teachers determine whether a learner who initially performed poorly on a reading diagnostic improves through assessment, targeted corrective feedback, and guided practice. Although the reassessment discussed by Mesny et al. (2026) was generalized to education assessment practices, this concept directly applies to early-grade reading. OROC learners need multiple chances to improve decoding accuracy, fluency, pronunciation, and comprehension. 
A Brookings article on the importance of early reading tests as countries recovered from the pandemic highlighted the value of reassessing students to document academic progress. The article centered around literacy screener results from fall and spring where students in kindergarten and Grade 1 were found to be behind in early literacy compared to expected benchmarks. Schools implementing frequent reassessments with tiered intervention could better understand which learners were falling behind and adapt instruction early. This directly supports the use case for ReaDirect by quantifying how students perform on reading activities at a point in time and then allowing teachers to monitor reading progress through subsequent lessons and reassessments. If learners are given the opportunity to practice reading fundamentals and improve through targeted intervention, teachers should be able to see growth over time by implementing reassessment into their instruction. The Brookings article focused on educational recovery from the pandemic and school-wide reading screenings. In comparison, this project aims to create a tool for classroom reading instruction. The linkage between these works is that reassessment only matters when educators use the data to drive instruction. 
A study conducted in Brazil on reading fluency loss during the pandemic analyzed how longitudinal reading assessments provided insight on changing learner performance. Researchers tracked students reading fluency rates and identified which learners began to fall behind in reading speed and accuracy. Schools could then focus resources on students who were losing ground through frequent reassessment. This study helps support the use case for ReaDirect by showing that some learners may pass an early reading assessment but still struggle to develop reading skills without support. Teachers should be able to track whether learners are improving in letters, words, sentences, or passages after practicing with the reading activities provided by ReaDirect. The study related to this project was specific to fluency metrics and defined by Brazil’s unique experience with the pandemic. This project focuses on early reading skills and can be applied to a global audience. Despite these differences, both emphasize the importance of monitoring learner progress whenever possible. 
When applied to the Philippine setting, the 2024 study Assessment of School-Based Reading Program among Grade 4 Struggling Readers highlighted the use of continuous assessment and reassessment to track the progress of students during reading interventions. The researchers mentioned reading components such as phonics, phonemic awareness, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension skills. They also discussed different intervention programs used to help struggling learners such as peer teaching and the Marungko Approach. With regular checking points, teachers were able to monitor which learners continued to struggle in reading and whether or not the activities they were doing was allowing students to make progress. This past study is relevant to ReaDirect because our system also aims to help teachers determine specific reading needs and monitor improvements after completing guided activities. However, the Philippine study focused on Grade 4 students who were already struggling with reading and school-based reading programs. ReaDirect will be used by teachers of Grade 1 learners who may need reading support at the beginning stages of literacy. This study narrows down the scope of reassessment to an earlier literacy stage where timely support can prevent struggling readers in later grades. 
In Instruction Partners’ Educator Guide “Essential Practices in Early Literacy” (2021), the authors highlighted the use of early literacy screeners that are quick and given multiple times throughout the school year. Examples were given for administering brief reading checklists at the beginning, middle, and end of the school year. If learners scored below a certain benchmark, they should receive short-term interventions and be reassessed shortly after to see if the intervention was helpful. I think this routine is important because teachers are more likely to use reassessment if it can inform their instruction. With ReaDirect, teachers can gather data on learner performance and use the technology to help them decide if the learner needs more practice, intervention through modules, or reading support at a different level. The partnering organization’s guide provides school-wide recommendations on implementing reading interventions, while the present study aims to develop these ideas into a digital oral reading support system for teachers. The similarities between these sources emphasize that reassessment should be done frequently, be low-stakes, and feasible enough for teachers to conduct. 
Teacher and Educational System Support 
The researchers explored how heavy teacher workloads interfere with the implementation of digital literacy programs in public primary schools. A descriptive-survey design was used, targeting headteachers, teachers, and Grade 1 pupils in Kitui West Subcounty, Kenya; data were collected through questionnaires, interviews, and focus-group discussions and analyzed using statistical tools such as two-sample t-tests and percentages. Findings indicated that high workloads significantly reduced teachers’ time for preparing and integrating ICT tools into instruction, thereby slowing down the adoption and effectiveness of digital literacy initiatives. (Teacher Workload and Implementation of Digital Literacy Programme in Public Primary Schools in Kitui West Subcounty, Kenya, M. Kitonga et al., 2024) 
The researchers conducted a 5-year longitudinal study to examine the development of reading skills and their predictors among Italian-speaking children from Grade 1 to Grade 5. The findings revealed that reading skills, including fluency and accuracy, develop linearly over time in both typical readers and children with reading difficulties. The study identified phonological awareness and verbal short-term memory as significant predictors of reading development, with phonological awareness influencing both reading fluency and accuracy, while memory skills contributed to reading accuracy. Additionally, early reading performance at the end of Grade 1 was found to be a strong predictor of later reading outcomes, explaining a substantial portion of variance in reading proficiency. These results highlight the importance of early cognitive skills and early intervention in supporting long-term reading development. (Development and predictors of reading skills in a 5-year Italian longitudinal study, Mascheretti et al., 2024). 
Oral Reading Assessment 
Dimzon (2023) explored the feasibility of automated oral reading fluency assessment with Filipino-speaking learners. The study investigated the possibility of quantifying learners’ spoken reading behavior through speech-based features. Oral reading samples were recorded and machine learning classifiers were trained to predict learners’ fluency labels based on features related to pauses, prosody, misread words, and other observable behaviors during reading. By doing so, the study provided evidence that oral reading performance can generate quantifiable traces that can serve as indicators of learners’ fluency and accuracy while reading. This work is relevant to the present study because ReaDirect uses spoken reading responses as evidence for measuring early literacy. Similar to Dimzon’s work, reading responses from ReaDirect can also be analyzed to gain information on learner performance. Both studies acknowledge that reading aloud gives more direct information than silent reading because errors can be heard and processed. The main difference is that Dimzon et al. concentrated on the automated classification of fluency using speech technology, while the present study expands on this notion by pairing oral reading assessment with other features like learner feedback, pronunciation support, progress tracking, and teacher-mediated decision support. Therefore, it introduces the need for structured oral reading practice. 
Cárdenas-Hagan (2024) tackled the operationalization of oral reading practice, specifically how calibrated reading passages, repeated read-aloud exercises, and regular monitoring of words correct per minute, accuracy, and phrasing can support reading instruction. Unlike Dimzon’s study which developed a machine learning model, this work provided practical strategies on how oral reading in classrooms can be more structured. One of the insights from the discussion was that collecting reading data from students through oral reading is more useful when students are assessed using leveled passages and when their performance is tracked over time. This is similar to the present study because ReaDirect aims to capture oral reading responses while also allowing learners to practice repeatedly and be navigated through reading passages. The primary distinction is the method of implementation since Cárdenas-Hagan’s framework was teacher-oriented while the present study leverages a web-based platform to supplement reading assessment, feedback, and monitoring. This further exemplifies how oral reading as a tool for assessment requires proper reading instruction techniques and technologies that can streamline the organization of reading data. 
The Monthly Oral Reading Assessment in Project READY-PLUS (2021) described the use of frequent oral reading checks to track learners’ reading progress and identify students in need of early intervention. Teachers would regularly administer reading sessions where they would listen to students read passages out loud and use their observations to improve their teaching. Continuous checks allowed teachers to spot struggling readers sooner and help them improve before their reading problems got worse. With regards to the present study, this highlights how assessment should be continuous instead of relying on a single reading examination. ReaDirect takes on a similar approach by allowing learners’ reading performance to be tracked throughout various diagnostic activities, modules, and re-check activities. However, Project READY-PLUS heavily relied on teacher-led monitoring while this study proposes speech recognition-supported assessment to automatically structure students’ reading responses and provide teachers with faster reading feedback. Monitoring reading skills on a larger scale becomes increasingly important when national and school-wide reading assessments are concerned. 
Oral reading is also a crucial component of reading assessments used in the Philippine setting. The oral reading component of Phil-IRI allows administrators to gauge learners’ reading levels and determines which learners need intervention. Learners are placed into one of three reading levels: independent, instructional, and frustration. Reading levels are determined through learners’ performance in oral reading and reading comprehension. Reading aloud helps teachers determine which students can read on their own, which students can still benefit from reading support, and which students need intensive reading interventions. This study connects to the present research because the goal of ReaDirect is also to provide screening, placement, and intervention decisions through learners’ performance in the system. Similar to how reading levels are determined in Phil-IRI, learners in ReaDirect are also given a reading level based on their scores in each module. Both processes aim to support reading decision-making but differs on the methods used to analyze learners’ reading performance. Administering reading through traditional pen-and-paper methods limits the reading tool to what a teacher can manually score. Digitizing reading assessment tools should not take away the educator’s judgment but should aim to support reading educators in managing reading data. 
“What do they say? Assessment of oral reading fluency in early grades” (2024) discussed how educators might integrate technology into the assessment of reading in the early grades. The study centered around computer-based measures of reading fluency and highlighted how results generated by automated scoring needed to be validated against expert ratings. A key takeaway from this discussion was how digital platforms could ease teacher workload, promote consistency, and organize reading data during instances where large numbers of learners needed to be assessed. Related to ReaDirect, this notion applies directly to speech-to-text processing and automated learner-response comparisons. The current study echoes these ideas by considering technology as a tool to assist teachers with capturing oral reading information, monitoring learner difficulty, and delivering feedback in a time-efficient manner. However, it also considers how automated platforms should not be viewed as a sole replacement for teacher judgment. This limitation creates opportunities to highlight the importance of rubrics, established benchmarks, and detailed scoring criteria for oral reading evaluation. 
Teachers’ oral reading fluency teaching and assessment strategies investigated how reading instructors applied strategies and assessment tools to make note of their students’ fluency. The article discussed words correct per minute, word-efficiency tests, and performance tracking records were used alongside instructional activities like repeated reading and choral reading. Learners were able to develop their fluency and confidence while their teacher kept track of developing skills. ReaDirect’s relationship with this study can be found in how assessment is considered an activity directly tied to instruction. While learners use oral reading tasks to complete assessments on the platform, they are also given corrective feedback and modules to practice improved reading. The difference between the two studies is how the 2021 article focused on teacher-dependent tools that are manually recorded, whereas, ReaDirect offers a speech recognition assisted technology that lessens the load of tracking learners’ oral reading on teachers. Moving from teacher-made forms to technology-enhanced assessment introduces questions about reliability and consistency. 
Oral reading verification forms have been used at school-levels in recent years to track student performance. These forms ask educators to document learner accuracy, prosody, and comprehension during reading activities. Organized data collection like this, pushes schools and teacher to assess reading performance on more than general “impressions.” Instead, learners are scored on reading abilities that can be measured, tracked, and used to inform instruction. This idea translates to the current study because ReaDirect relies on oral reading indicators to provide teachers with quantitative data. Some examples include transcript matching, pronunciation accuracy, reading fluency, words correct per minute, and learner responses that demonstrate comprehension. ReaDirect operates on the same principle that reading performance should be easy to track and organized. However, school forms are still largely dependent on teacher evaluation and could be compromised by time constraints, teacher workload, and score variation between evaluators. This drawback of teacher input forms strengthens the argument for interactive reading technologies that support instruction without overcomplicating the assessment process. 
Oral reading contains many benefits that allow teachers to extract information about students’ reading performance. Whether through assigned activities or informal assessment practices, having learners read out loud can provide insights about their fluency, pronunciation, expression, and comprehension. Research into oral reading and classroom applications have demonstrated how reading aloud allows teachers to better identify learners who may need support, implement reading strategies, and measure growth over time. Challenges still exist for oral reading assessment because many applications are manual, designed with English-first learners in mind, or use a single measure of fluency to define learner ability. Additional research is needed to explore how speech recognition technology can be applied to unique classroom contexts and developmental stages. The current study attempts to address this by introducing ReaDirect, an AI oral reading tool that teachers can use to (a) assess learners’ reading performance, (b) provide pronunciation support, (c) monitor learner progress over time, and (d) support decision-making. By designing a tool to complement teacher expertise, ReaDirect collects reading data in a structured way that can be used to support instructional decision-making. 
Letter-sound Correspondence 
Adebayo et al. (2024) explored whether “explicit instruction in letter-sound correspondence [benefits] early decoding skills” for nursery-school learners in Nigeria. Learners were taught to connect speech sounds with written letters through daily practice. The experimental research measured how well students learned letter-sound connections under systematic instruction versus typical classroom lessons. Students who participated in explicit letter-sound teaching showed “significantly higher gains” on measures of reading accuracy and word attack, with some effects approaching “twice the learning of the control group”. This article relates to ReaDirect because letter-sound mastery was viewed as one of the main goals during early reading instruction. In ReaDirect, learners would be prompted to identify, pronounce, and use letter sounds when engaging with text. While this study centered on teacher instruction, ReaDirect can achieve a similar purpose through technology. As learners interact with the reading support system, their responses can be evaluated, errors can be addressed, and learners needing more phonics instruction can be flagged to teachers. Research by Adebayo et al. (2024) is supported by additional literature that links letter sound knowledge to both spelling and decoding. 
Another study from 2024 highlighted the connection between sound-symbol learning and spelling. Routman and Dahl found that first-grade learners who wrote words with newly learned sound-symbol associations improved their decoding and spelling abilities. The study gave students spelling and decoding interventions that were unmatched by a control group. Both interventions required learners to write words serially, focusing on either real words or “made-up words” known as pseudowords. These tasks required learners to decode by regularly writing words with taught letter-sound patterns. First-grade learners who received additional instruction on specific sound-symbol associations demonstrated improved spelling and pseudo-word decoding performance. The study ties into the present study because ReaDirect views early reading development as a multisensory process where sounds are blended and matched with letters to read words. Specifically, the module concerning letters and simple words can utilize these findings by gradually introducing learners to word reading once they demonstrate mastery with isolated sounds. Unlike Routman and Dahl’s study, learners in ReaDirect will provide spoken, rather than written, responses when interacting with the system. Learner oral reading can also be captured to provide instructors with insights about performance. Despite these differences, both articles acknowledge that sound-symbol instruction should not occur in isolation. 
The National Center for Improving Literacy (2023) published a practice guide on how children learn to read. According to the guide, “children benefit when they master high-utility letters and easily confused letter-sound correspondence early”. These letters were specified as m, t, s, p, a, and i because these characters “enable children to read common CV(C) words sooner”. High utility letters were defined as letters that frequently appear in text, while easily confused sounds were letters that have a unique appearance (e.g., “b” and “d”). Unlike more traditional approaches that allow learners to feel letters, “systematic phonics instruction teaches children that letters are symbols that represent speech sounds”. This resource complements the current study because phonics reviews in ReaDirect will require learners to associate symbols with speech sounds. ReaDirect can encourage learners to transition from isolated letter sounds to applying those letters within words, sentences, and oral reading. The system will be able to store learner performance over time to identify which letter sounds are still challenging. This instructional resource is designed for teachers to use in the classroom, while ReaDirect allows for technology-assisted review. Therefore, phonics sequences and review activities will be critical to ensure learners can decode automatically. 
Reading Rockets (2023) provided strategies for children to attain automaticity with letter sounds. To learn letter sounds quickly, learners should engage in activities that allow them to see, say, and write letters with high frequency. The guide suggested sky-writing, tracing sandpaper letters, and quick card reviews as ways to stimulate memories of letter sounds. Associating letters with speech is critical for beginners because too much cognitive effort is placed on sounding out words. If learners struggle to recognize graphemes, “word recognition will be labored and slow”. By teaching letter sounds until they can be recognized automatically, learners will have more time and attention to blend, understand, and comprehend text. This article relates to the current study because ReaDirect can help learners automate their knowledge of letter sounds. Given enough exposure and practice, learners should be able to read words more effortlessly. Although the system may not allow for hands-on activities, similar learning outcomes can be programmed through technology. ReaDirect will allow learners to hear, say, and read target sounds during structured repetition. Reading Rockets offered literacy techniques that could be applied in person, while ReaDirect will monitor learner performance through speech recognition technology. Thus, the system will still need to be designed thoughtfully to maximize automaticity gains. 
The National Center for Improving Literacy (2026) described additional best practices for teaching letter-sound correspondences, including sequencing and pacing of instruction. The instructional guide discouraged the use of programs like “letter of the week” that space out instruction too much and hinder learners from gaining usable decoding skills. Instead, teachers should introduce 2-3 letters per week while continuing to review old correspondences daily. Letter/sounds that could be easily confused by beginning readers, like b and d or f and v should also be taught separately because similarities in how they look and sound can create interference during early decoding. These directives apply to ReaDirect because they consider how learners interact with letters and sound across lessons. If two confusing sounds are introduced back-to-back without spacing or review, learners may give incorrect responses that impact performance data and confidence levels. The system can account for this by tracking errors and prompting teachers to reteach certain letters. NCIL focused primarily on instruction, while ReaDirect’s premise is informed assessment and digital scaffolding. While this technical difference exists, the concepts remain influential to ReaDirect design because the system should practice letters systematically while enabling teachers to make instructional decisions. 
Timberlake et al. (2024) published a case-based report detailing how to teach a learner with complex communication needs letter-sound correspondences. This learner used an augmented and alternative communication device to participate in literacy activities. Rather than having him respond completely independent at the start, researchers used a most-to-least prompting approach with errorless learning and multiple-choice responses. As he answered more items correctly, the amount of support provided was reduced until he could correctly answer without support. This example relates to ReaDirect because any early literacy assessment system should do more than score learners. If learners struggle with vocal pronunciation, confidence, or simply knowing the correct letter, systematic prompts or guided multiple-choice answers can prevent them from feeling overwhelmed by assessment. The difference between this report and ReaDirect is that the study focused on working with a single learner who required special equipment to respond. ReaDirect will be used by many learners who can respond independently but may still need help via embedded prompts and activities. Nonetheless, the study’s emphasis on scaffolding instructions can influence how learners are supported through ReaDirect. 
A 2024 study on spelling acquisition agreed that teaching letter sounds is more beneficial when learners use the correspondences during blending, word building, and writing. When learners practiced writing real words and pseudowords with their newly learned sound-symbol correspondences, they outperformed their peers who practiced the sounds in isolation. Findings like these emphasize why decoding letters in isolation does not guarantee readers will recognize those same letters within words. Learners benefit from opportunities to blend sounds together, break apart words, and utilize their knowledge of letter-sounds within different types of literacy activities. ReaDirect’s foundation is similar because learners begin at the letter-level and gradually work toward pronouncing words, reading sentences, reading passages, and answering comprehension questions. The difference is that I will be gathering data from oral responses instead of written words and letters, but both techniques understand that letter sounds are meant to culminate in students reading whole words. 
Classroom materials titled “Phonics Mastery – From Basic to Advanced” (2023) outlined a sequence that began with identifying isolated sounds for each letter and progressed to blending CV and CVC words before moving into segmentation and spelling. This publication framed phonics instruction as a process that gradually builds students’ reading capabilities with each subskill acting as a prerequisite for the next. There is value in applying such scope and sequence to beginning reading because learners must develop automaticity before they can blend efficiently and comprehend what they read. The current study fits within this framework because ReaDirect progresses through stages of reading development starting with diagnostic lesson and advancing through corrective modules and reassessment. With ReaDirect, teachers can also see if learners are ready to progress from letters to whole words or if more practice is necessary. In contrast to general classroom resources, ReaDirect contributes speech recognition–powered assessment and analysis of learner responses, allowing for reading performance to be tracked in a more comprehensive way. This example demonstrates one way in which traditional scope and sequence can be augmented using digital technology to organize data and guide instruction. 
In general, the articles reviewed for this literature review highlighted the importance of explicit instruction in letter-sound correspondence. Direct instruction was emphasized because phonemic awareness alone is useless if learners cannot associate sounds with printed letters. Learners who rapidly match letters to sounds are expected to become better at decoding novel words, spelling CV words, and participating in oral reading activities. The literature also made it clear that effective phonics instruction is systematic, cumulative, and multisensory where possible. Learners should also be given frequent chances to review what they have learned and receive corrective feedback when mistakes are made. However, many articles and guides focus on instruction in English or software designed for small group or one-on-one settings. There are less examples that examine how speech recognition technology can reinforce letter-sound knowledge in multilingual, low-resource, and EGRC settings. This study aims to fill that gap by developing ReaDirect into an AI-powered oral reading coach for assessing letter sound knowledge, providing corrective feedback, tracking learner progress, and enabling teachers to plan data-driven phonics instruction for Grade 1 students. 
Decoding Skills 
Crawford et al. (2025) examined foundational decoding skills among pupils in low- and middle-income countries by analyzing Early Grade Reading Assessment data from more than half a million pupils across 48 countries. The study focused on early reading subskills such as letter name identification, letter sound identification, non-word reading, and oral reading fluency. Findings showed that many pupils in the first three instructional years were not acquiring the basic decoding skills needed for successful reading comprehension. The study also reported strong positive relationships between decoding tasks and reading comprehension, indicating that learners who performed better in letter knowledge, letter sounds, non-word reading, and oral reading fluency also tended to perform better in comprehension. This is relevant to the present study because ReaDirect focuses on Grade 1 learners’ foundational English reading skills, including letter-sound recognition, pronunciation, word reading, oral reading fluency, and comprehension. The findings support the need to assess and strengthen early decoding skills before expecting learners to perform higher-level reading tasks. However, Crawford et al. focused on large-scale reading assessment data across different countries, while the present study develops and evaluates a school-based AI-assisted oral reading coach for Grade 1 Developing readers. 
Olinghouse et al. (2020) explored decoding specifically as it may predict future reading comprehension after fluency. This longitudinal study involved first-grade learners who were assessed on their ability to identify what a written symbol says during early literacy instruction. By analyzing decoding skill in relation to fluency and comprehension measures, researchers were able to demonstrate that stronger decoding growth led to higher levels of comprehension. The connection between decoding and comprehension was dependent on fluency because accurate decoding enables learners to read smoothly and with understanding. It connects to the present study because ReaDirect also conceptualizes decoding as a component of oral reading development. Specifically, the system takes note of how learners recognize letter names and sounds, pronounce words, and transition from word-level reading into larger units of text. However, Olinghouse et al.’s work applied decoding as a longitudinal predictor of comprehension, whereas the present research uses immediate feedback through a speech recognition-assisted reading coach that detects learner responses. What links both studies is the crucial role of providing reading materials that correspond to the learner’s decoding ability. 
In her meta-analysis on decodable-text interventions, Odo (2024) sought to understand the effects of controlled texts on early reading performance.
