Studies included in the review implemented interventions using decodable texts, or texts with words centered around letter-sound relationships that learners have already been taught. Results indicated a moderate overall effect size for pseudoword decoding and small-to-moderate effect sizes for real-word reading. This was especially true if decodable texts were paired with explicit phonics instruction. Her work highlights the importance of using texts that align with learners’ phonics knowledge because it was found that decoding skills may plateau or regress if learners are prematurely exposed to leveled or whole-language texts. Related to ReaDirect is the notion that reading materials should have the proper scope and sequence, starting from letters and progressing all the way to sentences and connected passages. With the system, teachers can ensure that learners are provided reading exercises that align with their current decoding competence. Additionally, while Odo (2024) reviewed classroom-based studies on decodable-text intervention, the current project focuses on providing support through a technological application that allows educators to manage oral reading assessment, corrective feedback, and learner placement. Decodable texts are one piece of the reading development puzzle, but considerations should be made regarding learners’ general reading levels. 
Another article by Olinghouse et al. (2020) discussed decoding within the context of code-related intervention. Code-related skills include knowledge on grapheme-phoneme mapping and blending. Through mapping, learners recognize the relationship between printed letters and their corresponding sounds. They then learn how to blend those sounds together to form complete words. According to their study results, decoding instruction that focused on both mapping and blending also helped learners attain higher scores on reading comprehension, even if comprehension was not explicitly taught. This tells us that decoding should not be thought of as merely word identification. Accurate decoding is part of comprehension because it serves as the foundation for making meaning out of text. Similarly, ReaDirect encourages learners to develop decoding proficiency as they are gradually introduced to activities that promote oral reading fluency and comprehension. As they read aloud sentences and passages, their performance data is recorded and can be used by their teachers to decide on what reading materials to assign them next. Unlike the study by Olinghouse et al. (2020), which investigated instructional interventions, the current study implements automated decoding accuracy checks and collects performance data for teachers to use if they choose to provide additional assistance. However, one may ask what factors can influence decoding skills. 
Irish-based longitudinal research (2022) analyzed classroom programs that infused decoding instruction with comprehension skills across one year. The article focused on student outcomes when decoding was paired with comprehension-friendly instruction rather than taught in isolation. Data showed that improvement in decoding and comprehension skills partially explained higher reading and writing standards. This study was relevant because it demonstrated decoding instruction should not be the ceiling for reading development. High-performing readers should use their decoding skills to access text meaning, respond through literacy activities, and strengthen writing abilities. This article relates to the present study because ReaDirect does not leave decoding as an isolated skill. Learners take reading assessments that measure pronunciation accuracy and word errors, but they should also receive support toward fluency and comprehension. The present study differs by utilizing a technology-based reading coach, while the Ireland-based research focused on school program implementation. However, both recognize decoding instruction becomes more valuable when connected to comprehension and writing. 
Research from 2025 focused on enhanced decoding and spelling instruction to improve students’ oral reading fluency. The study compared students who received extra decoding instruction involving spelling patterns, word structure, and frequent high-interest texts. Learners who benefitted from these extended decoding and spelling lessons showed greater improvements in oral reading fluency than learners who experienced basic decoding lessons. This is important because some students may accurately decode beginner-level words but face challenges when words become longer or more complex. The present study connects to this because ReaDirect’s reading progression allows learners to master more than single words through sentence reading, passage reading, and repeated oral reading. While the 2025 study emphasized the importance of advanced decoding and spelling instruction, ReaDirect allows learners to practice these skills through speaking. As students read aloud to the program, they may decode words correctly but get inappropriate feedback if reading is not fluent. This demonstrates how a digital reading program can assess both early decoding skills and later reading fluency. 
Review discussions about Neurocognitive research by O’Donnell-Slater et al. (2005) inform another perspective on decoding in reading. Scientists found readers with stronger decoding skills showed less overall brain activation when reading words. Essentially, learners who decode efficiently can process words with less effort, freeing up cognitive resources for fluency and comprehension. This study was from decades ago and is more biology-based than classroom research, but it supports why decoding should be taught accurately and automatically. ReaDirect encourages this type of practice by allowing students to read aloud repeatedly, monitor pronunciation, and start with word recognition before fluent reading. The present study does not include neurological scans, but it applied the concept that decoding needs guided practice to reach automatity. Automatity in decoding allows comprehension to take center focus, which is why all three should be connected in a multi-tiered literacy model. 
Taken together, these studies suggest that decoding abilities play a critical role during early reading development when learners acquire foundational knowledge about how written symbols represent speech sounds as well as how to read novel words with greater accuracy and independence. Indeed, literature investigating decoding skills, decodable texts, code-related instruction, cognitive supports, SOR routines, as well as high-leverage and later-spawning spelling-based instruction like WilsonReading agree broadly: learners benefit from explicit, systematic, and sequentially-designed instructional support before they develop the ability to read fluently and understand text with confidence. However, much of this work continues to focus on classrooms and learners who primarily receive instruction in English and have relatively high-access to learning materials. In contrast, less is known about how to promote decoding development across multilingual, low-resource, and early-grade contexts where learners may have inconsistent exposure to texts that are highly aligned to phonics rules. The current study aims to address this gap through ReaDirect, an AI-enabled oral reading coach designed to measure decoding-related skill, produce interpretable learner profiles, give learners corrective feedback, and help teachers plan interventions that support diverse Grade 1 readers. 
Word-in-Sentence Reading 
The article “Letters, Words, Sentences, and Reading” (2024) detailed how the human brain processes language at different levels. Using behavioral and neuroimaging data, researchers explained how skilled readers interpret information starting with letters and progressing all the way to sentences. One takeaway from this study was that words are not processed in isolation, as semantic context from sentences can affect how long it takes a person to recognize a specific word. In simple terms, if a person is expecting to read a word because of sentence context, they will recognize the word quicker than if it did not make sense within the sentence. This is applicable to early literacy education because it demonstrates that learners use sentence context to connect decoding with reading comprehension. This study relates to the present study because ReaDirect measures more than just single-word oral reading. Learners also read words within the context of sentences and passages. Although the methods and results of this study are geared more toward skilled reading and cognitive function, it follows the same idea that reading sentences in the early grades can provide insights on whether learners are using syntax and meaning during oral reading. 
“Influence of Sentence-Level Reading Skills on Oral Reading Fluency: The Additive Benefits of Processing-Speed Skills Across Levels of Language” explained how letter, word, and sentence-level reading skills each contributed to a student’s overall reading fluency. To conduct this study, researchers analyzed how different learners processed reading information at the letter, word, and sentence level, focusing on sentence-processing speed. Learners who had higher abilities to read sentences quickly also showed higher general reading fluency scores. This study helps establish sentence reading as a bridge between word recognition and reading comprehension. In the context of the present study, it further validates using reading tasks that go beyond single-word recognition. It is possible for learners to successfully read isolated words but struggle when those same words are placed in sentences. ReaDirect uses oral reading exercises to identify struggling readers and provides connected text reading so teachers can observe how learners read words in the context of a sentence. While the focus of the study was processing speed as it relates to fluent reading, the present study combines word- and sentence-level reading with pronunciation feedback, learner monitoring, and teacher intervention. 
Some structured-literacy programs like Reading Words, Sentences, Stories” (2024) introduce students to words one at a time before moving on to controlled sentences and short stories. This process allows learners to focus on accurately decoding a list of words before reading those words within simple sentences. Notably, there has been a trend of increased accuracy, expression, and comprehension when sentence reading activities directly correspond with phonics patterns that have been previously taught. Structured-literacy lessons that use this methodology helped establish why ReaDirect gradually introduces reading skills that increase in complexity over time. Lessons that progress from flashcard words to reading words in sentence allow teachers to assess whether learners are simply memorizing words or if they can decode words in context. Although students benefit from word-focused reading lessons, technology such as ReaDirect allows teachers to capture learner responses spoken aloud and utilize that information for progress monitoring and personalized feedback. 
The study Word- and Text-Level Processes Contributing to Fluent Reading” (2022) detailed how measures of word recognition and sentence reading can provide insight on overall reading fluency. Researchers used measures like words correct per minute in sentence lists and reading time per sentence to show that reading speed embedded in sentence contexts had a strong relationship with fluent reading. The significance of this study is that reading fluency is more than just how many words per minute a learner can read. Learners must also demonstrate the ability to read words quickly within a sentence while maintaining reading accuracy and comprehension. This research study closely relates to the present study because ReaDirect allows educators to gather data from oral reading tasks at various levels. Learners can be evaluated on letters, words, sentences, and passages to gain additional insight on their reading performance. The primary difference between this study and the present study is that our application allows teachers to use assessment information to provide targeted reading support through pronunciation feedback. 
Prediction at the Intersection of Sentence Context and Word Form” (2023) is an empirical study measuring how sentence context and word form impact word recognition. Eye-tracking and reading-time measurements indicated that words are recognized faster when they are predictable by the surrounding sentence. Word recognition was also influenced by the “predictability of the written form”. The article advances knowledge on word-in-sentence reading because it highlights the fact that learners do not always rely on serial decoding. Learners use sentence context to predict and verify meanings of words during reading. This relates to the current study by justifying the use of sentence-level oral reading. If learners read words in a sentence aloud, teachers can begin to determine if learners are coordinating decoding, context, and meaning. Theory application: The study focuses on the theory of reading behavior and word recognition. ReaDirect implements this concept through a digital reading coach targeted at Grade 1 learners. The link between the two is that while the study focuses on theory, ReaDirect fills the gap of what this looks like in a classroom setting. 
Word- and Sentence-Level Contexts Upon Word Recognition” (1990) is a study frequently referenced by later research that explores how text context can impact word recognition. The study found that when there are associations between words, whether semantic or automatic, lexical decisions can be made faster. Another way of saying this is that reading a sentence can prime related word meanings and support recognition. This study was chosen because even though it is older than the other two articles, it helped form the foundation that word recognition is not processed in isolation. This connects to ReaDirect because having learners read sentences aloud can help determine if learners are using context effectively while reading. ReaDirect does not measure lexical decision-making, but it implements the idea of using context in reading through sentence-based oral reading tasks. 
The Effectiveness of Six-word Sentences as Reading Comprehension Tool for EFL Learners” (2022) investigated the use of six-word sentences as a reading comprehension tool. Rather than reading passages verbatim, students were given sentence frames to summarize information and identify central ideas. Learners who practiced with these six-word sentences showed improvements in identifying main ideas and reading connected text. They performed better when the sentence frame was related to the passage they read. I found this study inspiring because Grade 1 students may need the support of short, accessible sentence structures before tackling longer passages. ReaDirect may encourage this by providing sentence-level tasks that require learners to read short sentences with intent and comprehension. While this study was conducted with English language learners, and focused on comprehension, it inspired a feature that could support learners in reading programs. Both use the six-word sentence structure to support reading comprehension in learners. 
Reading Words, Sentences, Stories – Pride Reading Program” (2024) is an instruction manual that outlines reading instruction as follows: Learners read through a list of letter-sound patterns, read connected words using those sounds, read connected sentences using those words, answer questions about the sentence, then reread the sentence with better expression. I chose this reading guide because it closely mimics the methodology of ReaDirect. Sentences are used as a form of practice and assessment. By reading aloud, teachers can see how learners decode words, their pacing, how they phrase sentences, and if they understood the text. ReaDirect guides learners through activities with gradually increasing difficulty while teachers can monitor learner performance on each activity. ReaDirect differs because it records learners’ oral readings and uses speech recognition to determine how well their oral response matches the expected text. The reading program provides instructions for teachers to carry out during lessons. ReaDirect provides a technology-based approach to apply some of the same activities at an early grade level. 
Taken together, these studies highlight that word-in-sentence reading likely supports early literacy development by bridging the gap between meaningless isolated word recognition tasks and purposeful reading. Sentences contextualize learning how to decode, recognize words in relation to others, apply syntax, and start constructing meaning during oral reading. Sentence processing research, fluency measurement tools, reading prediction, structured literacy lessons, and scaffolding with short sentences all demonstrate benefits of sentence-level reading beyond those of isolated word recognition tasks. However, much of the research to date focuses on English-medium, monolingual, or otherwise higher-resource classrooms and learners, and many focus on skilled or older readers. Less focus has been put on examining how sentence-embedded oral reading can best be supported among early-grade learners in multilingual and low-resource classrooms where students may encounter code-switching, disparities in exposure to print, or difficulties mapping word recognition skills to connected text. The current study aims to bridge this gap by iteratively designing ReaDirect as an AI-enabled oral reading coach to detect learners' word-in-sentence reading performance, differentiate it from their ability to recognize isolated words, offer corrective feedback, and support teachers in designing personalized reading activities for their Grade 1 students. 
Pause Duration in Reading 
O’Connor et al. (2022) analyzed children’s pause habits during reading tasks as related to fluency and comprehension. In their study, researchers noted how frequently children paused between words and whether they were breaking down text into short phrases. This was measured while reading texts that were calibrated to children’s reading ability level. Less frequent pausing between words and proper phrasing were both found to contribute to higher fluency scores and correct answers on a comprehension quiz. The study’s significance lies in demonstrating how pauses can indicate whether a learner approaches reading word-by-word or smoothly through meaningful phrases. This work relates to ReaDirect in that the system could detect similar behaviors during oral reading evaluation. By looking at where learners pause in the middle of reading tasks, teachers could have a better idea of whether a learner struggles with decoding, proper phrasing, or reading flow. While the previous study treated pausing as a fluency and comprehension variable, the current study will use speech recognition to highlight important features of oral reading. If pause information can be used to support teacher feedback or learning intervention, its utility will be stronger when measured over time and across learners. 
Researchers in Chile conducted a longitudinal study on schoolchildren (2023) that analyzed pause length and reading comprehension. Across multiple assessments, researchers measured learners’ reading behavior and pause durations compared to their comprehension scores. High comprehension students tended to have shorter pauses that were less frequent, while low comprehenders paused longer and more often. Some longer pauses during reading even appeared to show learners struggling to recognize words or plan what to read next. The current study relates to this example by viewing pauses as indicators of difficulty during oral reading. When using ReaDirect, teachers could pay attention to long pauses as a learner reads to determine what type of support they need. If a learner consistently pauses before pronouncing words, they may need decoding or vocabulary support. Alternatively, if pauses occur at the end of phrases or sentences, a learner may need support with proper reading phrasing. The Chilean study utilized pause durations as part of a longitudinal analysis, while pauses will be analyzed at a single time point in the present study. Pauses can be used as instant measures of reading difficulty, but they are more useful when teachers can review past readings to notice patterns. 
A recent 2023 study exploring pause evolution in beginning and advanced elementary readers found that pausing behaviors develop over time and with reading skill. Beginners tended to pause within words while more advanced readers paused frequently around phrase and sentence breaks. There is value in this study because effective reading is not speech without pauses. Fluent readers make pauses that support appropriate expression, phrasing, and comprehension. ReaDirect extends this idea by supporting teachers in distinguishing between pauses that harm reading fluency (such as long pauses during words) versus pauses that improve comprehension (such as short pauses at periods, commas, or natural phrase breaks). This recent study observed how pause placement changes with reading skill, while the present study seeks to use pause analysis during early-grade reading assessment and teaching. This complementary relationship can support nuanced interpretation of pause duration as teachers work with individual students. 
Taken together, these studies demonstrate that pause duration can be meaningfully related to reading fluency, processing effort, and comprehension. Shorter pauses that are placed at appropriate points in text are typically associated with smoother reading and stronger comprehension. Long pauses or frequent pausing can indicate difficulty with decoding, word recognition, phrasing, or sentence processing. Pause duration should be considered alongside other information about the learner because long pauses can also occur when students reflect on punctuation, plan word expression, or process complex ideas. Additionally, most pause-related research has focused on lab-based eye-tracking, controlled reading tasks, or student populations with high proficiency in English. More research is needed to explore how pause behaviors can be interpreted during oral reading assessment and supported by teachers in early-grade classrooms where many learners read in multilingual settings. The present study aims to fill this gap by introducing ReaDirect, an AI-based oral reading coach that can analyze pause placement during oral reading, graphically represent fluent reading behaviors, deliver error feedback, and support teachers. 
Rhyming Words 
McArthur et al. (2024) explored rhyming poetry and its impact on oral reading fluency and comprehension instruction. Learners went through repeated reading of rhyme-rich texts where authors used rhythmic language that prompted thinking about how words sound, end, are paced, and what they mean. After repeating the poems, children showed signs of becoming more fluent readers while their reading comprehension was also being supported. This study relates to ReaDirect because rhyming enhances learners’ ability to hear word patterns which can be foundational to reading. Because our system allows learners to practice reading aloud, get pronunciation feedback, and respond to prompts, using rhyming activities can encourage learners to notice similar sounds and gain confidence reading certain words. McArthur et al. focused on repeated exposure to poetry as a vehicle for literacy fluency; the current study involves phonological components in the context of an AI reading system. Connecting the two studies generates additional support for how rhyming can improve reading fluency as well as phonemic awareness. 
Zavala et al. (2024) conducted a similar study on the impact of repeated reading and poetry with rhyme on first-grade learners. Learners were separated into two groups where one had rhyming-poetry instruction while the other had typical repeated-reading guidance. The students who engaged with rhyming poetry showed greater improvement in phonemic awareness, oral reading fluency, and comprehension. This research helps build the foundation for ReaDirect because rhyming can help support multiple reading skills for learners in Grade 1. Our system encourages learners to read aloud and provides feedback on pronunciation while presenting reading support activities. By learning to recognize rhymes, learners may connect how words sound when they are spoken to how they sound in their head when reading silently. Zavala et al. implemented their study in a classroom setting where students listened to and read poems; this study involves using speech recognition to better understand learner reading performance and deliver support. Both studies share an interest in how rhyme can promote reading fluency, which ties into how phonological awareness benefits reading comprehension. 
An extensive 2021 review discussed how phonological awareness serves as the foundation of reading acquisition. The article described how children learning to read become sensitive to certain sound patterns which help them read and spell. Children who recognize phonemes can also break down larger phonological units such as words into smaller components. Identifying rhymes was discussed as an activity to promote awareness of sound similarities, which may contribute to the development of phonemic awareness. Children can become better readers when they are first taught to read using phonics instruction. This correlates to ReaDirect because the system recognizes oral reading as multiple parts, not simply word pronunciation. Reading activities on the system require learners to recognize words, produce connected speech, and develop towards reading fluently. The review implied that, when used in isolation, rhyming can have limited effects on reading development. Rhyming can be made more effective when accompanied by the explicit teaching of phonemes, letter-sounds, and phoneme blending. This might further inform how rhyming activities will be used with ReaDirect; they should not serve as the sole reading intervention. 
Reading Rockets (2023) highlighted phonological awareness instructions and assessment through foundational sound awareness to phonemic awareness. Their guidelines detailed how learners may recognize larger sound units such as rhymes before distinguishing smaller sounds. There was also mention of children developing from being able to recognize rhymes to blending and segmenting phonemes. This ties into ReaDirect because the system offers reading activities that start with letter-sound recognition and gradually increase complexity towards full sentences. With ReaDirect, teachers can better determine if learners are able to hear phonemes within words before asking them to read sentences. Reading Rockets provided guidance for educators while ReaDirect allows for technological instruction and oversight. young children who are at risk of reading difficulties may require more support progressing through these stages. 
Deffieux et al. (2025) explored how phonological awareness skills could be enhanced in students with learning disabilities. The study used a learning intervention that allowed students to practice reading through rhyming games, computerized activities, and explicit teacher-modeled instructions. Students who went through the intervention showed improvements to their phonological awareness and ability to read. Their findings suggested that rhyming activities were useful when paired with letter-sound knowledge so students could associate how words sound when spoken and read. This ties into ReaDirect because our system could support learners who may need more exposure to letter-sound recognition, correct pronunciation, and reading practice. The study proved that students with learning disabilities could benefit from structured rhyming activities as part of their reading development. Deffieux et al. focused on learners with learning disabilities while this study considers all Grade 1 learners. However, the principle stands that children can improve their reading ability when they have ample time to engage with activities that promote phonological awareness. 
Literacy Learn’s how to teach rhyming words infographic (2025) highlighted classroom routines to help transition learners from rhyme recognition to rhyme production. Suggestions included starting with listening tasks, read aloud, and matching rhymes before having learners produce their own rhyming words. This progression is key because requiring learners to produce rhymes asks them to use a more sophisticated level of sound awareness than simply identifying two words as rhyming or not. This progression can help ReaDirect frame how rhyming-related activities may be distributed across early literacy exercises. Learners who demonstrate rhyming word recognition can later be prompted to produce words with similar sound-patterns. Producing rhymes can help bolster vocabulary, pronunciation, and decoding readiness. While Literacy Learn centered their resource around classroom practices, ReaDirect applies similar concepts through an AI oral reading coach. Both resources connect by implying that activities involving rhyming should be developmentally sequenced, not just introduced as standalone games with little relation to reading development. 
Edmonton Public Library’s rhythms and rhymes in early literacy blog post (2026) illustrated how books with rhyming text, nursery rhymes, and stories with rhythm allow children to hear smaller sounds within words. The article expanded on previous ideas by reinforcing how rhythm and rhyme exposure builds phonemic awareness, memory, vocabulary, and early language skills. Rhythm and rhyme practices can translate to early-grade reading as young learners tend to engage with language that is repetitive, predictable, and easy to memorize through song or dance. Pairing this information with ReaDirect, rhythm and rhyme can strengthen oral reading activities by having learners closely listen to text, repeat sounds, and build confidence saying words out loud. The article provides a contrast to ReaDirect because library resources to support early literacy are typically created for parents, caregivers, and adult readers looking for tips to support informal reading. ReaDirect is an artificial intelligence tool meant to structure how teachers interact with learners during school-time reading activities. Despite this difference, using meaningful and often familiar language patterns with young learners is crucial to ensure they complete confidence-building exercises before being pushed to more challenging literacy skills. 
Rhyming words have been consistently connected to reading development because sound patterns encourage learners to think about phonological awareness, decoding, vocabulary, and oral reading fluency. Singing songs, playing games, and learning rhymes are fun activities for young learners, but they become more effective when taught systematically and linked to larger reading skills. Children should experience rhymes through listening, closely recognizing similar sounds, matching rhyming words, and production of rhymes. Once learners show proficiency with rhyming words, they can apply their sound-pattern awareness to reading and spelling. Although research into rhyme development is steadily growing, most studies are isolated to English-medium K–2 classrooms. Further research is needed to support how children develop rhyming skills in multilingual classrooms or understand rhyming patterns in their first language if local language rhymes are not used during instruction. The current study hopes to address these gaps by developing ReaDirect as an AI oral reading coach that can elicit rhyming-related responses, measure accuracy, provide corrective feedback, and help teachers individualize reading instruction for diverse Grade 1 learners. 
ASR for Children’s Speech 
Wills et al. (2023), Rolland and Abad (2024), and Zhang et al. (2024) examined Automatic Speech Recognition for children’s read and spontaneous speech. Their findings show that age, non-native pronunciation, recording conditions, and limited child-speech data can reduce recognition accuracy. They also reported that targeted adaptation and child-like speech augmentation can improve recognition performance. These studies are relevant to ReaDirect because the system processes young learners’ speech under similar constraints. However, the reviewed studies focused mainly on improving or evaluating recognition models, while ReaDirect applies ASR within a Grade 1 oral reading coach that combines audio-quality checks, expected-answer matching, rule-based scoring, and child-friendly feedback. 
Evers and Chen (2022), Dillon and Wells (2023), and Inceoglu, Chen, and Lim (2023) highlighted the role of Automatic Speech Recognition in pronunciation instruction, feedback, and speech intelligibility assessment. Evers and Chen (2022) studied the use of ASR in conjunction with peer feedback in pronunciation learning. Evers and Chen (2022) studied ASR with peer feedback in pronunciation learning, finding that learners improved more when ASR was combined with peer feedback and classroom interaction. This suggests that automatic recognition is more effective when learners receive comments, compare their pronunciation, and adjust their speech based on feedback. Dillon and Wells (2023) examined ASR-based pronunciation training among Korean learners of English. Learners showed some improvement in accurate word pronunciation after practicing with ASR, though the improvement was modest. This suggests that ASR can support pronunciation learning when used with teacher guidance, structured activities, and regular practice. However, ASR should be viewed as a support tool, not a replacement for teacher guidance, since pronunciation development still requires human feedback and proper instructional support. Inceoglu et al. (2023) examined how ASR-based assessment compares with the judgment of human listeners. Inceoglu et al. (2023) compared ASR-based assessment with human listeners' judgment, finding that ASR can check speech intelligibility by providing an initial view of how understandable a learner's speech may be. However, the study also emphasized that ASR has limitations. It may miss certain pronunciation errors or interpret a learner's speech differently from human listeners. ASR should be used as a supporting tool in speech evaluation, with results reviewed carefully to avoid inaccurate or unfair conclusions. ASR should be used as a supporting tool in speech evaluation, with results reviewed carefully to avoid inaccurate or unfair conclusions. 
Words Correct Per Minute and Reading Fluency Studies 
Dogar, Shah, Imran, and Almusharraf (2025) examined oral reading fluency in English as a Second Language among Grade 7 learners in Pakistan. The researchers used a one-minute reading test to calculate learners’ WCPM and determine their oral reading fluency level. Their results showed that the average oral reading fluency rate was 72 WCPM, which was below the expected benchmark for Grade 7 ESL learners. The study also noted that many learners fell within a low percentile compared with general fluency norms. This study is connected to the present study because it demonstrates how WCPM can identify learners who may need reading support. Unlike Dogar et al., who focused on Grade 7 ESL learners in Pakistan, the present study applies WCPM within an early-grade oral reading coach designed to provide assessment, feedback, and guided practice for younger learners. 
Wang, Beigman Klebanov, O’Reilly, and Sabatini (2024) investigated oral reading fluency monitoring through electronic shared book reading using Relay Reader. The study involved 50 children who took turns reading aloud with a prerecorded narrator, producing 6,092 oral reading responses across 1,093 book passages. Each response was evaluated using a speech engine that calculated WCPM. The researchers found that reading performance varied across text level, individual differences, and within-learner changes, with learners improving by about 2.26 WCPM for every 10,000 words read. This study is closely related to the present study because it shows that speech technology can be used to monitor oral reading fluency repeatedly across many reading samples. While Wang et al. focused on shared book reading and growth monitoring, the present study applies WCPM within an assessment and feedback system for structured learner activities. 
Mutema and Pretorius (2024) explored the relationship between reading comprehension and oral reading fluency. Their study examined whether slow reading matters by analyzing how fluency measures relate to learners’ comprehension performance. The work is important because WCPM is often used as a fluency measure, yet comprehension remains the broader goal of reading instruction. Their study reinforces the idea that learners who read slowly may face difficulty understanding text, especially when reading speed limits, The ability to process meaning efficiently. For the present study, this supports the use of WCPM as a practical indicator for identifying learners who may need fluency support. Still, WCPM alone cannot fully explain comprehension, so the proposed system should combine it with other reading indicators such as accuracy, pronunciation, and comprehension-related tasks. 
Da Silva et al. (2025) developed a machine learning approach for assessing reading fluency in elementary grades. Their study used audio recordings from Grade 2 and Grade 3 learners across 144 Brazilian schools and applied automatic speech recognition to extract fluency-related features. The researchers compared eleven machine learning algorithms to classify and estimate reading fluency levels using accuracy in word decoding, reading speed, and prosody. Their results showed that reading speed was the strongest indicator across experiments, while prosody still contributed to more detailed fluency estimation. This study is strongly connected to the present study because it combines automated speech processing, fluency features, and machine learning for scalable reading assessment. The distinction is that da Silva et al. focused on model comparison for fluency classification, while the present system integrates ASR-based fluency indicators with learner feedback and reading support. 
Word Recognition 
Several studies found that rhyming activities support phonological awareness and early literacy development by helping the children recognize sound patterns and word boundaries through repeated and engaging language play. For instance, MacKenzie et al. (2021) found that rhyming in preschool settings significantly contributes to phonological awareness development with focused rhyme tasks improving sound segmentation among children aged 4 to 6 years. Similarly, Šimunović et al. (2021) emphasized that rhyme production and matching activities are strongly associated with broader pre reading phonological skills especially in structured early education environments. These studies are related because both highlight rhyming as an accessible entry point for developing phonemic sensitivity which supports children’s transition toward decoding even before they acquire advanced letter knowledge. 
Reading Fluency 
Several researches have shown that the reading fluency improves through targeted interventions because it builds automatic in word recognition, prosody, and reading rate essential for comprehension. For example, Gorsuch et al. (2021) found that repeated reading with prosody feedback significantly increased words correct per minute and expression in elementary students. Similarly, Sánchez et al. (2021) demonstrated that digital fluency programs during COVID 19 enhanced oral reading speed and accuracy in grades 2 to 5. These studies are connected because both have highlight repeated exposure's role in shifting cognitive resources from decoding to meaning making. 
Letter-Sound Correspondence 
Several recent studies indicate that instruction in letter sound correspondence strengthens decoding skills by establishing clear grapheme phoneme connections that are necessary for accurate word recognition. For example, Ehri et al. (2022) found that explicit phonics teaching of letter-sound links improved reading accuracy by 25% in kindergarteners compared to embedded approaches. Similarly, Mesmer and Griffith (2021) demonstrated that the systematic synthetic phonics, emphasizing individual letter sound blends, outperformed analytic methods in early grade 1 reading gains. These studies are connected because both highlight the superiority of direct explicit mapping over implicit discovery for building foundational decoding. 
Early Reading Comprehension 
Recent researches shows that the early reading comprehension improves when children are directly taught reading strategies. These strategies help young learners understand the meaning of a text, not just read the words. For example, Kim and Phillips (2022) found that the teaching inference and summarizing strategies improved comprehension scores by 30% among kindergarten to grade 3 students. Similarly, Elleman and Compton (2021) showed that reading aloud activities with vocabulary and questions helped the young readers to understand story elements better. These studies are connected because both show that the children learn better when they actively use reading strategies instead of just listening or reading without any guidance. 
Synthesis 
In order to accomplish the objectives of the present study, the researchers carefully examined the related literature and studies that support the development of ReaDirect, an AI-based speech recognition-assisted oral reading coach for Grade 1 learners using the DepEd ARAL Program. The reviewed materials provide a foundation for understanding artificial intelligence in language education, automatic speech recognition, transcript evaluation, audio preprocessing, pronunciation assessment, reading fluency measurement, child-friendly feedback, and technology-supported literacy intervention. Through these works, the researchers were able to identify the concepts, methods, and system features that can guide the development of a reading support tool designed for young learners. 
The review of literature on artificial intelligence in language education shows that AI is increasingly used to support language learning, pronunciation practice, tutoring, and learner feedback. Studies by Zhu and Wang (2025), Dennis (2024), and Létourneau et al. (2025) show that AI tools such as automated tutoring systems, speech recognition, and feedback systems can improve learner engagement and performance. These works are important to the present study because ReaDirect also uses AI to support language learning, particularly oral reading and pronunciation. However, many existing studies focus on general language learning, older learners, or broad K–12 applications, while the present study focuses on Grade 1 learners who need early reading support under the DepEd ARAL Program. 
Several studies on speech technologies and automatic speech recognition further support the technical direction of the system. Works on speech-to-text, text-to-speech, and ASR explain how spoken learner responses can be converted into readable transcripts and used for assessment. Studies by Madhusudhana Reddy et al. (2023), Kouidri (2023), Bachiri et al. (2024), and Prabhavalkar et al. (2023) show that ASR can be useful for pronunciation practice, reading assessment, and learner-centered feedback. These studies are closely related to ReaDirect because the system depends on ASR to capture learner reading responses, generate transcripts, compare them with expected answers, and provide feedback. Still, these studies also show the need for careful validation because speech recognition may produce errors, especially when used with young learners or beginner readers. 
The studies on audio preprocessing, voice activity detection, noise reduction, and 16 kHz audio resampling also contribute to the technical design of the system. Works by Oh (2020), Barkovska and Havrashenko (2023), Dinkel et al. (2021), Xu et al. (2021), Zhu et al. (2023), Jia et al. (2024) and Huang (2025) show that speech quality affects recognition accuracy. These studies explain the importance of preparing audio through normalization, silence handling, noise reduction, and proper sampling before it is passed to the ASR model. In the present study, these processes are needed because learner recordings may include background noise, unclear speech, pauses, or inconsistent microphone quality. Proper preprocessing helps improve transcript generation and supports more reliable scoring. 
The review also shows the importance of robust ASR, phoneme recognition, phoneme similarity, grapheme-to-phoneme conversion, and task-specific classification in speech-based assessment. Studies by Radford et al. (2023), Gong et al. (2022), Korzekwa et al. (2022), El Kheir et al. (2023), Ryu et al. (2023), Shahin and Ahmed (2024), Do et al. (2024), Kadambi et al. (2024), Cao et al. (2026), Mahmudi and Veisi (2021), Řezáčková et al. (2021), and Ploujnikov and Ravanelli (2022) show that recognition can be strengthened when a system considers sound-level and phoneme-level information. These studies are useful to ReaDirect because young learners may produce developing or unclear speech, and the system must determine whether a response is close to the expected sound or word rather than relying on exact transcript matching alone. 
The literature on rule-based scoring and AI feedback design supports the need for transparent and understandable evaluation. Rule-based and hybrid scoring approaches allow the system to use clear indicators such as pronunciation accuracy, transcript matching, Words Correct Per Minute, fluency, and comprehension-related responses. At the same time, studies on AI tutoring and feedback show that learners benefit from immediate, supportive, and personalized guidance. This is connected to the present study because ReaDirect is not only intended to score learner responses but also to guide learners through child-friendly feedback. The system must therefore balance automation with fairness, clarity, and teacher-guided interpretation. 
The related studies on reading fluency, word recognition, letter-sound correspondence, and early reading comprehension show that reading development depends on several connected skills. Studies by MacKenzie et al. (2021), Šimunović et al. (2021), Gorsuch et al. (2021), Sánchez et al. (2021), Ehri et al. (2022), Mesmer and Griffith (2021), Kim and Phillips (2022), and Elleman and Compton (2021) emphasize the importance of phonological awareness, repeated reading, decoding, letter-sound mapping, and comprehension strategies. These studies support the structure of ReaDirect because the system is designed to help Grade 1 learners practice different reading skills, not just produce a score. The reviewed works also show that young learners need guided practice, clear prompts, and meaningful feedback to improve their reading ability. 
The empirical studies on ASR-based reading assessment, AI-assisted pronunciation tools, application-based learning systems, and teacher support further strengthen the need for the present study. Studies involving Whisper, ELSA Speak, ASR-supported pronunciation systems, and digital fluency tools show that speech-based technologies can support pronunciation improvement, fluency monitoring, and learner engagement. Other studies on teacher workload and early reading interventions show that teachers need support tools that can help monitor learners and reduce repetitive assessment tasks. These studies are relevant because ReaDirect is designed not to replace teachers but to assist them in assessing oral reading, monitoring learner progress, and providing reading support aligned with ARAL. 
Overall, the reviewed literature and studies show that AI, ASR, transcript evaluation, phoneme-based analysis, audio preprocessing, WCPM, and child-friendly feedback can work together in building an oral reading support system. However, many existing works are focused on older learners, second-language learners, foreign datasets, commercial pronunciation applications, or standalone technical models. Few directly address Grade 1 learners in the Philippine basic education context, particularly under the DepEd ARAL Program. This gap supports the development of ReaDirect as a localized AI-based oral reading coach that combines speech recognition, learner-response analysis, rule-based scoring, pronunciation support, fluency monitoring, and teacher-guided feedback for early-grade reading interventio 
CHAPTER III 
RESEARCH METHODOLOGY 
This chapter presents the research methodology used in the design, development, implementation, and evaluation of the study entitled “ReaDirect: AI-Based Speech Recognition-Assisted Oral Reading Coach as a Learning Support Intervention for Grade 1 Learners Using the DepEd ARAL Program.” It discusses the research design, locale of the study, respondents and sampling technique, ethical considerations and data privacy, applied concepts and techniques, algorithm analysis, data collection methods, data model generation, system development methodology, software tools used, system architecture, and software testing procedures. 
The methodology followed a systematic process beginning with the identification of the English reading and pronunciation support needs of Grade 1 learners classified under the Developing-reader profile, followed by the design and development of the ReaDirect system. The study involved the preparation of ARAL-informed English reading content, collection and organization of speech-related data, integration of Automatic Speech Recognition, audio preprocessing, transcript generation, transcript correction, scoring, feedback generation, system integration, and software testing. These procedures were necessary because the study required both a functional educational software application and an AI-assisted speech-processing workflow capable of analyzing learner pronunciation and oral reading responses. 
ReaDirect was developed as a web-based oral reading and pronunciation support system for Grade 1 learners, with the thesis implementation focused on learners classified as Developing readers through the ReaDirect English CRLA-style reading profile. The system enables learners to complete English diagnostic tasks, alphabet pronunciation activities, rhyme-decision exercises, word-reading activities, sentence-reading tasks, module exercises, module mastery checks, final assessment, and final reassessment as part of a scaffolded English oral reading support process. It also provides teachers and administrators with structured learner records for progress monitoring, transcript review, and quality assurance. 
The system used a separated application and AI-service structure. The Laravel web application served as the main system responsible for authentication, learner flow, system scoring, module placement, module progression, assessment records, dashboards, and database storage. The Python FastAPI service served as the AI component responsible for audio processing, Whisper transcription for longer responses, isolated-letter classification, phoneme evidence generation, audio-quality validation, transcript correction, and speech-analysis metadata. The system also used a deterministic Miss Ciel feedback component for coaching responses and a voice-line support pipeline for spoken agent messages. These components made the learner-facing interaction more natural and engaging while learner outcomes remained controlled by the Laravel application. 
To maintain transparency in speech processing, the system preserved the raw transcript, corrected transcript, and displayed transcript for ASR-supported tasks, together with the predicted label and confidence for isolated-letter tasks. The raw transcript represented the direct Whisper output. The corrected transcript represented the output after safe expected-centric correction, while the displayed transcript represented the learner-facing version. Preserving these fields allowed the researchers to compare direct model behavior with evidence-based interpretation while still providing understandable feedback to learners. 
The overall methodology of the study followed a sequential process that began with identifying the English reading and pronunciation support needs of Grade 1 Developing readers, followed by requirement analysis, content preparation, system design, prototype development, ASR model preparation, integration, testing, and refinement. This methodological flow is presented in figure 3 to show how the educational, technical, and evaluation procedures were connected throughout the development of ReaDirect. 

Figure 3 . Research Methodology Flow of the Study 
As shown in this figure, the methodology did not focus only on software development, but also included speech-processing preparation, English reading content organization, system testing, and refinement. This flow served as the basis for the research design and development procedures discussed in the succeeding sections. 
Research Design 
The study used a developmental research design with a one-group pretest-posttest pre-experimental design, supported by technical evaluation. This design was selected because the study involved the development of a functional educational technology prototype and the measurement of learner performance before and after using the ReaDirect system. The developmental design addressed the design, implementation, testing, and refinement of the AI-assisted oral reading coach, while the pre-experimental design was applied through the system’s initial diagnostic assessment, scaffolded English reading support activities, and final reassessment. Since the study used one group of Grade 1 Developing readers without a separate control group, the design was appropriate for observing changes in learner performance after system use, but not for making strong causal claims. Knapp (2016) emphasized that the one-group pretest-posttest design has limited causal interpretability because it lacks a control group and may be affected by threats such as history, maturation, testing, instrumentation, and regression. 
The study was developmental because the main output was a working software prototype. The researchers designed and developed ReaDirect as an AI-assisted oral reading coach with learner, teacher, scoring, database, AI-service, Miss Ciel’s feedback component, and agent voice-line support components. The developmental aspect of the study was reflected in the creation of the learner interface, audio recording module, ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic flow, module-based English reading activities, final reassessment flow, teacher monitoring features, administrator quality assurance tools, scoring services, feedback mechanisms, agent voice support, and AI service integration. This is aligned with research and development because R&D in education focuses on producing useful educational products, including software-based programs for learning, evaluation, management, and instructional support. Development research also involves the systematic design, development, testing, evaluation, and revision of a product until it satisfies its intended educational purpose. Therefore, the developmental design was appropriate for ReaDirect because the study aimed to create, test, and refine a functional educational technology prototype based on defined learner, teacher, and system requirements (Umar et al., 2023). 
The study was pre-experimental because it used a one-group pretest-posttest structure. The same group of Grade 1 learners classified as Developing readers first completed the ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic assessment, which served as the pretest or baseline measure. Afterward, the learners used the scaffolded English reading support activities and modules provided by the system. They then completed the final reassessment, which served as the posttest measure. The comparison between the initial diagnostic results and final reassessment results allowed the researchers to determine whether there were observable improvements in pronunciation, phonological awareness, word recognition, oral reading fluency, and reading comprehension after using ReaDirect. However, following Knapp’s (2016) caution on the limitations of one-group pretest-posttest designs, the observed differences were interpreted as performance changes within the same group and not as conclusive evidence that the system alone caused the improvement. 
The study also included technical evaluation of system procedures. The researchers tested ASR behavior, audio preprocessing, expected-centric transcript correction, phoneme comparison, Word Error Rate, Character Error Rate, fluency metrics, ReaDirect English CRLA-style scoring, reading comprehension classification, module placement, mastery progression, feedback generation, and voice-output behavior. This technical evaluation was necessary because ReaDirect was not only an educational intervention tool but also a software system with AI-assisted speech-processing components. Different learner response types created different technical challenges. Isolated letters may be transcribed as common words, word-level tasks may involve homophones or near-homophones, and sentence-level tasks require alignment, fluency, pacing, and error analysis. 
The research design reflected both Computer Science and Information Technology components. The Computer Science component focused on speech recognition, Whisper inference, isolated-letter classification, phoneme evidence, transcript correction, similarity comparison, error metrics, scoring algorithms, ASR evaluation, and agent feedback generation. The Information Technology component focused on web application development, database management, user interface design, system integration, deployment preparation, voice-line integration, and software testing. This combination was appropriate because the study did not only propose an algorithmic solution but also integrated the solution into a functional educational system prototype. 
Locale of the Study 
The study was conducted at Duhat Elementary School, located in Duhat, Santa Cruz, Laguna. The target participants were Grade 1 learners classified as Developing readers based on the updated Grade 1 Filipino Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment results and subsequently assessed through the ReaDirect English CRLA-style reading profile. The locale provided a school-based learning environment where English reading assessment, pronunciation practice, learner-support activities, intervention procedures, and teacher monitoring could be implemented and observed. 
As part of the preliminary data-gathering and research-locale selection process, the researchers coordinated with the DepEd Santa Cruz Sub-Office to identify a suitable school for the proposed pilot implementation. Based on the available comparative Grade 1 assessment data and the implementation requirements of the study, the DepEd Santa Cruz Sub-Office endorsed Duhat Elementary School as the most suitable research locale. The endorsement was supported by assessment data showing that 89.10% of the assessed Grade 1 learners in Duhat Elementary School were identified as struggling readers, representing the highest documented proportion among the schools included in the site-selection comparison. 
For the purpose of selecting the research locale, the term struggling readers was operationally used to refer collectively to assessed Grade 1 learners whose Filipino CRLA reading profiles indicated a need for reading intervention. The term was used only as a school-level indicator of the extent of reading-support need and was not treated as a separate or official CRLA classification. 
The broader struggling-reader data were used to establish the reading-intervention need of the school and did not directly determine which learners would participate in ReaDirect. Although Duhat Elementary School demonstrated a substantial overall need for reading intervention, the English oral-reading intervention was limited to learners classified as Developing readers. These learners continued to require scaffolded reading support but possessed sufficient foundational readiness to participate in guided English oral-reading, pronunciation, fluency, and comprehension activities. Thus, the proportion of struggling readers established the school-level need for intervention, while the availability of qualified Developing readers established the suitability of Duhat Elementary School for the specific instructional scope of ReaDirect. 
The endorsement of Duhat Elementary School was further supported by the preliminary needs-assessment interview conducted with the school principal. The interview confirmed that many learners continued to experience reading difficulties, as reflected in the school’s Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment and Philippine Informal Reading Inventory results. The principal also confirmed the school’s need for additional reading support and agreed that a digital artificial intelligence-based system could assist in reading assessment, monitoring, and intervention-related activities. The school administration’s willingness to support the implementation also made it possible to conduct the diagnostic, intervention, monitoring, and reassessment procedures under teacher and researcher supervision. 
The selected locale matched the objectives of the study because ReaDirect was designed for early-grade learners who complete guided English reading and pronunciation activities. Learners interacted with the system by reading prompts, recording their voices, submitting spoken responses, completing diagnostic and instructional module activities, receiving feedback, listening to agent guidance, and progressing through the assigned learning flow. This made an elementary school setting appropriate for evaluating the system’s learner interface, audio-recording process, ASR behavior, agent feedback, voice guidance, scoring process, and reading-support features. The setting was also appropriate because early-grade reading support requires attention to foundational reading skills such as letter knowledge, decoding, oral-reading fluency, and comprehension, which Crawford et al. (2025) identified as important components of early reading development. 
The system was designed for learner, teacher, and administrator roles. During the thesis testing, learners served as the primary users of the English oral-reading support flow, while teachers served mainly as observers and teacher-dashboard users. The administrator role was retained in the system design for user management, transcript review, system monitoring, and future deployment support. However, no administrator served as an actual respondent or evaluator during the thesis testing. 
Only one elementary school was included in the testing phase because the system required individualized learner interaction. Each learner needed to complete English reading and pronunciation activities, record audio responses, submit attempts, and undergo ASR-assisted transcript generation and performance evaluation. Since the testing process involved audio recording, transcript checking, ASR correction review, scoring validation, agent-feedback review, teacher monitoring, and researcher quality assurance, expanding the study to several schools would have required a longer implementation period, additional devices and facilitators, and greater coordination with school personnel. 
Bachiri et al. (2024) evaluated an AI-ASR reading-assessment tool among 100 Grade 1 to Grade 3 learners from two primary schools over a four-week period, during which the learners used a tablet-based ASR assessment that provided feedback on reading accuracy, fluency, and comprehension. In the same manner, the initial testing of ReaDirect was limited to one elementary school so that the researchers could closely evaluate the system’s functionality, usability, feasibility, ASR behavior, scoring process, intervention procedures, and learner interaction within a manageable school-based environment. 
Kunselman (2024) explained that pilot studies are useful for assessing feasibility, identifying procedural and implementation issues, and determining whether the established methods can support a larger future study. Therefore, limiting the initial implementation to Duhat Elementary School was appropriate for the developmental and pilot-stage evaluation of ReaDirect before possible expansion to additional schools and learner populations. 
Respondents and Sampling Technique 
The selection of the research locale and the selection of the learner participants were conducted through separate procedures. Duhat Elementary School was selected through criterion-based purposive site selection based on the endorsement of the DepEd Santa Cruz Sub-Office, the documented school-level need for reading intervention, the availability of qualified Developing readers, and the feasibility of conducting a controlled and closely supervised pilot implementation. After the research locale had been established, a separate two-stage participant-selection procedure was applied, consisting of criterion-based purposive screening followed by simple random sampling among the eligible learners. 
The primary respondents of the study consisted of 20 Grade 1 learners from multiple Grade 1 sections of Duhat Elementary School. The participants were selected from a target population of 29 learners who were currently classified under the Developing-reader profile based on the updated Grade 1 Filipino Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment results. The Filipino CRLA classification served as the initial eligibility criterion because it indicated that the learners had acquired sufficient foundational reading readiness while still requiring structured reading intervention. 
After participant selection, the learners completed the ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic. The diagnostic served as the English pretest and as the basis for instructional module placement rather than as the initial criterion for inclusion in the study. The ReaDirect English CRLA-style reading profile was not presented as an official Grade 1 CRLA English assessment. Instead, it adapted the structure of reading profiles to support English oral-reading assessment, module placement, guided practice, final reassessment, and teacher-monitored progress documentation. 
The learners served as the primary participants because they were the intended users of ReaDirect and directly completed the English oral-reading, pronunciation, diagnostic, instructional module, and final reassessment activities. The Developing-reader profile represents the middle level among the five reading profiles: Low Emerging, High Emerging, Developing, Transitioning, and Reading at Grade Level. Learners classified under the Developing-reader profile have progressed beyond the earliest emerging stages but have not yet reached transitioning or grade-level reading performance. 
Developing readers were therefore considered appropriate participants for scaffolded English oral-reading support because they continued to require intervention while possessing sufficient foundational readiness to participate in guided English literacy activities. Crawford et al. (2025) emphasized that foundational decoding skills, including letter knowledge, letter-sound mapping, decoding, and oral-reading fluency, are necessary for the development of later reading comprehension. 
The selection of Developing readers was also aligned with the purpose of early-grade reading intervention. Graham and Kelly (2018) explained that early-grade reading interventions are intended to address early literacy difficulties through evidence-based instruction, appropriate instructional materials, coaching or monitoring, and assessment tools. They further noted that instruction should correspond to learners’ actual abilities because learning goals that are not appropriately matched to student ability may reduce instructional effectiveness. For this reason, ReaDirect focused on learners who continued to require scaffolded reading support but were sufficiently prepared to participate in English oral-reading, pronunciation, fluency, and comprehension activities. 
The study also involved one to two Grade 1 teachers who served mainly as observers and teacher-dashboard users during system testing. Their role was to observe how the system supported learner assessment, intervention delivery, feedback generation, and progress monitoring. They were also oriented on how the teacher dashboard could be used to view learner records, diagnostic and reassessment scores, English reading progress, module placement, module completion status, and system-generated transcripts. The teachers were not treated as the primary participants of the reading-support intervention but as educational users who could assess the usefulness of ReaDirect for monitoring learner performance and supporting instructional follow-up. 
The study employed a two-stage sampling procedure consisting of criterion-based purposive sampling followed by simple random sampling. During the first stage, criterion-based purposive sampling was used to establish the eligible target population. Learners were considered eligible when they were currently enrolled in Grade 1 at Duhat Elementary School and were classified as Developing readers based on the updated Grade 1 Filipino CRLA results. 
Palinkas et al. (2015) explained that purposive sampling is appropriate when participants are selected because they possess characteristics that are directly relevant to the purpose and phenomenon being investigated. Criterion-based purposive sampling was therefore appropriate because the study required participants who satisfied a clearly defined reading-readiness classification and who were suitable for the specific English oral-reading intervention provided through ReaDirect. 
The updated Filipino CRLA results identified 29 Grade 1 learners who met the Developing-reader eligibility criterion. Since the study intended to include only 20 participants because of the intensive and individualized implementation of ReaDirect, simple random sampling was subsequently applied among the 29 qualified learners. Each eligible learner was assigned an equal opportunity to be selected, thereby reducing researcher subjectivity and selection bias in determining which qualified learners would participate. The random selection was conducted only after the criterion-based eligible group had been established. 
The intervention scope was limited to learners classified as Developing readers under the updated Filipino CRLA. Learners under the earliest reading profiles were excluded because they may require more intensive Filipino foundational reading instruction before participating in guided English oral-reading activities. Transitioning readers and learners Reading at Grade Level were also excluded because they already demonstrated higher reading readiness and might require less scaffolded intervention than the target group. 
Learners who did not receive parental or guardian consent, did not provide assent, transferred from the school, or were unavailable during the scheduled testing period were also excluded. In cases where a randomly selected participant became ineligible or withdrew before the intervention, a replacement participant was selected randomly from the remaining qualified Developing readers. 
The sample size of 20 learners was considered appropriate because the study constituted a developmental and pilot implementation of ReaDirect rather than a large-scale confirmatory experiment. The purpose of the implementation was to evaluate whether the system workflow, learner interaction, ASR-assisted oral-reading assessment, scoring procedures, module progression, teacher monitoring, and final reassessment could function properly in an actual school environment. 
ReaDirect required individualized system use, with each learner needing approximately one hour or more per supervised session to complete the diagnostic and instructional activities. The intervention also involved repeated learner interaction through diagnostic testing, module completion, progress monitoring, and final reassessment. Including 20 participants allowed the researchers to conduct the intervention within the available school schedule, devices, supervision, and implementation period while still obtaining preliminary pretest-posttest performance data. 
Kunselman (2024) stated that pilot studies are not confirmatory trials and generally do not require a formal statistical power calculation. However, the sample size must still be justified according to the feasibility, developmental, and procedural objectives of the pilot study. Therefore, the sample of 20 learners was considered sufficient for closely observing learner-system interaction, identifying technical and usability concerns, evaluating scoring and module-progression consistency, and generating preliminary evidence regarding changes in English oral-reading performance. 
The results were interpreted as preliminary findings specific to the pilot participants and were not intended to be generalized to all Grade 1 learners, all Developing readers, or other schools outside the study setting. A broader implementation involving more learners and additional schools may be conducted in future research after the system procedures, scoring rules, ASR performance, and implementation requirements have been further validated. 
The administrator role was included in the system design for user management, transcript review, system monitoring, and future deployment support. However, no administrator served as an actual respondent or evaluator during the thesis testing. The administrator features were examined mainly from a system-development perspective and may be further utilized after the completion of the study, particularly if ReaDirect is prepared for broader deployment or technical review by authorized information technology personnel, DepEd Santa Cruz Sub-Office representatives, Schools Division Office personnel, or curriculum implementation personnel. 
Ethical Considerations and Data Privacy 
The researchers observed ethical safeguards during the conduct of the study because the system involved Grade 1 learners, learner records, and speech-based English reading activities. Permission from the school was secured before the testing of ReaDirect. The system testing was conducted as part of a school-approved English reading support activity informed by the ARAL Program, and learner participation was limited to the reading, pronunciation, module, and reassessment activities needed for system evaluation. 
Learner identities were handled with confidentiality. Although learner names and Learner Reference Numbers were needed during testing for identification, record matching, and teacher monitoring, these details were anonymized in the thesis document and were not presented in a way that could personally identify individual learners. The collected learner information was used only for system testing, reading-support evaluation, progress monitoring, and academic research. 
Learner audio recordings were captured and processed for ASR transcription and pronunciation-related analysis within the local research and development environment. The recordings were not used to build a long-term learner voice database or to train a biometric voice-identification model. They were used to generate speech-processing outputs such as raw transcript, corrected transcript, displayed transcript, phoneme evidence, audio-quality metadata, and scoring support. Access to audio files and derived records was limited to authorized research and system-evaluation use. 
During testing, learner data and audio-related system records were stored locally in the researchers’ development environment, including the local application database used by ReaDirect. If the system is later prepared for deployment, learner records, audio references, and retention or deletion procedures must follow authorized school or division-level data policies and PostgreSQL access controls. Access to learner records, transcripts, scores, and progress data was limited to authorized users. Teachers were allowed to view learner progress and assessment results through the teacher dashboard, while researcher or administrator access was used for system checking, transcript review, and quality assurance. 
ReaDirect was designed as a learning support tool and not as a replacement for teacher judgment. The ASR model, transcript correction, scoring rules, and feedback generation were used to support assessment and English reading support decisions, but the system outputs were not intended to replace the teacher’s professional evaluation of learner reading performance. All learner data were handled with confidentiality and were used only for academic and system-evaluation purposes. 
Applied Concepts and Techniques 
This section presents the major concepts and techniques applied in the development of ReaDirect. These concepts served as the technical foundation for the system design, speech-recognition workflow, transcript correction process, scoring procedure, feedback generation, agent interaction, and learner support features. Since the study focused on an AI-assisted oral reading coach, the applied concepts were mainly drawn from Artificial Intelligence, Automatic Speech Recognition, deep learning, audio processing, phoneme-based analysis, algorithmic scoring, adaptive recommendation, Miss Ciel’s deterministic coaching feedback, voice-line generation, and software integration. 
Artificial Intelligence in Reading Assessment 
Artificial Intelligence was applied in the study to support the analysis of learner speech audio, transcript generation, pronunciation-related comparison, Miss Ciel’s coaching feedback, spoken agent interaction, and teacher monitoring. In ReaDirect, AI was not used to replace the teacher or independently decide learner outcomes. Instead, the AI component functioned as a support layer that produced transcript, phoneme, audio-quality, pause, similarity, correction, and feedback-related outputs. These outputs were used by the Laravel application for scoring, feedback display, learner record keeping, and progress monitoring. 
AI is useful in educational systems because it can support assessment, learner monitoring, personalization, and feedback generation. Dja’far and Hamidah (2024) examined the use of AI-based speech recognition technology through the Google Read Along application and found that it helped improve learners’ English pronunciation by providing immediate feedback, supporting independent practice, and allowing learners to recognize and correct pronunciation errors. In the present study, AI was used specifically for English oral reading and pronunciation support.
