Since learners were required to speak their answers, the system needed a way to process audio recordings and compare spoken responses with expected answers. AI-assisted speech recognition made this possible by converting learner audio into text and supporting phonetic comparison. This made the system more suitable for reading-based activities because learner performance could be checked not only through typed responses but also through spoken output. It also supported the goal of providing timely and consistent feedback, which can help learners become more aware of their pronunciation mistakes and improve through repeated practice. 
Automatic Speech Recognition and Hybrid Speech Processing 

Figure 4 . Attention-Based Encoder-Decoder Process of Automatic Speech Recognition by Prabhavalkar et al. (2023) 
Automatic Speech Recognition, or ASR, refers to the process of converting spoken audio into text through computational models. This figure illustrates an attention-based encoder-decoder ASR process, where input speech frames are first passed into an encoder to produce higher-level acoustic representations. The attention mechanism then identifies the relevant encoded speech information for each output step, while the decoder uses this context to generate the corresponding text or label sequence. This shows how spoken input can be processed as a sequence and converted into text output. In ReaDirect, ASR was used to transcribe learner speech during diagnostic tasks, alphabet pronunciation activities, word pronunciation activities, rhyme-decision activities, sentence reading tasks, module mastery checks, final assessment, and final reassessment. 

Figure 5. Hybrid Speech Processing Architecture of ReaDirect 
Figure 5 presents the hybrid speech-processing architecture used in ReaDirect. After audio-quality validation, the FastAPI service routes the recording according to the activity type. Whisper large-v3-turbo processes words, sentences, and passages to generate a raw transcript for alignment and fluency analysis. A separate isolated-letter audio classifier processes A–Z responses and returns a predicted label with confidence information. The outputs are then interpreted using expected-answer rules, supporting phoneme evidence, and correction safeguards before Laravel applies deterministic scoring and learner-progression rules. 
The hybrid design was selected because isolated alphabet letters provide very little acoustic and linguistic context, while words, sentences, and passages benefit from a general-purpose ASR model. ReaDirect therefore uses Whisper large-v3-turbo for longer spoken responses and a task-specific audio classifier for isolated letters. This separation allows each response type to be handled by the component most appropriate to its recognition problem without transferring scoring control away from Laravel. 
Isolated-Letter Audio Classification and Phoneme Evidence 
A separate isolated-letter audio classifier was used for A–Z activities. It produced the most likely letter label, confidence score, and alternative candidates from the learner’s audio. The classifier did not serve as the official scorer; its output was checked against the expected letter, audio-quality state, accepted aliases, and supporting sound-level evidence before Laravel determined the activity result. 
Phoneme-level analysis is useful in pronunciation-related systems because it allows comparison based on sound structure instead of relying only on written text output. Cao et al. (2026) explained that phoneme-level pronunciation assessment can measure how closely a spoken phoneme matches its expected canonical pronunciation and can account for sound-level pronunciation issues such as substitution, deletion, and insertion errors. This was important in ReaDirect because isolated letters and short words could be transcribed differently even when the learner pronounced them correctly. For example, the letter C may be recognized as see, Q as cue, U as you, and X as ex or axe. Phoneme evidence helped support safer expected-centric correction and pronunciation comparison by allowing the system to consider whether the learner’s spoken response was sound-wise close to the expected answer. 
Audio Preprocessing and Audio Quality Validation 
Audio preprocessing was applied before ASR inference to prepare learner recordings for analysis. The system performed audio loading, mono conversion, 16 kHz resampling, duration checking, low-volume detection, silent-audio detection, clipping detection, speech and silence segmentation, pause detection, and audio-quality warning generation. 
Audio preprocessing is necessary in speech-recognition systems because audio signals must be prepared into a consistent and processable form before they are used by deep learning models. Mehrish et al. (2023) explained that audio signals are one-dimensional time-series signals that undergo preprocessing and feature extraction, where preprocessing may include noise suppression, silence removal, and channel equalization. In ReaDirect, browser-supported recordings such as WAV, MP3, M4A, WEBM, OGG, or FLAC were converted into a consistent 16 kHz mono waveform before Whisper transcription, isolated-letter classification, and supporting speech analysis. 
Audio quality validation was included to prevent unfair scoring. A learner may pronounce the correct answer, but the recording may be too short, too quiet, clipped, mostly silent, or affected by noise. If these technical issues were treated as pronunciation mistakes, the learner could be unfairly penalized. For this reason, ReaDirect used audio-quality metadata to determine whether an attempt should be scored, marked uncertain, or returned for retry. 
Expected-Centric Transcript Correction 
Expected-centric transcript correction was used to reduce false rejection caused by ASR errors. This technique means that correction was applied only when the expected answer was known and when the raw transcript was sufficiently related to that expected answer. The system did not freely rewrite transcripts without a prompt target. 
This approach was appropriate because each ReaDirect activity had a known expected answer from the content bank. For example, if the expected answer was the letter Z, the system could check whether the raw transcript matched a known ASR error such as they, zy, or zey. If the expected word was tree, the system could check whether the transcript was a known confusion such as three. Li et al. (2024) explained that ASR output may contain erroneous transcripts when speech characteristics differ from the data used to train the ASR system, making ASR error correction useful as a post-ASR processing approach. Their study also emphasized that different ASR systems may produce different error patterns, including substitutions, insertions, and deletions. In ReaDirect, this supported the use of expected-centric transcript correction and phonetic similarity checking because the system needed to determine whether the learner’s spoken response was close to the expected answer even when the direct ASR transcript was imperfect. 
The correction process used exact matching, text normalization, letter alias matching, known ASR confusion matching, phoneme similarity, critical phoneme rules, and reinforcement correction memory. The reinforcement correction memory was a human-curated CSV-based source. Although the folder used the term ‘reinforcement,’ it was not true reinforcement learning. It did not retrain the ASR model or letter classifier, update model weights, or automatically learn from every learner attempt. Instead, it stored known recognition errors and their correct expected labels for controlled correction. 
Letter-Level, Word-Level, and Sentence-Level Analysis 
Letter-level correction was applied because isolated alphabet letters are difficult for ASR systems. Short utterances provide limited acoustic context, which may cause the model to transcribe a spoken letter as a common word or alternate spelling. For example, L may be transcribed as el, ell, or elle; C as see; Q as cue; and U as you. ReaDirect used aliases, spoken-letter normalization, known confusion rules, and phoneme evidence to reduce false rejection while blocking unsafe acceptance between confusable letters. 
Word-level correction was used for word pronunciation tasks, rhyming activities, and activities where the expected answer was a word. The system considered known ASR confusions, homophones, near-homophones, text similarity, Levenshtein distance, and phoneme similarity. Examples included tree and three, red and read, right and write, and hear and here. These corrections were applied only when the raw transcript was sufficiently related to the expected answer and when the correction was safe within the activity context. 
Sentence-level scoring was handled differently from letter-level and word-level scoring. The system did not force the displayed transcript to become the expected sentence. Instead, it used sentence alignment, Word Error Rate, Character Error Rate, substitutions, deletions, insertions, WPM, WCPM, pacing, pause detection, and fluency metadata. This preserved actual learner reading behavior and avoided hiding omissions, substitutions, insertions, or incomplete reading. This approach allowed the system to evaluate sentence reading more accurately because it considered both correctness and oral-reading behavior rather than relying only on exact transcript matching. 
Reading Metrics and Fluency Analysis 
Word Error Rate and Character Error Rate were used to evaluate transcript accuracy by comparing recognized transcripts with reference text. WER helped identify word-level errors such as substitutions, omissions, and insertions, while CER helped capture smaller character-level differences. These metrics were useful for evaluating both raw transcripts and corrected transcripts. 
Fluency analysis was included because oral reading performance is not limited to word correctness. A learner may read the correct words but pause too often, read too slowly, or rush through the sentence. To support sentence-level evaluation, the system calculated Words Per Minute, Words Correct Per Minute, pause count, long-pause count, pacing label, and fluency metadata. In the current implementation, WCPM serves as the primary fluency score, while pacing and pause information support feedback, monitoring, and reassessment comparison. WPM and WCPM are commonly used indicators in oral reading fluency assessment because they represent reading rate and correct reading rate. 
Adaptive Recommendation and Feedback Generation 
Adaptive recommendation was used to suggest the next practice item or action based on learner performance. The system considered recent accuracy, correct streaks, incorrect streaks, weak skills, error types, target phonemes, and content difficulty. If the learner showed consistent correct performance, the system could recommend more difficult items. If the learner struggled, the system could recommend easier or more focused practice items. 
Feedback generation was supported by rule-based feedback templates and approved coaching messages for Miss Ciel, the reading coach agent. The system used approved feedback templates to make Miss Ciel’s coaching response more natural, conversational, and lifelike for Grade 1 learners. The generated feedback was designed to sound supportive and child-friendly while still being based on the learner’s scoring result and activity context. This allowed Miss Ciel to function as an interactive reading coach, while Miss Vivian and Miss Estelle delivered their guidance and evaluator messages through predefined or approved spoken outputs. 
The feedback layer was separated from system scoring. Laravel first computed the score, classification, module decision, or mastery result. After that, the deterministic feedback component selected or prepared the learner-facing response. This separation ensured that feedback sounded natural and supportive without allowing the coaching component to control grades, assessment results, module placement, or learner progression. 
Miss Ciel Feedback Design 
Miss Ciel’s feedback component was used to provide learner-facing coaching responses during guided reading practice. It supported Miss Ciel’s coaching behavior by presenting short, supportive, and learner-friendly messages based on safe system context, such as the learner’s activity result, error type, and current reading task. This was important because Miss Ciel served as the system’s main reading coach during guided practice and feedback. 
Miss Vivian served as the assessment guide, Miss Ciel served as the reading coach, and Miss Estelle served as the evaluator or completion guide. Among these agents, the feedback component was used only for Miss Ciel’s coaching responses. Miss Vivian and Miss Estelle used predefined or approved guidance and evaluator messages, which were delivered through generated voice-line output when spoken messages were needed. Although Miss Ciel’s feedback component supported conversational coaching behavior, it did not independently determine learner outcomes. The Laravel scoring engine remained responsible for scores, classifications, module placement, mastery decisions, and final reassessment results. 
Agent Voice-Line Generation and Voice Support 
Agent voice support was used to deliver approved spoken messages for Miss Vivian, Miss Ciel, and Miss Estelle. The reference voices used for generating the voice lines were created with the ElevenLabs Voice Design feature, which produced voice identities from text prompts. These reference voices were then used in the IndexTTS generation workflow to produce spoken lines with the intended delivery and emotional tone for each agent. This voice-line component was necessary because ReaDirect aimed to make the agents more lifelike and accessible for Grade 1 learners. 
Text-to-speech and voice-line generation can support accessibility and learner engagement by allowing written messages to be delivered as spoken feedback or instructions. In ReaDirect, IndexTTS-generated voice lines supported the learner experience by making agent interaction more natural and child-friendly. However, the voice-line component did not affect scoring, progression, assessment results, or learner records. Its role was to deliver approved agent messages as speech after the system had already determined the appropriate feedback or instruction. 
Adaptive recommendation helped guide learner practice. The feedback component made Miss Ciel’s coaching feedback more natural and engaging, while IndexTTS-generated voice lines provided spoken output for Miss Vivian, Miss Ciel, and Miss Estelle without transferring scoring control away from Laravel. 
Algorithm Analysis 
This section presents the analysis of the major algorithms used in ReaDirect. Algorithm analysis was included to determine whether the procedures used in the system had clear inputs, expected outputs, definite steps, finite execution, effective results, measurable performance, and appropriate complexity. Since ReaDirect combines speech recognition, phonological analysis, transcript correction, ReaDirect English CRLA-style scoring, module placement, feedback generation, and reassessment, the algorithms are discussed according to their role in the complete system workflow. 
The algorithmic workflow begins when the learner records an audio response through the web application. The audio is submitted to the Laravel backend and processed by the Python FastAPI AI service. The service prepares the audio, validates its quality, routes it either to Whisper large-v3-turbo or the isolated-letter classifier, generates speech evidence, and applies expected-centric correction when appropriate. Laravel then computes the system score, stores the learner record, generates or retrieves the appropriate feedback, updates learner progress, and determines the next activity. 
Before discussing the individual algorithms, the AI speech-processing pipeline is presented to show how learner audio is transformed into transcript or classification outputs. Figure 6 illustrates the sequence from audio submission and quality validation to task routing, Whisper transcription or isolated-letter classification, evidence checking, correction, and final output selection. This figure provides the technical overview of the FastAPI workflow before Laravel applies the scoring rules. 
In addition, the algorithm analysis explains how each component contributes to the reliability and consistency of ReaDirect as an educational reading-support system. Each algorithm is examined not only based on its technical function but also based on how it supports the learner’s assessment process, including the conversion of speech into measurable reading evidence, the comparison of the learner’s response with the expected answer, and the generation of appropriate instructional feedback. Through this analysis, the system’s procedures can be evaluated in terms of accuracy, efficiency, and suitability for scaffolded English oral reading support. 
This also helps clarify the separation of responsibilities between the AI service and the main application layer. By presenting the algorithms in sequence, the researchers can show how ReaDirect maintains a traceable flow from learner recording to transcript generation, scoring, feedback, module progression, and reassessment. 

Figure 6. AI Speech Processing Pipeline 
As shown in the figure, the FastAPI speech service ends at transcript, classification, and supporting metadata generation. Scoring, module placement, learner progression, and final educational decisions remain under the Laravel application layer. 
Hybrid Speech Output Generation and Correction Algorithm 
The hybrid speech-output algorithm explains how the system converts a learner recording into reviewable transcript or classification fields and supporting metadata. Its input consists of the learner audio recording, expected answer, accepted answers, prompt type, activity type, and learner-attempt context. Its output consists of the raw, corrected, and displayed transcript fields for ASR-supported tasks; the predicted letter and confidence values for isolated-letter tasks; and phoneme evidence, audio-quality metadata, correction metadata, retry decisions, and uncertainty indicators returned to Laravel. 
The algorithm begins by validating the uploaded audio and converting it into a 16 kHz mono waveform. The prompt type routes words, sentences, and passages to Whisper large-v3-turbo, while isolated alphabet responses are routed to the letter classifier. The ASR path produces a raw transcript; the classifier path produces a predicted label and confidence values. These outputs, together with the expected answer, accepted aliases, known recognition confusions, phoneme evidence, and audio-quality state, are checked by the expected-centric interpretation layer before the system selects the fields used for display, scoring support, and review. 
This algorithm also helps ensure that learner responses are not judged based on the raw ASR transcript alone. By combining audio-quality validation, phoneme evidence, accepted answers, and expected-centric correction rules, the system can identify whether the transcript is reliable enough for scoring support or whether the learner should be asked to record again. Through this process, the AI-ASR service provides Laravel with safer transcript data and supporting metadata for fairer scoring, feedback, and learner-progress recording. 
This allows teachers and researchers to review whether the displayed transcript came from the raw ASR result, the corrected transcript, or a retry decision caused by poor audio quality or uncertain recognition. As a result, the system can support more careful interpretation of learner responses while reducing the risk of unfair scoring caused by ASR errors, unclear speech, or incomplete recordings. 
corrected_transcript 
Transcript produced after expected-centric correction when the raw transcript is safely related to the expected answer. 
Supports scoring for short responses when correction evidence is strong; not used when audio is unreliable, the output is only a fragment, or phoneme evidence conflicts with the expected response. 

displayed_transcript 
Learner-facing transcript selected for interface display after transcript selection rules are applied. 
Presents the most appropriate transcript to the learner while preventing unsafe correction from hiding actual reading errors. 

phoneme_evidence 
Observed phoneme sequence, expected phoneme sequence, phoneme alignment, and similarity indicators. 
Provides sound-level support for pronunciation comparison and safe correction, but does not replace Laravel scoring rules. 

audio_quality_metadata 
Duration, volume, silence, clipping, speech-segment, retry, and uncertainty indicators. 
Prevents technical recording problems from being treated as learner pronunciation errors.

Direct transcript generated by Whisper for word, sentence, and passage responses. 
Preserved as the original ASR output for comparison, transcript review, quality assurance, and transparency. 

correction_metadata 
Information about the correction rule used, blocked correction reason, known confusion match, or safety decision. 
Supports researcher/admin review by showing why a transcript was corrected, preserved, or marked uncertain. 

Field or Metadata 
Description 
Purpose and Safety Use 

The output-field design was necessary because ReaDirect does not treat a model output as a single final answer. The raw transcript or classifier prediction remains available for review, correction is used only when supported by evidence, and the displayed result is selected for learner-facing feedback. This separation helps reduce false rejection while preserving transparency for teachers, researchers, and future administrator review. 
Audio Processing and Quality Validation Algorithms 
The audio processing algorithms prepare learner recordings for ASR and phonological analysis. These include audio loading, mono conversion, 16 kHz resampling, duration checking, low-volume detection, silent-audio detection, clipping detection, speech segmentation, and pause detection. The input is the learner’s audio file, while the output is a standardized waveform and audio-quality metadata. 
These algorithms are necessary because ASR models require audio input to follow expected signal conditions. Learner recordings may come from different browser-supported formats and may contain issues such as low volume, silence, clipping, background noise, or very short duration. Audio preprocessing and validation help prevent technical recording problems from being treated as learner pronunciation errors. 
The audio processing algorithms are definite because each step follows a specific rule, such as converting audio to mono, resampling to 16 kHz, calculating loudness, checking silence ratio, detecting clipping, and identifying speech segments. They are finite because each procedure processes a limited audio file. They are effective because they produce usable audio input for ASR and identify recordings that should be scored, marked uncertain, or returned for retry. Their time complexity is generally O(n), where n represents the number of audio samples or frames. 
Whisper ASR and Isolated-Letter Classification Algorithms 
In this process, the learner’s recording is converted into a 16 kHz mono waveform and routed according to the activity type. Whisper large-v3-turbo processes word, sentence, and passage recordings through an encoder-decoder Transformer and produces the raw transcript. Isolated-letter recordings are processed by the A–Z audio classifier, which produces class probabilities, a predicted letter, and a confidence score. The separate paths address the different context available in longer reading responses and single-letter utterances. 
For ASR-supported tasks, the direct Whisper transcript is preserved as the raw transcript for transparency. Expected-centric correction may create a corrected transcript only when the recording is usable and the available evidence supports the change. For isolated-letter tasks, the original classifier prediction and confidence are retained together with the interpreted label. Despite producing these outputs and supporting metadata, the FastAPI service does not determine scores, module placement, progression, or final learner outcomes; those decisions remain under Laravel. 
The Whisper ASR algorithm generates the raw transcript for words, sentences, and passages. Its input is the processed 16 kHz mono waveform together with the task context, and its output is raw_transcript. The isolated-letter classification algorithm receives the same standardized waveform for A–Z tasks and returns the predicted letter label, confidence score, and ranked alternatives. 
For word, sentence, and passage reading tasks, the Whisper transcript was compared with the expected text using word-level alignment and error metrics. For isolated-letter tasks, the classifier output was compared with the expected label and accepted spoken-letter aliases. Laravel and the correction layer handled expected-centric comparison, scoring, and record review for both paths. 
Phoneme Generation, Alignment, and Similarity Algorithms 
The phoneme-related algorithms support pronunciation comparison. These include expected phoneme generation, observed phoneme recognition, phoneme normalization, phoneme alignment, and phoneme similarity computation. The input consists of the expected answer and learner audio, while the output consists of expected phonemes, observed phonemes, alignment results, and a phoneme similarity score. 
Expected phonemes are generated through a lookup process. The system first checks the custom A–Z phoneme dictionary for alphabet letters. If the expected answer is not available there, it checks the CMU Pronouncing Dictionary and then uses grapheme-to-phoneme fallback when needed. Observed sound evidence is derived from the learner audio through the supporting pronunciation-analysis component. Both expected and observed representations are normalized before comparison. 
The supporting pronunciation-analysis path converts the learner recording into sound-level evidence that can be aligned with the expected phoneme sequence. The system compares the normalized expected and observed representations to identify possible substitutions, insertions, and deletions. This evidence supports pronunciation feedback and safe correction, but it does not replace the Whisper transcript, the isolated-letter classifier result, or Laravel’s deterministic scoring rules. 
Equation 1. Phoneme Similarity 
Equation 1 measures how close the observed phoneme sequence is to the expected phoneme sequence based on edit distance. 
These algorithms are definite because the phoneme lookup, normalization, alignment, and comparison steps are fixed. They are finite because the compared phoneme sequences are limited in length. They are effective because they provide pronunciation-related evidence for correction and feedback. Phoneme generation is generally O(k), where k is the number of tokens in the expected text, while phoneme edit distance is O(mn), where m and n are the lengths of the compared phoneme sequences. This means that the algorithms remain practical for short reading tasks while still producing useful pronunciation evidence for system evaluation. 
Pronunciation Quality Algorithms 
Pronunciation quality algorithms determine whether the learner’s spoken response is close enough to the expected pronunciation. These algorithms use expected phonemes, observed phoneme evidence, alignment results, and distance-based comparison. They help identify whether the learner produced the correct sound, substituted a sound, omitted a sound, inserted an extra sound, or produced a phonetically close response. 
Goodness of Pronunciation was used as a pronunciation-quality measure by estimating how closely the learner’s spoken phonemes matched the expected phoneme sequence. Cao et al. (2026) explained that Goodness of Pronunciation is commonly used in phoneme-level pronunciation assessment because it measures how closely a spoken phoneme matches its expected canonical pronunciation. This measure supported ReaDirect in identifying pronunciation closeness at the phoneme level by comparing expected phoneme patterns with observed phoneme evidence from learner audio. In this way, GOP provided a quantitative indication of pronunciation accuracy that helped the system assess whether the learner’s spoken response was phonetically close to the expected answer. In ReaDirect, Goodness of Pronunciation served as supporting pronunciation-quality evidence for correction and feedback, but it did not replace the official Laravel scoring rules. 
These algorithms are important for letter pronunciation, rhyming responses, word reading, and sentence reading activities. They are definite because they use defined phoneme references, alignment rules, and similarity measures. They are finite because learner responses and expected answers are limited in length. Their time complexity usually depends on sequence alignment, commonly O(mn). 
Text Normalization and Transcript Correction Algorithms 
Text normalization and transcript correction algorithms reduce false rejection caused by ASR output errors. Text normalization converts expected and actual text into a standard format by applying lowercase conversion, punctuation removal, Unicode normalization, and whitespace normalization. This prevents formatting differences from being treated as actual response errors. 
The transcript correction process uses expected-centric rules. Correction is applied only when the expected answer is known and when the raw transcript is sufficiently related to that expected answer. The correction techniques include exact matching, reinforcement correction memory, letter alias matching, known ASR confusion matching, phoneme similarity, and critical phoneme detection. 
This correction approach helps ReaDirect interpret short learner responses more carefully, especially when the ASR model produces outputs that are close to the expected answer but not written exactly the same way. By combining text-based rules with phoneme-level evidence, the system can reduce unnecessary incorrect marks while still keeping the correction process controlled and evidence-based. 
The correction algorithm also applied safety checks before replacing the raw transcript with the expected answer. Correction was blocked when the recording was marked unreliable, when the raw ASR output appeared to be an incomplete fragment, or when observed phoneme evidence contradicted a critical sound required by the expected response. For sentence and passage prompts, the system did not overwrite the whole transcript with the expected text; instead, it preserved the ASR-based transcript and used word-level alignment metadata to identify correct words, substitutions, deletions, insertions, split errors, and merge errors. 
The decision process used in expected-centric transcript correction is summarized in Figure 7. Through this process, ReaDirect makes transcript interpretation more transparent because the system does not immediately treat the raw ASR output as the final learner response. Instead, it checks whether the transcript can be safely normalized, corrected, or preserved based on available evidence. This helps reduce unfair scoring caused by common ASR mistakes while still protecting the learner’s actual spoken response from being replaced without sufficient support. As a result, the correction layer strengthens the reliability of the scoring, feedback, and teacher-review process. 
In addition, the correction process supports accountability because the system keeps separate records for the raw transcript, corrected transcript, and displayed transcript. This allows teachers and administrators to review how the final transcript was produced and determine whether the correction was appropriate. By preserving these transcript fields and alignment details, ReaDirect provides a traceable basis for understanding learner performance instead of relying on a single unverified ASR output. 

Figure 7 . Expected-Centric Transcript Correction Flow 
As shown in this figure, the correction process was designed to reduce false rejection without freely replacing learner responses. This allowed the system to improve transcript usability while preserving the raw ASR output for review. In this way, the algorithm supports a safer balance between correcting likely ASR mistakes and keeping the learner’s actual response traceable for teacher review. 
ASR Correction Safety Rules 
The ASR correction safety rules define when the system may accept an expected-centric correction and when it must preserve the raw transcript. A correction is considered only when the expected answer is known, the activity type supports correction, and the raw ASR output has enough textual or phoneme-level relationship to the expected response. This prevents the system from freely rewriting learner responses without a prompt target. 
Correction is blocked when the audio-quality metadata indicates an unreliable recording, when the transcript appears to be only an incomplete fragment, when the observed phoneme evidence contradicts a critical sound in the expected answer, or when the possible correction could cause confusion between two different valid answers. For isolated letters and short words, the system may use letter aliases, known ASR confusions, phoneme similarity, and correction memory only when these conditions are safe within the expected-label context. 
For sentence and passage prompts, the system preserves the ASR-based transcript instead of replacing the whole response with the expected text. In these longer reading tasks, the system relies on word-level alignment, substitutions, deletions, insertions, fluency indicators, and reading-completion data. This allows the system to detect actual reading behavior while still using ASR and phoneme evidence as supporting tools. 
The reinforcement correction memory matches the expected label and raw transcript against curated CSV records of known ASR errors. For example, if the expected answer is Z and the raw transcript matches a known error such as they, the system can apply correction only within that expected-label context. This prevents correction rules from blindly changing unrelated transcripts. 
Letter-level correction addresses cases where isolated alphabet letters are transcribed as common words or alternate spellings. Word-level correction addresses homophones and near-homophones such as tree and three, red and read, right and write, and hear and here. These corrections are used only when the raw transcript is sufficiently related to the expected answer and when the correction is safe within the activity context. 
These algorithms are definite because each correction rule has a defined condition. They are finite because the alias lists, confusion dictionaries, correction CSVs, and transcript strings are limited. They are effective because they reduce false rejection while preserving raw transcripts for administrator review. Their complexity ranges from O(1) for dictionary lookup to O(mn) when edit distance or sequence alignment is used. 
Error Metrics and Sentence Alignment Algorithms 
Error metric algorithms evaluate transcript accuracy and sentence reading performance by comparing the expected text with the learner’s actual transcript. These include Word Error Rate, Character Error Rate, corrected WER, corrected CER, and sentence alignment. The input is the expected text and the actual transcript, while the output includes error rates, substitutions, deletions, insertions, and alignment metadata. These results help the system identify whether the learner’s response contains missing words, added words, replaced words, or minor character-level differences. The corrected WER and corrected CER are also used to show how the transcript changes after evidence-based correction, making it easier to compare raw ASR performance with the refined transcript used for final interpretation. Through these algorithms, ReaDirect can provide a more transparent basis for scoring, feedback generation, and teacher review of learner reading performance. 
Equation 2. Word Error Rate 
Equation 2 measures word-level transcript error using substitutions, deletions, insertions, and the number of expected words. This allows the system to identify how much the learner’s spoken response differs from the expected text based on missing, added, or incorrectly recognized words. 
Equation 3. Character Error Rate 
Equation 3 measures transcript error at the character level. 
WER and CER were used as ASR evaluation metrics because they compare a recognized transcript with a reference transcript at different text levels. WER measures transcript accuracy by calculating word-level insertions, deletions, and substitutions between an ASR-generated hypothesis and a reference transcript, while CER applies a similar comparison at the character level. In ReaDirect, these metrics were used to evaluate both raw transcripts and corrected transcripts. This allowed the researchers to determine whether transcript correction improved scoring reliability while still preserving the visibility of raw ASR behavior during system testing and quality assurance. Using both metrics was important because word-level errors show whether the learner’s response matched the expected answer, while character-level errors reveal smaller differences common in short utterances. It also helped the researchers compare raw and corrected transcripts without losing evidence of the system’s original ASR behavior (Kuhn et al., 2024). 
Sentence alignment is used for sentence reading tasks. It identifies matched words, substitutions, deletions, and insertions between the expected sentence and the learner’s transcript. This is necessary because sentence reading cannot be evaluated through exact matching alone. A learner may read most words correctly but omit one word, insert an extra word, substitute a similar word, or fail to reach some words within the time limit. 
These algorithms are definite because the formulas and alignment operations are fixed. They are finite because they process finite word or character sequences. They are effective because they provide measurable transcript and sentence-reading indicators. Their time complexity is generally O(mn) because dynamic programming is used for edit distance and alignment. 
Fluency and Pacing Algorithms 
The fluency algorithms analyze sentence-level and passage-level oral reading behavior. These include Words Per Minute, Words Correct Per Minute, pause detection, long-pause detection, pacing evaluation, and fluency metadata generation. The input consists of transcript data, correct word count, audio duration, speech segments, and pause metadata. The output includes WPM, WCPM, pause count, long-pause count, pacing label, WCPM-based fluency score, and fluency label. These fluency indicators help the system determine whether the learner reads smoothly, too slowly, or with frequent interruptions during oral reading activities. They also allow teachers to review whether the learner’s difficulty is related to speed, accuracy, hesitation, or overall reading continuity. 
Equation 4. Words Per Minute 
Equation 4 measures the learner's oral reading speed. 
Equation 5. Words Correct Per Minute 
Equation 5 measures the learner's correct oral reading rate. 
WPM and WCPM were used as oral reading fluency indicators because they represent reading rate and correct reading rate. In traditional oral reading fluency assessment, WCPM is calculated by subtracting incorrectly read words from the total number of words read within a set time. In ReaDirect, WCPM serves as the primary fluency score, while pacing, pause behavior, and completion are retained as separate feedback and monitoring signals. This supports a more transparent evaluation of whether the learner reads correctly, steadily, and completely (Nese, 2022). 
The fluency algorithms are definite because the formulas, pacing labels, and WCPM-based scoring behavior are configured. They are finite because they use a limited number of computed values and speech segments. They are effective because they provide reading-performance metadata beyond transcript accuracy. Their complexity ranges from O(1) for WPM and WCPM calculation to O(s log s) for pause analysis when speech segments require sorting. 
After the speech-processing, transcript correction, error metric, and fluency procedures are completed, Laravel applies the educational scoring and progression rules of the system. Figure 8 presents the complete diagnostic, module placement, module mastery, and reassessment flow used in ReaDirect. This figure shows how the learner moves from the initial ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic assessment to English reading profile scoring, reading passage assessment, module assignment, mastery checking, and final reassessment. This analysis also helps identify possible limitations in the system, such as unclear audio input, inaccurate transcription, or mismatched learner responses. By defining these steps clearly, the system ensures that learner placement, progress tracking, and reassessment are based on consistent rules rather than manual judgment alone. It also shows how each stage of the English reading support flow is connected, allowing the system to determine whether the learner should proceed, repeat an activity, or receive additional support. Through this flow, ReaDirect can provide a structured process for monitoring learner performance from the first diagnostic activity up to the final reassessment. 
This process also strengthens the reliability of the system because fluency results are not interpreted as isolated numerical scores. Instead, the system connects WPM, WCPM, pause behavior, miscue counts, and completion status to form a clearer picture of the learner’s oral reading performance. The use of these indicators allows teachers to identify whether a learner needs support in speed, accuracy, pronunciation, or reading continuity. As a result, the algorithm supports both automated progress monitoring and teacher-guided intervention planning within ReaDirect. Furthermore, the recorded fluency data can help compare the learner’s performance before and after completing the assigned reading modules. 
This makes the reassessment process more meaningful because improvement can be observed through measurable changes in reading speed, correctness, and overall oral reading behavior. These measurements are useful because they allow the system to show not only whether the learner finished the activity, but also how the learner performed while reading. For example, a learner may read quickly but still commit many word errors, or may read accurately but with long pauses and slow pacing. Because of this, ReaDirect interprets fluency as a combination of speed, correctness, and reading behavior rather than a single score. This helps the teacher understand the specific reading difficulty of the learner and provide the appropriate follow-up activity or intervention. 

Figure 8 . Diagnostic, Module Placement, and Reassessment Flow 
As shown in this figure, the learner’s instructional path is determined by the combined results of the ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic tasks and reading comprehension classification. The succeeding subsections explain the scoring algorithms and decision rules used in each part of this flow. Although the system keeps several profile branches for record completeness, the thesis intervention scope focuses on learners classified as Developing readers. 
ReaDirect English CRLA-style Diagnostic Scoring Algorithm 
The ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic scoring algorithm determines the learner’s English phonological reading readiness within the system. The diagnostic process begins with Task 1A, where the learner pronounces ten letters individually. Each correctly pronounced letter is assigned one point, giving a maximum score of ten points. If the learner scores 0-6 in Task 1A, the learner proceeds to Task 2A only, where Ms. Vivian says two words and the learner answers Yes or No depending on whether the pair rhymes. After this lower-readiness branch, Task 2B and the reading passage are not administered and are recorded as zero. 
If the learner scores 7-10 in Task 1A, Task 2A is automatically given a full score of ten points because the learner is considered to have adequate phonological awareness for the next English reading task. The learner then proceeds directly to Task 2B, where highlighted target words embedded in sentences are read and evaluated using the sentence-reading scorer. This task flow avoids unnecessary assessment while still identifying whether the learner has enough readiness to proceed to passage reading and reading-comprehension activities. 
Equation 6. ReaDirect English CRLA-style Total Score 
Equation 6 computes the learner's total English phonological reading readiness score by combining the results of the three diagnostic components. The total score provides a basis for determining the learner’s initial support level and helps the system decide whether the learner may proceed to passage reading and module placement. Through this scoring process, ReaDirect can apply a consistent and structured method for organizing learner support while still keeping teacher judgment central.

After this branch, Task 2B and the reading passage are automatically recorded as zero. When the learner scores 7-10 in Task 1A, Task 2A is automatically credited as 10 points and the learner proceeds directly to Task 2B. This table is important because it clearly defines how ReaDirect applies its English-adapted diagnostic rules and prevents learners from being advanced to word-reading or passage-reading activities when their phonological readiness is still limited.

This table is important because it clearly defines the branching logic of ReaDirect and ensures that each learner is placed in a diagnostic path appropriate to their current English phonological readiness. It also prevents learners from being required to answer tasks that may be beyond their current reading readiness

These labels are used by the system for support routing and record organization. They are not presented as an official Grade 1 English CRLA implementation. Learners with lower readiness results may require teacher review and stronger foundational reading support before participating in English-based oral reading tasks, while learners with adequate readiness may proceed to passage reading and reading-comprehension activities. 
Reading Accuracy and Comprehension Scoring Algorithms 
The reading comprehension scoring algorithm evaluates the learner’s ability to read a short passage and answer comprehension questions. The learner reads a 50-word passage within a maximum time limit of 60 seconds, with an expected average reading time of 45 seconds. If the learner does not finish the passage within the time limit, the unread words are counted as incorrect. This ensures that the reading score reflects both accuracy and completion. 
Table 5 . Reading Passage Timing Rules 
Parameter 
Value 
Description 

Task 1A score 0-6 or total score 0-16 
Do not administer Task 2B for the 0-6 Task 1A branch; do not administer the reading passage 
Automatically recorded as 0 

Total score 17-30 
Administer the reading passage 
Compute using reading accuracy and comprehension 

Maximum Allowed Time 
60 seconds 
Reading stops at this limit 

Unread Words 
Counted as incorrect 
Words not reached within one minute 

Table 5 presents the reading passage eligibility and timing rules applied in ReaDirect after the English diagnostic tasks. It shows that learners with a Task 1A score of 0-6, or a total score of 0-16, do not proceed to the reading passage, and their passage score is automatically recorded as zero. This prevents learners with limited phonological readiness from being given a task that may be beyond their current reading ability. For learners with a total score of 17-30, the reading passage is administered, and the final passage result is computed using reading accuracy and comprehension. The table also includes the 60-second maximum reading time and the rule that unread words are counted as incorrect. This is important because it ensures that ReaDirect evaluates both accuracy and completion while keeping the assessment fair, structured, and appropriate to the learner’s readiness level. Through these rules, the system can avoid over-assessing learners while still allowing prepared learners to demonstrate passage-level reading performance.

Table 6 presents the miscue and error counting rules used during the reading passage assessment in ReaDirect. It identifies the types of reading errors that are counted as incorrect, including mispronunciation, substitution, omission, and unread words. Each error type is counted as one incorrect word, which allows the system to compute reading accuracy in a consistent and measurable way. This table is important because it clarifies how ReaDirect evaluates the learner’s oral reading performance beyond simply checking whether the passage was completed. By counting miscues and unread words, the system can more accurately reflect the learner’s decoding accuracy, word recognition ability, and reading completion within the passage activity. 
Equation 7. Reading Accuracy 
Equation 7 computes reading accuracy by subtracting two percent for every incorrect word. 
The final reading score combines comprehension and reading accuracy. This weighting was used because reading assessment should measure whether the learner understood the passage, not only whether words were decoded correctly. Tobia and Bonifacci (2015) explained that reading comprehension is the ultimate goal of reading and found that comprehension-related language skills were stronger predictors of reading comprehension than reading accuracy, although accuracy still played a significant role. Therefore, ReaDirect gave higher weight to comprehension while still including reading accuracy to reflect both passage understanding and correct word reading. 
Equation 8. Final Reading Score 
Equation 8 combines comprehension and reading accuracy into the final reading score.

It shows how the final score is grouped into five profile labels: Low Emerging Reader, High Emerging Reader, Developing Reader, Transitioning Reader, and Reading at Grade Level. These classifications help the system describe the learner’s English reading comprehension performance after combining comprehension and reading accuracy results. For the scope of this study, the Developing Reader classification served as the included target profile because it represents learners who still need scaffolded support while having enough foundational readiness for guided English oral reading activities. 
Comprehension Answer Evaluation Algorithm 
The Comprehension Answer Evaluation Algorithm evaluates learner answers to comprehension questions. The input is the learner’s selected multiple-choice answer and the expected correct choice, while the output is a correctness decision based on the stored answer key. The learner selects from the available choices, and the selected letter is compared with the correct choice for the selected passage. This approach supported consistent checking of comprehension answers without relying on free-text formatting differences. 
Module Placement Algorithm 
The module placement algorithm uses the learner’s ReaDirect English support level and reading comprehension classification to assign the appropriate instructional path. The system retains routing rules for different profile outcomes for record completeness and future teacher review; however, the thesis intervention scope focuses on learners classified as Developing readers. Lower-readiness outcomes such as Low Emerging and High Emerging are treated as indications that learners may first require stronger Filipino foundational reading support before participating in English-based ReaDirect activities. Table 8 presents the profile-handling rules used to clarify how ReaDirect interprets each outcome in relation to the study scope. This helps ensure that module placement remains transparent while also respecting the defined limits of the study’s English-based intervention. It also allows teachers to review why a learner was assigned to a specific instructional path before making further intervention decisions. This rule is necessary because ReaDirect is designed to support English reading intervention only for learners who are ready for English-based activities, while lower-readiness learners may require foundational support outside the system’s main intervention scope. This also helps prevent the system from forcing English module activities on learners whose current readiness may not yet match the required task level. In this way, ReaDirect supports more appropriate intervention planning by separating English reading support from foundational reading needs that may require teacher-led remediation.

Table 8 presents the profile-handling rules used by ReaDirect after the learner’s English diagnostic support level and reading comprehension classification are determined. It clarifies that the system may still store lower-readiness and higher-readiness outcomes for monitoring, but the thesis intervention target remains the Developing Reader profile. This distinction is necessary because Low Emerging and High Emerging learners may need stronger Filipino foundational reading support first, while Transitioning and Reading at Grade Level learners may require less scaffolded intervention than the target group. Through these rules, ReaDirect keeps the diagnostic results useful for teacher monitoring while limiting the main intervention flow to learners who match the study’s intended English reading support scope. 
Module Mastery and Progression Algorithm 
The module mastery scoring algorithm evaluates learner performance within each instructional module. The system collects module performance metrics such as pronunciation accuracy, word reading accuracy, and sentence reading fluency. These are aggregated into an overall module performance score. This score helps determine whether the learner has mastered the module, needs additional practice, or should proceed to the next reading activity. 
Equation 9. Module Performance Score 
Equation 9 computes the learner's module activity or mastery-check percentage score. 
After the percentage score is computed, the system evaluates the result using the rule-based mastery thresholds configured for the module flow. These thresholds guide the system in deciding whether the learner should move to the next module, repeat the current module, return to a previous module, or receive additional practice drills. 
This process allows ReaDirect to make module mastery decisions in a consistent and measurable way instead of relying only on manual judgment. By using earned points, possible points, and predefined threshold rules, the system can identify whether the learner has demonstrated sufficient mastery or still needs further reading support before advancing. 
It also helps teachers determine which module skills may need review when a learner does not meet the required mastery level. Through this algorithm, ReaDirect supports a more organized learning progression from practice activities to mastery checking, module adjustment, and reassessment. 
This also makes the module progression more transparent because each decision is based on recorded scores and clearly defined mastery conditions. As a result, teachers can better understand the reason behind the learner’s assigned module path and provide additional support when needed.

It shows how the learner’s module performance score determines whether the learner will proceed to the next module, repeat the current module, return to a previous module, or receive additional practice. Within the thesis scope, these rules are interpreted mainly through the Developing-reader support path, while lower-readiness branches are treated as system safeguards and teacher-review indicators rather than the central intervention target. 
The progression rules also help ensure that learners do not advance to more difficult reading activities without showing enough mastery of the current skill. Since the modules move from phoneme and letter-sound practice to word reading, sentence reading, and reassessment, each decision point supports a gradual and scaffolded intervention flow. This allows ReaDirect to provide additional practice when needed while still giving teachers a clear basis for reviewing learner progress and deciding whether further support is necessary. 
Feedback, Agent Response, and Voice Output Algorithms 

Figure 9. ReaDirect Intelligent Agent Architecture 
Figure 9 presents the ReaDirect Intelligent Agent Architecture as a progressive set of subfigures below. The architecture is divided into learner-state observation, perception, session memory, rule-based policy, and multimodal execution to maintain readability in portrait format. 

Figure 9.1. Learner State Observation and Attempt Context 
This figure presents the learner state observation and attempt context stage of the ReaDirect intelligent agent architecture. In this stage, the system observes the learner’s current attempt by collecting the submitted answer, displayed transcript, expected answer, ASR evidence, scoring result, reading behavior, and listening state. These data serve as the context used by Miss Ciel to understand the learner’s current reading performance before selecting a coaching response. 

Figure 9.2. Perception and Learner State Classification 
As shown in this figure, the perception and learner state classification stage. In this stage, Miss Ciel processes the learner’s response by normalizing the input, checking confidence indicators, identifying possible error types, and determining the learner’s current state. This allows the system to classify whether the learner needs praise, correction, retry guidance, slow practice, or additional support. 

Figure 9.3. Session Memory and Repeated Error Tracking 
This figure presents the session memory and repeated error tracking stage. This part allows the system to monitor learner patterns across attempts, especially when the learner repeatedly commits the same type of mistake. By tracking repeated errors, Miss Ciel can provide more appropriate feedback instead of giving the same response for every attempt. 

Figure 9.4. Rule-Based Tutoring Policy and Selected Action 
This figure shows the rule-based tutoring policy and selected action stage. In this stage, the system uses predefined feedback rules to choose the most suitable coaching action based on the learner’s state, error type, and attempt context. The selected action may include praise, hint, soft retry, slow practice, focus teaching, or completion feedback. 

Figure 9.5. Structured Decision, Multimodal Execution, and Agent Loop 
As shown in this figure and its subfigures, the ReaDirect intelligent agent architecture presents the Miss Ciel feedback and coaching flow as a progressive process. The system first computes the official learner result through Laravel, ensuring that scoring, result storage, and progression remain controlled by the main application. After the official result is computed, Miss Ciel’s deterministic feedback algorithm uses safe system context to select an appropriate learner-facing coaching response. This allows Miss Ciel to provide encouraging and guided feedback during reading practice without changing the learner’s official score or classification. 
The voice-line support algorithm connects approved agent messages to generated spoken audio. Reference voices were first created through ElevenLabs Voice Design from text prompts, and IndexTTS was then used to generate the final voice lines with the intended agent delivery and emotion. At runtime, ReaDirect serves only the approved voice-line output for the corresponding fixed agent role. This makes Miss Vivian, Miss Ciel, and Miss Estelle more interactive while keeping scoring and progression under Laravel control. 
These algorithms are definite because the feedback context, agent roles, response length, fallback conditions, and voice-line assignments are defined. They are finite because they process limited prompts, short messages, and bounded learner-state inputs. They are effective because they improve learner engagement and support a guided reading experience. The feedback generation time depends on message selection and voice-output retrieval, while voice-line generation depends on message length and the IndexTTS generation process used during asset preparation. 
In addition, the feedback and voice-line algorithms separate instructional support from official scoring decisions. Miss Ciel’s feedback is used to encourage the learner and guide practice, while Laravel remains responsible for computing scores, storing results, and controlling progression. This separation helps ensure that the system remains consistent, child-friendly, and reliable during learner interaction. It also prevents the feedback component from producing responses that may affect the learner’s official score or classification. Since the coaching messages are based only on safe system context, the feedback remains focused on encouragement, correction, and guided practice rather than independent decision-making. This makes the agent interaction useful for learner motivation while keeping the assessment process transparent and teacher-supervised. 
Data Collection Methods 
This section presents the data sources and data collection methods used in the development, testing, and evaluation of ReaDirect. The study required both educational content data and speech-processing data because the system was designed to support pronunciation assessment, English oral reading practice, transcript generation, transcript correction, scoring, feedback generation, module placement, and teacher monitoring. 
The data used in the study came from ARAL-informed English reading materials, learner-generated audio responses, learner identification and progress records, system-generated records, researcher-prepared CSV files, a custom English letter audio dataset, and public speech datasets. These data sources were necessary because ReaDirect required expected answers, learner audio input, ASR transcripts, phoneme evidence, scoring results, correction records, audio-quality metadata, fluency data, module mastery records, feedback metadata, and reassessment results. 
Table 10 summarizes the major data sources used in the study, including the educational content, learner-generated outputs, system-generated records, correction references, ASR model-generation datasets, and feedback-related metadata. The table also shows how each data source supported the development, testing, scoring, transcript analysis, module placement, and evaluation of ReaDirect. These data sources also help maintain transparency because the researchers can trace how learner inputs, ASR outputs, scoring results, and module decisions are produced within the ReaDirect workflow. 
Overall, the data sources listed in Table 10 show that ReaDirect depends on both educational content and system-generated evidence. The reading prompts guide the learner activities, while the audio responses, ASR transcripts, phoneme evidence, and assessment records support scoring, feedback, and progress monitoring. This makes the data collection process important because each data source contributes to a specific part of the learner’s diagnostic, module, and reassessment workflow. 
It also ensures that the researchers can review whether the system used the correct input data and generated the appropriate records for each learner activity. Through this process, the data collection methods support both the technical development of ReaDirect and the evaluation of its usefulness as a reading intervention support tool. 
Furthermore, the data collection methods helped ensure that ReaDirect could be tested using information that reflects the actual workflow of the system. Since the platform processes reading prompts, learner recordings, transcript outputs, correction metadata, and progress records, the collected data served as the basis for checking whether each component worked properly. This allowed the researchers to evaluate not only the ASR process, but also the scoring, feedback, module placement, and teacher-monitoring functions of the system. 
In addition, the data collection process supports the reliability of the study because it connects the learner’s recorded response to the system outputs used for evaluation. Each record, such as the transcript, score, correction result, and module progress, provides evidence that can be reviewed by the researchers and teachers. This helps ensure that the system’s results are not based on unsupported assumptions, but on collected learner inputs and traceable system-generated data.
