Task 1A, Task 2A, Task 2B, total score, English support level, passage accuracy, miscues, comprehension answers, final score, and reader classification. 
Provided the basis for English phonological readiness classification, reading comprehension classification, module placement, learner progress monitoring, and reassessment comparison. 

The speech-processing entries in Table 10 were updated to reflect the hybrid runtime direction. Whisper large-v3-turbo was used for words, sentences, and passages, while the custom ReaDirect English letter dataset supported the separate A–Z classifier used for isolated-letter responses. 
Since the study involved Grade 1 learners and voice-based reading activities, data collection required proper coordination and permission from the school. The testing was conducted as part of a school-approved English reading support activity informed by the ARAL Program. Learner audio was captured and processed only for ASR processing, pronunciation analysis, scoring validation, learner feedback, teacher monitoring, and system evaluation within the local research environment. The recordings were not used to build a long-term learner voice database or biometric voice-identification model. 
Learner Audio Recordings 
Learner audio recordings served as the primary input for the speech-recognition and pronunciation-analysis component of ReaDirect. These recordings were produced when learners completed diagnostic tasks, alphabet pronunciation activities, rhyming word tasks, word reading tasks, sentence reading tasks, module activities, module mastery checks, final assessment, and final reassessment. 
Each learner audio recording contained the learner’s spoken response to a known prompt. The audio was captured through the browser recording interface and temporarily submitted to the Laravel backend for processing. During testing, the audio was forwarded to the Python FastAPI AI service for ASR analysis and was not retained as a permanent learner audio record. The AI service prepared the audio through loading, mono conversion, 16 kHz resampling, audio-quality validation, ASR inference, phoneme evidence extraction, transcript correction, and metadata generation. 
These recordings were important because ReaDirect focused on oral reading and pronunciation support. Since the learner’s response was spoken rather than typed, the system needed audio recordings to generate transcripts, compare learner responses with expected answers, evaluate pronunciation-related evidence, detect audio-quality problems, compute fluency indicators, and provide feedback. 
ARAL-Aligned Reading Content and Prompt Data 
The system used ARAL-informed English literacy materials and Grade 1 English reading prompts as the instructional content source. These materials included letters, sounds, rhyming decision prompts, words, short sentences, reading passages, and comprehension questions. The content served as the basis for diagnostic assessment, module activities, mastery checks, final assessment, and final reassessment. 
Every learner task had a corresponding expected answer or expected performance output. Letter pronunciation tasks had expected alphabet-letter responses. Rhyme-decision tasks had expected Yes or No responses. Word tasks had expected word outputs. Sentence tasks had expected sentence text. Comprehension questions had expected multiple-choice answers. This structure allowed the system to compare learner responses with known targets and apply scoring rules consistently. 
The use of structured reading content was necessary because ReaDirect used expected-centric speech analysis. The system could only apply safe transcript correction and scoring when the expected answer or expected response was known. This prevented the system from freely rewriting learner transcripts without a defined instructional target. It also ensured that every correction decision remained connected to the learning task, making the transcript analysis more controlled, transparent, and aligned with the reading activity. 
ReaDirect Content Bank CSV 
The ReaDirect content bank was stored in CSV format. It contained prompt items, expected answers, accepted answers, prompt types, activity categories, module items, diagnostic items, and assessment items. This CSV served as the main reference source for Laravel scoring and AI expected-centric correction. 
The expected answer field was used to compare the learner’s spoken response with the target answer. The accepted answer field allowed the system to recognize valid response variations when appropriate. The prompt type helped the system determine whether the activity involved a letter, word, rhyme, sentence, passage, or comprehension item. This was important because each activity type required a different scoring or correction approach. 
The CSV-based content bank made the system easier to revise during development and testing. Instead of hardcoding all learning items directly into the system source code, the researchers could update instructional content, expected answers, and accepted answers through structured CSV files. 
ASR Transcript Data 
The system generated transcript data after processing learner audio in ASR-supported tasks. These data included raw_transcript, corrected_transcript, and displayed_transcript. The raw transcript represented the direct Whisper output, the corrected transcript represented the result after safe expected-centric correction, and the displayed transcript represented the learner-facing version shown in the interface. Isolated-letter tasks additionally stored the classifier’s predicted label and confidence metadata. 
These transcript fields were collected because each served a different purpose. The raw transcript allowed the researchers and administrators to inspect the actual output of the ASR model. The corrected transcript supported scoring when the correction rules had enough evidence. The displayed transcript supported learner-facing feedback and interface presentation. 
Preserving separate transcript fields was necessary because ASR systems can produce errors, especially in short utterances, isolated alphabet letters, low-volume recordings, clipped audio, background noise, and phonetically similar words. By storing the raw, corrected, and displayed transcripts separately, the researchers could evaluate both the original ASR behavior and the effect of correction rules. 
Phoneme Evidence Data 
Phoneme evidence was collected and generated to support pronunciation-related comparison. The expected phoneme sequence came from the displayed prompt or expected answer, while the observed phoneme evidence came from learner audio processed by the phoneme-support model. These phoneme sequences were used to compare the learner’s spoken output with the expected pronunciation. 
This data was especially important for isolated letters, rhyming responses, and short words. For example, the spoken letter C may appear as see, Q as cue, U as you, and X as ex in ASR output. In these cases, the text transcript may differ from the expected answer even when the learner’s speech is phonetically close. Phoneme evidence helped the system make safer correction and acceptance decisions. 
Phoneme-based comparison is useful in pronunciation assessment because it allows sound-level analysis beyond plain text matching. In ReaDirect, phoneme evidence was used as supporting data for pronunciation analysis, transcript correction, and feedback generation. It did not independently replace the system scoring rules handled by Laravel. 
Reinforcement Correction CSV 
The reinforcement correction CSV stored human-curated known recognition errors and their correct expected labels. Although the term reinforcement was used, this data source was not true reinforcement learning. It did not retrain Whisper or the isolated-letter classifier, update model weights, or automatically learn from every learner attempt. 
Instead, the correction CSV functioned as a correction memory. When the researchers observed repeated ASR errors during testing, they could record the expected label and the observed transcript error. For example, if the expected answer was Z and the ASR repeatedly returned they, that known error could be stored in the correction CSV. During runtime, the correction would apply only when the expected label and raw transcript matched the stored correction entry. 
This data source improved practical correction behavior without requiring model retraining. It also supported transparency because the correction entries were reviewable by the researchers and administrators. 
Audio Quality, Pause, and Fluency Metadata 
The system generated audio-quality metadata during audio validation. These data included duration, RMS, RMS dBFS, silence ratio, speech ratio, clipping ratio, speech segment count, warnings, uncertainty flags, and retry decisions. These metadata were necessary because poor audio quality could affect ASR output and scoring fairness. 
Pause and fluency metadata were generated for sentence reading and passage reading tasks. These included pause count, long-pause count, very-long-pause count, longest pause, total pause time, pause ratio, WPM, WCPM, pacing label, fluency score, and fluency label. These data supported oral reading evaluation because reading performance involves not only word accuracy but also rate, pacing, completion, and hesitation. 
Fluency-related measures such as WPM and WCPM are commonly used in oral reading assessment to describe reading rate and correct reading rate. In ReaDirect, these indicators were used as supporting metadata for learner feedback, teacher monitoring, and final reassessment comparison. 
ReaDirect English CRLA-style Diagnostic and Reading Comprehension Results 
The system collected ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic results from Task 1A, Task 2A, and Task 2B. These results included the number of correctly pronounced letters, correct rhyme-decision responses, and target-word reading performance in sentence context. The scores were combined to produce the ReaDirect English CRLA-style total score and initial English support level. 
The system also collected reading comprehension assessment results. This included passage reading accuracy, miscues, unread words, comprehension answers, multiple-choice answer checking, final score, and reader classification. These outputs were necessary for determining whether the learner’s English reading profile was Low Emerging Reader, High Emerging Reader, Developing Reader, Transitioning Reader, or Reading at Grade Level. For the thesis intervention scope, only learners classified as Developing readers were included as the primary target group. 
These assessment results served as the basis for module placement and teacher monitoring. The ReaDirect English support level and reading comprehension classification were combined to determine the learner’s instructional path within the system. This rule-based placement process helped ensure that each learner received a reading support path that matched the learner’s current assessment result and identified reading needs, while keeping the teacher responsible for final instructional judgment. 
Module Mastery and Reassessment Data 
Module mastery data were generated after learners completed instructional module activities. These data included module performance score, pronunciation accuracy, word reading accuracy, sentence WCPM and fluency metadata, mastery status, and system decision. The system used predefined thresholds stored in module mastery threshold records to determine whether the learner should proceed to the next module, repeat the current module, return to a previous module, or receive additional drills. 
Final reassessment data were collected after the learner completed the required instructional modules. During this phase, the system reran the phoneme reading assessment and reading comprehension assessment. The reassessment results were compared with the learner’s earlier diagnostic results to determine whether there was improvement in pronunciation accuracy, phonological awareness, reading fluency, and comprehension ability. 
Custom ReaDirect English Letter Audio Dataset 
The researchers prepared a custom English letter audio dataset to support isolated alphabet letter testing and future ASR improvement. The dataset was created because isolated alphabet letters produced frequent ASR errors during early testing. Each audio sample corresponded to one English alphabet letter from A to Z. 
The final processed dataset contained 1,300 usable English letter recordings from five adult speakers. Each speaker contributed 260 recordings covering 26 letters with repeated samples. The processed-audio evaluation reported 1,228 OK files, 72 warning-level low-volume files, and 0 critical files. Since no critical files remained, all 1,300 rows were considered usable for downstream ASR experimentation. 
All processed files were standardized as WAV audio, 16,000 Hz sample rate, mono channel, and PCM 16-bit encoding. This standardization was necessary because the speech-processing components required consistent audio preparation before inference and evaluation. Radford et al. (2023) described 16 kHz audio preparation as part of the Whisper pipeline. In ReaDirect, the same standardized format allowed recordings from different browser-supported formats to be processed consistently by Whisper, the isolated-letter classifier, and the supporting audio-analysis procedures. 
The dataset used a speaker-aware split. The training set contained speaker_001, speaker_002, and speaker_003, with 780 rows. The validation set contained speaker_004, with 260 rows. The test set contained speaker_005, with 260 rows. This supported speaker-held-out evaluation, but it also introduced a limitation because the training set contained adult male speakers while the validation and test sets contained adult female speakers. 
The custom dataset had an important limitation. It contained adult speaker recordings only and did not fully represent Grade 1 learner voices, classroom noise, varied learner accents, or real-world school recording conditions. Therefore, the dataset was suitable for controlled English letter ASR testing and correction validation, but it should not be used to claim broad generalization to child speech without further learner-based speech data collection. 
Public Speech Datasets 
The study used the custom ReaDirect English letter dataset to develop and evaluate the isolated-letter classifier. It also used controlled evaluation recordings and reference transcripts to assess Whisper transcription on word, sentence, and passage tasks. These sources were kept separate because the classifier required labeled A–Z examples, whereas the ASR component required reference text for transcript and fluency evaluation. 
The ReaDirect isolated-letter recordings represented the target A–Z classes used by the classifier and included the available speaker variations assigned to each letter. For longer reading responses, the expected words, sentences, and passages from the content bank served as reference transcripts during evaluation. This separation allowed the researchers to evaluate the two speech-processing paths according to their actual outputs: classification accuracy for letters and transcript error metrics for longer responses. 
However, public datasets alone were not enough for the needs of ReaDirect. The system needed to handle short isolated alphabet letters, known educational prompts, learner-like pronunciation patterns, and expected-centric correction cases. For this reason, the final model history combined broad speech sources with ReaDirect isolated-letter data and retained transcript correction and phoneme evidence as separate runtime safeguards. 
Miss Ciel Feedback and Agent Voice-Line Data 
The system generated additional data related to Miss Ciel’s coaching feedback and agent voice support. Feedback metadata included safe feedback context, generated feedback result, sanitized feedback result, Miss Ciel’s agent role, and response status. These records were used to verify that Miss Ciel’s feedback remained aligned with the learner’s activity result and did not replace system scoring. 
The system also used voice-line records for approved agent messages. These records included agent key, selected voice profile, text hash, cache key, and generated audio reference. They supported efficient playback of agent voice messages and helped verify that the correct generated voice line was assigned to the correct agent. The reference voices were created through ElevenLabs Voice Design, while IndexTTS was used to generate the final voice lines and emotional delivery. These records supported the lifelike agent interaction of the system but did not affect system learner scores or module progression. 
The data collection methods supported both the educational and technical requirements of ReaDirect. Learner audio recordings provided the spoken input needed for ASR, isolated-letter classification, phoneme analysis, pronunciation scoring, and fluency analysis. The ARAL-informed English content and ReaDirect content bank supplied the expected answers and prompts used for scoring and correction. Transcript fields, classifier predictions, confidence scores, correction CSVs, audio-quality metadata, diagnostic scores, comprehension results, module mastery records, feedback metadata, and learner attempt logs supported analysis, quality assurance, module placement, and system evaluation. 
Data Model Generation 
This section presents the process used to prepare, configure, evaluate, and integrate the hybrid speech-processing components of ReaDirect. The data model generation focused on audio preparation, label and transcript organization, isolated-letter classifier development, Whisper runtime configuration, component evaluation, artifact export, and FastAPI integration. 
The data model generation process was limited to the speech-recognition component of the system. It did not include ReaDirect English CRLA-style scoring, module placement, mastery progression, feedback generation, or voice-line generation because those processes were handled separately by the Laravel application and other system components. The purpose of this section is to explain how the ASR model was prepared and integrated to support learner speech recognition, especially for short utterances, words, sentences, and oral reading prompts. 
Base Checkpoint and Runtime Direction 
Whisper large-v3-turbo served as the pretrained ASR checkpoint for word, sentence, and passage transcription. The model was loaded through the Python speech service using a fixed runtime configuration so that audio preparation, language settings, output handling, and evaluation remained consistent across testing. 
The final runtime used a hybrid configuration. Whisper large-v3-turbo generated transcripts for words, sentences, and passages, while a separate isolated-letter classifier handled A–Z responses. Supporting pronunciation analysis and expected-centric correction remained separate evidence components and did not replace the primary transcript or classification output. 
Data Acquisition 
The speech data were organized according to the two runtime paths. The custom ReaDirect English letter recordings were labeled by A–Z class for classifier development and evaluation. Word, sentence, and passage recordings were paired with reference transcripts for Whisper testing, transcript alignment, WER/CER computation, and fluency analysis. 
The custom ReaDirect English letter audio dataset was prepared to address the difficulty of recognizing isolated alphabet letters. It contained 1,300 usable English letter recordings from five adult speakers, with each speaker contributing repeated samples for the 26 English alphabet letters. This dataset supported controlled testing and future improvement for alphabet-letter recognition, but it was not treated as sufficient by itself because it did not represent Grade 1 learner voices or real classroom conditions. 
The isolated-letter dataset preserved the application’s A–Z recognition needs and was divided into training, validation, and test partitions without allowing the same recording to appear in more than one split. The longer-response evaluation set used controlled words, sentences, and passages from the ReaDirect content bank with their corresponding reference transcripts. 
Dataset Organization 
After acquisition, the datasets were organized into structured manifests that paired each audio file with a corresponding transcript label. The manifest records included information such as audio path, transcript, speaker or source identifier, data source, prompt type, and dataset split. This organization allowed the researchers to track where each audio sample came from and how it was used during training, validation, and testing. 
For the custom ReaDirect English letter dataset, each recording was labeled according to its target alphabet letter. The labels were standardized to ensure consistency during training and evaluation. For public and external speech datasets, available transcripts were normalized before use so that the model received consistent audio-text pairs across different sources. 
Audio Preprocessing 
Audio preprocessing prepared recordings for classifier development, Whisper inference, and evaluation. The steps included audio loading, mono conversion, 16 kHz resampling, duration checking, silence checking, low-volume detection, and clipping inspection. These procedures reduced format differences and helped identify recordings that should be accepted, marked uncertain, or returned for retry. 
The custom ReaDirect English letter dataset was standardized as WAV audio with a 16,000 Hz sample rate, mono channel, and PCM 16-bit encoding. The processed dataset contained 1,300 usable audio files, with 1,228 files marked as acceptable and 72 files marked with warning-level low volume. Since no critical files remained, all 1,300 recordings were considered usable for controlled ASR experimentation. 
Transcript Normalization 
Transcript normalization was applied to keep text labels consistent across data sources. The normalization process included converting text to a standard case, removing unnecessary punctuation, normalizing spaces, and ensuring that transcript labels matched the expected output format of the model. This prevented formatting differences from being treated as speech-recognition differences. 
For isolated alphabet letters, transcript labels were standardized so that each recording had a clear target label. This was important because short letters such as C, Q, U, and X can be transcribed by general ASR models as spoken-word equivalents such as see, cue, you, or ex. The label preparation process ensured that the model and evaluation scripts treated these letter targets consistently. 
Data Splitting and Controlled Sampling 
The custom ReaDirect English letter dataset used a speaker-aware split. The training set contained speaker_001, speaker_002, and speaker_003 with 780 recordings, the validation set contained speaker_004 with 260 recordings, and the test set contained speaker_005 with 260 recordings. This reduced speaker leakage because validation and testing used speakers not included in the training split. 
For the isolated-letter classifier, labeled recordings were organized by letter class and split deterministically for reproducible training, validation, and testing. Class counts were reviewed so that overrepresented letters did not dominate model learning. For Whisper, reference-aligned recordings were retained as an evaluation set rather than being mixed with the letter-classifier training data. 
Model Selection 
Whisper large-v3-turbo was selected for words, sentences, and passages because it supports robust multilingual speech transcription and can be integrated locally through Python FastAPI. A separate supervised audio classifier was selected for isolated letters because single-letter utterances contain too little context for reliable general transcription. Together, the two components supported local speech processing without depending entirely on cloud-based services. 
The final FastAPI service routed each recording according to prompt type. Whisper served as the transcript generator for longer responses, while the isolated-letter classifier returned an A–Z prediction and confidence values. Supporting phoneme evidence and correction rules were used only for interpretation and did not independently control scoring. 
Hybrid Model Preparation and Runtime Configuration 
The hybrid preparation process maintained two independent model paths. The isolated-letter path used the ReaDirect English letter recordings to train and validate an A–Z audio classifier. The ASR path used the pretrained Whisper large-v3-turbo checkpoint with a controlled local inference configuration for word, sentence, and passage transcription. Keeping the paths independent prevented letter-classifier training from altering the general ASR model. 
The production runtime selected the appropriate component from the activity type. Isolated-letter prompts were sent to the classifier, while words, sentences, passages, and other longer oral-reading prompts were sent to Whisper. The selected output was then packaged with audio-quality, uncertainty, correction, and review metadata. 
During classifier development, standardized waveforms were paired with A–Z labels and optimized using a supervised classification objective. Validation monitored class-level performance and confusion among acoustically similar letters. Whisper was not fine-tuned on the letter dataset; it retained its pretrained transcription capability and was evaluated separately on the longer-response tasks used by ReaDirect. 
Model Evaluation 
After fine-tuning, the ASR models were evaluated using validation and test sets. The main metrics used were Word Error Rate and Character Error Rate. Word Error Rate measured word-level transcription errors, while Character Error Rate captured smaller character-level differences that were especially important for short utterances such as alphabet letters and short words. 
Evaluation treated the two paths according to their outputs. The isolated-letter classifier was evaluated using overall accuracy, per-letter precision, recall, F1-score, and a confusion matrix. Whisper was evaluated using WER and CER on word, sentence, and passage recordings. Runtime testing also examined audio-quality rejection, uncertainty flags, transcript correction, and end-to-end response handling. 
Model Export 
After development and evaluation, the isolated-letter classifier artifacts and configuration were exported separately from the Whisper runtime configuration. The artifacts included the classifier weights, class-label mapping, preprocessing parameters, and inference configuration required by the FastAPI service. 
The exported model artifacts were kept separate from raw recordings and evaluation manifests. The training and test data remained development resources, while only the required classifier artifacts and approved Whisper runtime files were loaded during system operation. This separation helped prevent dataset files from being confused with deployed model resources. 
Model Integration 
The hybrid speech-processing components were integrated into the ReaDirect AI service through Python FastAPI. During runtime, the service received learner audio and activity context from Laravel, preprocessed the recording, routed it to Whisper or the isolated-letter classifier, and returned structured outputs and supporting metadata. 
The production configuration explicitly identified the Whisper checkpoint, letter-classifier artifact path, language and task settings, confidence thresholds, audio-quality thresholds, and correction configuration. Explicit routing and fallback rules made the deployed behavior easier to audit and prevented the service from silently using the wrong component for an activity type. 
The model integration was limited to speech recognition and classification. Whisper generated raw transcripts, and the letter classifier generated A–Z predictions, but neither component directly assigned scores, determined module placement, decided mastery status, or classified final learner outcomes. Those decisions were handled by Laravel using the defined ReaDirect English CRLA-style scoring rules and system logic. 
Limitations of the Model Generation Process 
The model generation process had several limitations. The custom ReaDirect English letter dataset was based on adult speakers and did not fully represent Grade 1 learner voices, classroom noise, varied accents, or real-world school recording conditions. Because of this, the dataset was useful for controlled letter-recognition improvement but could not fully represent the speech patterns of young learners. 
Another limitation was the difficulty of recognizing short utterances. Isolated letters provide less acoustic context than words or sentences, making them more challenging for ASR models. Even after model refinement, some letters may still be confused with phonetically similar words or spoken-letter equivalents. For this reason, ReaDirect also used transcript correction and phoneme evidence during runtime, but those processes remained separate from the model generation phase. 
Overall, the data model generation process focused on preparing, evaluating, exporting, and integrating the hybrid speech-processing design. Whisper large-v3-turbo supported longer-response transcription, while the separate isolated-letter classifier addressed A–Z recognition. This allowed ReaDirect to support different oral-reading task types while keeping scoring and learner progression under the Laravel application layer. 
System Development Methodology 
This section discusses the system development methodology used in designing and implementing ReaDirect. The study followed Agile Prototyping and Iterative Incremental Development because the system required repeated design, testing, revision, and integration of several components, including the learner interface, ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic flow, learning modules, audio recording, ASR service, transcript correction, scoring engine, Miss Ciel feedback component, agent voice-line support, teacher dashboard, administrator monitoring, and final reassessment flow. 
Agile Prototyping was appropriate because ReaDirect was not developed as a single fixed system. The researchers needed to create early versions of the system, test individual features, identify issues, revise the design, and improve the system through repeated development cycles. Iterative development allowed the researchers to refine the system based on observed problems in learner interaction, audio recording, ASR output, scoring behavior, feedback clarity, and system usability. 
The development process was also incremental because the system was built in stages. Each stage focused on a specific group of features before being integrated into the complete system. This approach allowed the researchers to test the learner interface, audio recording, AI service communication, scoring rules, transcript correction, module progression, agent feedback, and dashboard features separately before combining them into one complete workflow. 
Requirement Analysis 
The first phase was requirement analysis. In this phase, the researchers identified the educational and technical requirements of the system. The system needed to support Grade 1 Developing readers who required assistance in English pronunciation, phonological awareness, word recognition, sentence reading, oral reading fluency, and reading comprehension. It also needed to support teachers and administrators by providing structured records, assessment results, progress monitoring, and transcript review. 
The researchers identified three main user groups: learners, teachers, and administrators. Learners needed a guided interface where they could complete diagnostic assessments, reading activities, module tasks, mastery checks, and final reassessment. Teachers needed access to learner progress, assessment results, module performance, and reading-related indicators. Administrators needed tools for user management, AI service monitoring, transcript review, testing support, and system quality assurance. 
The requirement analysis also identified the technical needs of the system. These included browser-based audio recording, audio upload and local processing, ASR transcript generation, phoneme evidence extraction, transcript correction, reading accuracy computation, ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic scoring, comprehension scoring, module placement, module mastery progression, Miss Ciel feedback generation, IndexTTS-based voice-line generation, database storage, and Laravel-to-FastAPI communication. 
System Design 
The second phase was system design. In this phase, the researchers designed ReaDirect as a separated web application and AI service. The Laravel application was designed as the main system layer responsible for authentication, role-based access, learner routing, system scoring, module progression, dashboard display, database operations, and server-side communication with the AI service. 
The Python FastAPI service was designed as the speech-processing layer. It handled audio preprocessing, Whisper inference, isolated-letter classification, supporting phoneme evidence, audio-quality validation, transcript correction, retry or uncertainty decisions, and speech-analysis metadata. This separation was necessary because the web application and AI service had different responsibilities. 
The system design also included the agent support structure. The feedback component was used to generate natural and child-friendly coaching feedback for Miss Ciel, while the voice-line support pipeline used ElevenLabs-designed reference voices and IndexTTS-generated spoken lines for Miss Vivian, Miss Ciel, and Miss Estelle. These components were included to make the reading coach interaction more lifelike and engaging for Grade 1 learners while keeping each agent’s role clearly separated. 
Prototype Development 
The third phase was prototype development. The researchers created initial versions of the learner interface, diagnostic assessment screens, module activity screens, final assessment flow, audio recording module, scoring services, and administrator monitoring tools. The prototype allowed the researchers to test whether the basic system workflow could function before adding more advanced ASR, transcript correction, and agent features. 
The early learner prototype allowed students to view prompts, record their voice, submit audio responses, and receive feedback. Teacher and administrator pages were also developed to support learner monitoring and system review. This phase helped identify issues in learner navigation, recording behavior, interface responsiveness, audio submission, and initial scoring logic. 
Web Application Development 
The fourth phase was web application development. The Laravel application was developed to manage user roles, learner accounts, assessment pages, module activities, scoring services, dashboards, and database records. The learner interface was designed to provide guided reading tasks with recording controls, prompts, feedback messages, and progress indicators. 
The teacher interface was developed to allow teachers to monitor learner performance, view assessment results, and check module progress. The administrator interface was developed to manage users, inspect transcript behavior, monitor AI service connection, review system outputs, and support quality assurance during testing. 
Audio Recording and Submission Development 
The fifth phase was audio recording and submission development. Since ReaDirect required learners to answer through speech, the system needed a browser-based recording feature. The recording interface allowed learners to record their spoken response, review or submit the recording, and continue through the assessment or module activity. 
After submission, the Laravel application prepared the required information for the AI service. This included the audio reference, expected answer, accepted answers, prompt type, activity type, and learner attempt context. The submission process was designed to ensure that each audio recording was connected to the correct learner, activity, and scoring rule. 
AI Service Integration 
The sixth phase was AI service integration. In this phase, the Laravel web application was connected to the Python FastAPI AI service. Laravel sent learner audio and activity context to the AI service, while FastAPI returned structured speech-analysis results. 
The response from the AI service included raw transcript, corrected transcript, displayed transcript, phoneme evidence, audio-quality metadata, pause metadata, correction metadata, uncertainty flags, and retry decisions. Laravel used these returned values to compute system scores, store learner records, update progress, and prepare feedback context. 
Transcript Correction Development 
The seventh phase was transcript correction development. In this phase, the researchers implemented expected-centric correction rules to reduce false rejection caused by ASR errors. Correction was applied only when the expected answer was known and when the raw transcript had sufficient evidence of being related to the expected response. 
The correction process included exact matching, text normalization, letter alias matching, known ASR confusion matching, phoneme similarity, critical phoneme detection, and reinforcement correction memory. This phase was important because speech-recognition systems may produce text outputs that are phonetically close but textually different from the expected answer. 
Scoring and Module Flow Integration 
The eighth phase was scoring and module flow integration. In this phase, the researchers implemented the scoring logic for the ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic assessment, reading comprehension assessment, module placement, mastery progression, and final reassessment. Laravel handled this system scoring and progression rules. 
After each mastery check, the system evaluated the learner’s performance score to determine whether the learner was ready to proceed, needed to repeat the same module, or had to return to an earlier skill area for reinforcement. This process helped maintain a scaffolded reading flow because learners were not moved to higher reading tasks without enough evidence of mastery. It also allowed teachers to review the learner’s module path and understand which reading skills still required additional guidance or intervention. 
Agent Feedback and Voice Integration 
The ninth phase was agent feedback and voice integration. The feedback component was integrated to generate natural, short, and child-friendly coaching responses for Miss Ciel, the reading coach agent. Reference voices were created through ElevenLabs Voice Design, and IndexTTS was used to generate spoken versions of approved agent messages for Miss Vivian, Miss Ciel, and Miss Estelle. 
This phase was necessary because the system was designed not only as a scoring tool but also as an oral reading coach. The agents needed to communicate with learners in a friendly and supportive manner. However, the system still kept scores and progression decisions under Laravel control. 
Final Reassessment and Completion Flow 
The tenth phase was final reassessment and completion flow development. After the learner completed the required English reading support modules, the system initiated a final reassessment. This reassessment reran the necessary reading assessment tasks to measure changes in pronunciation accuracy, phonological awareness, reading fluency, and comprehension ability. 
The final reassessment results were compared with the learner’s initial results and Grade 1 English reading expectations. This allowed the system to determine whether the learner improved after completing the assigned modules. The completion flow also provided a clear ending to the learner journey through a final result screen and agent completion message. 
Testing and Debugging 
The eleventh phase was testing and debugging. The researchers tested the main system functions, AI service communication, transcript correction behavior, scoring rules, audio-quality validation, module placement, mastery progression, agent feedback, voice playback, final reassessment flow, and administrator monitoring tools. 
Testing was necessary because ReaDirect combined several connected components. A change in transcript correction could affect scoring, a change in audio validation could affect retry decisions, and a change in feedback generation could affect learner guidance. Because of this, the system required repeated testing across individual functions and integrated workflows. 
Deployment Preparation 
The twelfth phase was deployment preparation. In this phase, the researchers prepared the Laravel application, PostgreSQL database, FastAPI AI service, Whisper runtime configuration, isolated-letter classifier artifacts, environment configuration, Miss Ciel feedback component, voice-line assets, and system health checks. 
Deployment preparation ensured that the different parts of the system could run together. Laravel needed to communicate with the FastAPI AI service, the AI service needed access to the configured Whisper checkpoint, isolated-letter classifier, and audio-processing tools, PostgreSQL needed to store learner records and outputs, and the voice-line support pipeline needed to provide approved spoken messages for the system agents. 
Evaluation and Refinement 
The final phase was evaluation and refinement. The researchers reviewed system outputs, transcript behavior, scoring results, audio-quality warnings, module placement decisions, mastery progression results, Miss Ciel coaching feedback messages, voice-line outputs, final reassessment outcomes, and administrator observations. These findings were used to refine the correction memory, audio validation thresholds, scoring behavior, feedback wording, interface flow, voice assignment, and testing procedures. 
Overall, the system development methodology followed a staged and iterative process. The researchers began by identifying requirements, designing the system architecture, developing prototypes, building the web application, integrating audio recording, connecting the AI service, implementing transcript correction, integrating scoring and module flow, adding Miss Ciel learner-facing coaching feedback and IndexTTS-based voice-line support, testing the system, preparing deployment, and refining the system based on observed results. 
Software Tools Used 
This section presents the software tools, programming languages, frameworks, libraries, and development utilities used in the design, development, training, integration, and testing of ReaDirect. These tools were selected based on the system’s requirements for web application development, database management, audio recording, speech recognition, transcript correction, Miss Ciel coaching feedback, voice-line support, testing, and deployment preparation. 
Model Generation and Speech Processing Tools 
The model-generation and speech-processing tools were used to prepare, train, evaluate, and integrate the hybrid speech components of ReaDirect. These tools supported audio preprocessing, isolated-letter dataset preparation, classifier training, Whisper loading and inference, phoneme support, transcript correction, and component evaluation. 
Python 
Python was used as the main programming language for the AI and speech-processing component of ReaDirect. It was used to prepare audio files, train and run the isolated-letter classifier, run Whisper inference, apply transcript correction, generate supporting phoneme evidence, and build the FastAPI AI service. 
PyTorch 
PyTorch was used as the deep learning framework for training and running the isolated-letter classifier and for executing compatible local speech-model operations. It supported tensor processing, supervised classification, model inference, checkpoint management, and evaluation. 
Hugging Face Transformers 
Hugging Face Transformers was used to access and configure the Whisper large-v3-turbo model and processor for local transcription. It provided the components required to prepare model input, run generation, decode transcript output, and integrate Whisper into the Python FastAPI service. 
Whisper large-v3-turbo Runtime Model 
Whisper large-v3-turbo was used as the Automatic Speech Recognition model for words, sentences, passages, module activities, and reassessment tasks that required transcript output. It converted standardized learner recordings into raw transcripts for alignment, fluency analysis, correction, and teacher review. 
Isolated-Letter Audio Classifier 
The isolated-letter audio classifier was used for A–Z prompts. It produced a predicted letter, confidence score, and ranked alternatives from the standardized recording. The output supported expected-label comparison and uncertainty handling but did not independently control system scoring. 
Supporting Phoneme Analysis 
The supporting phoneme-analysis component provided sound-level evidence from learner audio and expected text. It helped compare pronunciation patterns, supported safe correction and feedback, and remained separate from the primary Whisper transcript and isolated-letter classifier output. 
Audio Processing Libraries 
Audio processing libraries were used to load, convert, resample, and inspect learner audio. These tools supported mono conversion, 16 kHz resampling, duration checking, loudness measurement, silence checking, clipping detection, and audio-quality validation. 
CMU Pronouncing Dictionary and Grapheme-to-Phoneme Tools 
The CMU Pronouncing Dictionary and grapheme-to-phoneme tools were used to generate expected phoneme sequences for words and fallback pronunciations. These supported phoneme comparison, pronunciation analysis, and expected-centric transcript correction. 
Text Similarity and Alignment Tools 
Text similarity and alignment tools were used to compare expected and actual outputs. These included edit distance, Levenshtein distance, Word Error Rate, Character Error Rate, sentence alignment, phoneme distance, and similarity scoring. 
FastAPI and Uvicorn 
FastAPI was used to build the AI service that connected the Laravel application to the speech-processing pipeline, while Uvicorn was used to run the FastAPI service. Together, they allowed Laravel to send audio-analysis requests to the Python service and receive structured ASR metadata. 
System Development Tools 
The system development tools were used to create the main ReaDirect web application, database, learner interface, dashboards, agent feedback, voice support, and development workflow. These tools supported the implementation of the system as a functional educational software application. 
Laravel and PHP 
Laravel was used as the main backend framework of ReaDirect, while PHP served as the server-side programming language. Laravel handled authentication, role-based access, learner routing, assessment flow, system scoring, module placement, mastery progression, final reassessment flow, database operations, and communication with the AI service. 
PostgreSQL and Laravel Eloquent ORM 
PostgreSQL was used as the database management system of ReaDirect. Laravel Eloquent ORM was used to interact with PostgreSQL through models and relationships, making learner records, attempts, modules, results, feedback records, and user roles easier to organize and maintain. 
Vue.js, Inertia.js, Tailwind CSS, Vite, HTML, CSS, and JavaScript 
Vue.js, Inertia.js, Tailwind CSS, Vite, HTML, CSS, and JavaScript were used to build the learner, teacher, and administrator interfaces. These tools supported responsive layouts, recording controls, dashboard pages, feedback displays, frontend asset compilation, and interactive page behavior. 
MediaRecorder API and Web Audio API 
The MediaRecorder API and Web Audio API were used to capture learner audio through the browser and support recording-related behavior such as recording controls, playback, and possible audio-level monitoring. 
IndexTTS Voice-Line Generation and Agent Voice Support 
IndexTTS was used to generate approved spoken agent voice lines, while the voice-line support pipeline connected the generated audio assets to the main system. The reference voices used in generation were created through ElevenLabs Voice Design from text prompts, and IndexTTS supplied the final delivery and emotional tone of the voice lines for Miss Vivian, Miss Ciel, and Miss Estelle. 
Development, Dependency, and Testing Tools 
Visual Studio Code, Composer, Node.js, npm, Python virtual environments, Git, GitHub, and testing tools supported code editing, dependency management, frontend asset building, Python environment isolation, version control, debugging, and validation. 
Overall, the software tools used in ReaDirect supported development from speech-component preparation to web application implementation. Python, PyTorch, Hugging Face Transformers, Whisper large-v3-turbo, the isolated-letter classifier, phoneme tools, and audio-processing libraries supported speech processing. Laravel, PHP, PostgreSQL, Vue.js, Inertia.js, Tailwind CSS, JavaScript, browser audio APIs, the Miss Ciel feedback component, IndexTTS, and the voice-line support pipeline supported the educational web application and learner-facing interaction. 
System Architecture 
This section presents the system architecture of ReaDirect. The system was designed as a separated, service-based architecture composed of a Laravel web application, PostgreSQL database, Python FastAPI AI service, deterministic Miss Ciel feedback component, and IndexTTS-generated voice-line support for spoken agent messages. This structure was used because ReaDirect required both educational web application functions and AI-assisted speech-processing functions. 
This architecture also improved system maintainability because each service had a specific responsibility within the ReaDirect workflow. Through this design, updates to the AI-ASR pipeline, feedback generation, voice-line support pipeline, or web application could be managed separately without affecting the entire system. It also helped ensure that learner data, assessment results, and AI-generated outputs were processed in an organized and traceable manner. This helped support a more reliable implementation of ReaDirect as both an English oral reading support system and an AI-assisted assessment tool. 
The overall structure of the system is shown in Figure 10. The figure presents the relationship among the learner, teacher, and administrator interfaces, the Laravel web application, PostgreSQL database, FastAPI speech service, Whisper runtime, isolated-letter classifier, deterministic Miss Ciel feedback component, and approved agent voice-line support. 

Figure 10 . ReaDirect System Architecture and AI-ASR Runtime Flow 
As shown in the figure, the learner, teacher, and administrator access the system through a web browser. The browser communicates with Laravel, which controls authentication, learner flow, scoring, records, dashboards, and service communication. PostgreSQL stores learner records, transcript and classification fields, scores, module progress, feedback records, and reassessment results. The FastAPI service validates audio and routes it to Whisper or the isolated-letter classifier before applying supporting evidence and correction procedures. The Miss Ciel feedback component and approved voice-line assets support learner interaction without controlling scores. 

Figure 10.1. Learner Input and Audio Capture 
Figure 10.1 presents the learner input and audio capture stage of the ReaDirect full system architecture. In this stage, the learner accesses the system through the web browser and responds to the assigned reading task using the device microphone. The system captures important activity details such as the task context, expected text, learner interaction, attempt number, and audio input before sending them to the backend for processing. This stage serves as the starting point of the entire speech-processing workflow because the recorded learner response becomes the basis for transcript generation, scoring support, feedback, and progress monitoring. It also ensures that the learner’s oral reading activity is connected to the correct prompt and assessment context before the system proceeds to validation and analysis. 

Figure 10.2. Upload Validation and Context Building 
As shown in this figure shows the upload validation and context building stage handled by the Laravel application layer. In this part, Laravel checks the uploaded audio, verifies the activity context, prepares the learner record, and organizes the necessary information before communicating with the AI-ASR service. This ensures that the audio request contains the correct learner, activity, prompt, and expected-answer details needed for transcript generation and scoring support. 

Figure 10.3. Audio Quality Check and ASR Routing 
This figure presents the audio quality check and ASR routing stage. The validated audio is sent to the FastAPI ASR service, where the system checks whether the recording has enough speech, acceptable duration, and usable audio quality. If the recording is valid, the system routes it to the ASR processing pipeline; if not, the learner may be asked to record again. 

Figure 10.4. Hybrid Speech Processing and Transcript Refinement 
Figure 10.4 presents the hybrid speech-processing and refinement stage. The FastAPI service receives validated learner audio and routes it according to the task type. Whisper large-v3-turbo produces raw transcripts for longer responses, while the isolated-letter classifier produces A–Z predictions and confidence values. Audio-quality metadata, expected-answer rules, aliases, known confusions, and supporting phoneme evidence are then used to preserve, safely correct, or mark the response uncertain before structured results are returned to Laravel. 

Figure 10.5. Response Normalization, Storage, and Final Output 
This figure presents the response normalization, storage, and final output stage. After the ASR service returns the processed result, Laravel normalizes the response, stores the learner record in PostgreSQL, and updates transcript fields, scores, feedback records, module progress, and reassessment data. This final stage connects the speech-processing output to the learner interface, teacher dashboard, Miss Ciel feedback component, and approved voice-line support for Miss Vivian, Miss Ciel, and Miss Estelle. 
Client Layer 
The client layer consisted of the learner, teacher, and administrator interfaces accessed through a web browser. Learners used this layer to complete diagnostic assessments, record spoken responses, answer reading activities, complete modules, receive feedback, listen to agent messages, and take final reassessment. Teachers used the interface to view learner progress, assessment results, reading performance, and module status. Administrators used the interface to manage users, inspect transcripts, monitor system behavior, and support quality assurance. 
The learner interface was designed to be simple, guided, and child-friendly because the target users were Grade 1 learners. It displayed prompts, recording controls, visual feedback, spoken guidance, activity status, and progress indicators. The teacher and administrator interfaces contained more detailed records because these users needed to monitor learner performance and review system outputs. 
Laravel Application Layer 
The Laravel application layer served as the main controller of ReaDirect. It handled authentication, role-based access, learner routing, assessment flow, module progression, final reassessment, dashboard display, database operations, and communication with external services. Laravel also controlled the system scoring workflow of the system. 
Laravel computed ReaDirect English CRLA-style scores, reading accuracy, comprehension scores, final reading score, module placement, module mastery decisions, and reassessment results. This was important because AI-generated transcripts, Miss Ciel feedback, and voice-line messages were support components only. They helped the system analyze speech and interact with learners, but they did not independently control learner outcomes. 
Database Layer 
The database layer used PostgreSQL as the main storage system. It stored user accounts, learner profiles, teacher records, administrator records, audio attempt references, raw transcripts, corrected transcripts, displayed transcripts, scores, diagnostic classifications, reader classifications, module progress, mastery decisions, feedback records, voice-output references, and final reassessment results. 
The database also supported teacher and administrator monitoring. When learners completed activities, Laravel stored the results in PostgreSQL. Teachers and administrators could later retrieve these records through dashboard pages. This made learner progress persistent, organized, and reviewable. This also allowed teachers to monitor learner performance over time and identify which learners needed additional reading support or reassessment. 
AI Service Layer 
The AI service layer was developed using Python FastAPI. It served as the speech-processing component of ReaDirect. When a learner submitted an audio response, Laravel sent the audio reference and activity context to the FastAPI service. The service then processed the recording and returned structured speech-analysis results to Laravel. 
The AI service performed audio loading, mono conversion, 16 kHz resampling, duration checking, low-volume detection, silence checking, clipping inspection, Whisper inference, isolated-letter classification, supporting phoneme analysis, expected-centric transcript correction, pause analysis, retry flagging, and uncertainty detection. The response returned to Laravel included transcript or letter-prediction fields, confidence information, audio-quality metadata, correction metadata, pause metadata, retry flags, and uncertainty indicators. 
Hybrid Speech Recognition and Classification Components 
The hybrid speech component was responsible for converting learner audio into a transcript or an isolated-letter prediction. Whisper large-v3-turbo handled words, sentences, and passages, while the A–Z classifier handled isolated letters. Each direct output was preserved for review before expected-centric interpretation and Laravel scoring were applied. 
The raw transcript was not used blindly. It was reviewed through expected-centric correction, phoneme evidence, audio-quality metadata, and Laravel scoring rules. This was necessary because learner recordings may contain short utterances, low volume, classroom noise, or phonetically similar words. These conditions can affect ASR output and may cause false rejection if the raw transcript is used without correction or review. 
Phoneme Evidence and Transcript Correction Component 
The phoneme evidence component supported pronunciation analysis by comparing expected sound patterns with observed learner speech. This was useful for alphabet pronunciation, rhyming responses, word reading, and short responses where plain text transcription may not be reliable. 
The transcript correction component corrected ASR outputs only when the expected answer was known and when the correction was supported by evidence. It used exact matching, letter aliases, known ASR confusions, phoneme similarity, and reinforcement correction memory. The system preserved raw_transcript, corrected_transcript, and displayed_transcript separately to maintain transparency and support administrator review. This separation is important because the system can show the original ASR output while still allowing evidence-based correction when the learner’s response is likely recognized incorrectly. It also helps prevent the system from automatically changing a response without support from phoneme evidence, aliases, or known correction rules. Through this process, ReaDirect can make transcript interpretation more transparent, fair, and useful for teacher review. 
Scoring and Progression Component 
The scoring and progression component was handled by Laravel. It used the AI service outputs, expected answers, learner responses, and predefined scoring rules to compute system results. This component included ReaDirect English CRLA-style scoring, reading accuracy computation, comprehension scoring, module placement, module mastery progression, and final reassessment classification. 
This component ensured that learner outcomes were based on deterministic scoring rules rather than uncontrolled AI-generated responses. The AI service provided speech-analysis data, but Laravel made the system decisions about scores, classifications, assigned modules, mastery status, and final learner results. 
Miss Ciel Feedback Component 
The Miss Ciel feedback component generated natural and child-friendly coaching feedback for Miss Ciel, the reading coach agent of ReaDirect. It produced short feedback messages based on safe system context such as the learner’s activity result, error type, and current reading task. This component helped make Miss Ciel’s coaching response more conversational while keeping the response connected to the learner’s actual performance. 
The feedback generated for Miss Ciel helped make the reading coach more conversational and lifelike. However, the feedback component did not control scores, module placement, mastery progression, or final reassessment results. Laravel first computed the system result, then the system used the result as context for Miss Ciel’s feedback message. This separation ensured that the feedback remained supportive and personalized while the evaluation stayed rule-based, consistent, and verifiable. 
Voice-Line Support Component 
The voice-line support component used ElevenLabs-designed reference voices and IndexTTS-generated audio lines to provide spoken agent messages. The generated voice-line assets made the agents more lifelike and accessible for Grade 1 learners while keeping voice output separate from scoring and learner progression. 
The voice-line component converted approved agent messages into spoken audio during asset preparation. Miss Vivian provided assessment guidance, Miss Ciel provided reading-coach feedback, and Miss Estelle provided evaluator or completion messages. Although voice-line support was needed for the intended lifelike agent experience, it did not control scoring, assessment logic, module progression, or final reassessment. 
System Workflow 
The system workflow began when a learner opened an assessment or module activity. Laravel displayed the prompt and recording interface. The learner recorded a spoken response, and the audio was submitted to Laravel. Laravel stored the audio and sent the audio reference, expected answer, accepted answer, prompt type, and activity context to the FastAPI AI service. 
The FastAPI service processed the audio and returned speech-analysis results. Laravel then used the returned data to compute the system score, store the result in PostgreSQL, update the learner’s progress, and prepare the next system action. If feedback was needed from Miss Ciel, the system sent safe context to the Miss Ciel feedback component. If spoken agent output was needed, the system retrieved or served the corresponding approved voice-line message. 
For diagnostic assessment, the system computed the learner’s initial English reading classification and module placement. For module activities, the system computed module performance and mastery status. For final reassessment, the system reran the required assessment flow and compared the learner’s updated results with the target English reading support outcomes. 
Overall, the ReaDirect architecture separated educational workflow control from AI-assisted speech processing. Laravel served as the main application and scoring layer, PostgreSQL served as the database layer, and FastAPI served as the speech-processing layer. Whisper generated transcripts for longer responses, the isolated-letter classifier generated A–Z predictions, supporting phoneme evidence assisted pronunciation comparison, and transcript correction reduced false rejection. The feedback and approved voice-line components supported learner interaction while keeping system decisions rule-based and reviewable. 
Software Testing 
The testing procedure of ReaDirect covered both the software application and the AI-assisted speech-processing component. Since the system included learner, teacher, and administrator interfaces, Laravel scoring services, PostgreSQL storage, FastAPI ASR processing, transcript correction, Miss Ciel feedback generation, and voice-line output, the testing process required multiple levels of evaluation. Figure 11 presents the overall software testing and evaluation framework used to validate the major components of the system. This framework helped the researchers check whether each component could function correctly on its own and still work properly when connected to the complete ReaDirect workflow. It also ensured that the testing process considered not only transcript accuracy, but also scoring reliability, data storage, user interaction, feedback delivery, and system stability. 

Figure 11 . Software Testing and Evaluation Framework 
As shown in this figure, the testing process included functional testing, integration testing, ASR model testing, audio-quality testing, transcript correction testing, scoring validation, usability testing, security and access testing, and regression testing. The succeeding discussion first presents the broader software testing coverage, followed by the ASR model testing procedure using WER and CER. 
To make the software testing procedure broader than ASR performance measurement, the researchers evaluated ReaDirect through several testing categories. The testing covered the learner workflow, teacher dashboard, administrator tools, Laravel scoring services, PostgreSQL data handling, FastAPI AI-ASR service, transcript correction, Miss Ciel feedback generation, voice-line output, and final reassessment flow. The purpose of this testing was to verify whether each component worked individually and whether the complete workflow functioned properly when connected. 
This testing approach helped confirm that ReaDirect could process learner activities from recording to scoring without breaking the system flow. It also allowed the researchers to check whether errors in one component, such as audio quality or transcript correction, could affect the accuracy and reliability of the final learner result. Through this evaluation, the researchers were able to determine whether the system could support both learner-facing activities and teacher-facing monitoring in a stable and consistent manner. 
Overall, the testing framework helped demonstrate that ReaDirect was evaluated as a complete English reading support system and not only as an ASR-based transcription tool. It also helped confirm that the results shown to teachers were supported by stored records, scoring rules, and system-generated metadata. This made the testing process useful for checking both the technical performance of the system and its practical role in classroom English reading support. 
In addition, the framework shows that testing was organized into application testing, AI-ASR testing, and system quality testing so that each part of ReaDirect could be checked based on its specific function. Application testing focused on the main system features and scoring flow, while AI-ASR testing focused on speech recognition, audio validation, and transcript correction. System quality testing helped determine whether the system was usable, secure, and stable after changes were made. Through this structure, the researchers were able to identify possible errors, refine weak components, and improve the reliability of the system before final evaluation. 
This also helped ensure that the testing process followed the actual sequence of system use, from learner recording to teacher review. By testing the system in this manner, the researchers were able to check whether the outputs remained accurate, traceable, and consistent across different user roles and system functions. The results of these tests served as the basis for determining whether ReaDirect was ready for evaluation and classroom-based use. 
Table 11 presents the software testing procedures and components evaluated in the study.

Functional testing was conducted by running the major learner, teacher, and researcher-admin workflows from start to finish. The researchers checked whether learners could log in, complete diagnostic tasks, record spoken responses, submit attempts, receive feedback, proceed through modules, complete mastery checks, and take the final reassessment. The teacher dashboard was also checked to determine whether learner records, scores, module progress, and reassessment results were displayed properly. 
Integration testing was conducted because ReaDirect was composed of several connected components. The researchers verified whether Laravel could send learner audio and activity context to the FastAPI AI service, whether the AI service could return structured ASR outputs, whether PostgreSQL could store the resulting learner records, whether the Miss Ciel feedback component could generate Miss Ciel’s coaching feedback, and whether the voice-line support pipeline could produce or serve the correct spoken agent messages. 
Audio-quality testing and transcript correction testing were included to prevent unfair scoring caused by technical recording problems or unsafe ASR correction. The researchers tested cases involving low volume, silence, clipping, very short recordings, incomplete fragments, known ASR confusions, letter aliases, homophones, phoneme similarity, and critical phoneme conflicts. These tests helped verify whether the system marked uncertain attempts properly and whether correction was applied only when supported by safe evidence. 
Scoring validation was conducted to check whether Laravel remained the scoring and progression controller. The researchers verified the ReaDirect English CRLA-style diagnostic score, reading accuracy score, comprehension score, final reading classification, module placement decision, module mastery decision, and final reassessment result using prepared test cases and expected outcomes. This ensured that ASR, feedback messages, and voice-line output supported the workflow without independently controlling learner scores or progression. 
Usability testing, security and access testing, and regression testing were conducted to support the reliability of the full system. Usability testing focused on the clarity of learner screens, recording steps, teacher monitoring, and feedback presentation. Security and access testing checked whether learner, teacher, and researcher/admin roles were properly separated. Regression testing was conducted after revisions to ensure that changes in ASR processing, correction rules, scoring logic, dashboards, feedback, or voice output did not introduce new errors in previously working workflows. 
The following discussion presents the ASR model testing component in detail because transcript accuracy directly affects transcript review, correction behavior, and scoring support. This focus was necessary because any error in ASR output could influence how the system interprets the learner’s response before feedback and progress records are generated. 
Speech Model Testing 
The speech-model testing component evaluated the two runtime paths used in ReaDirect. Whisper large-v3-turbo was evaluated by comparing generated transcripts with reference transcripts using WER and CER. The isolated-letter classifier was evaluated using overall accuracy, per-letter precision, recall, F1-score, and a confusion matrix. Separating the metrics was necessary because transcript generation and A–Z classification produce different kinds of outputs. 
For Whisper testing, the evaluation set contained word, sentence, and passage recordings with reference transcripts. For isolated-letter testing, the held-out set contained labeled A–Z recordings that were not used during classifier training. Runtime tests used the same audio preprocessing, routing rules, confidence thresholds, and correction safeguards configured for the FastAPI service. This allowed the researchers to measure both component-level performance and end-to-end behavior under the intended deployment configuration. 
Word Error Rate 
Word Error Rate, or WER, was used as the primary metric for evaluating ASR transcript accuracy. It measures the number of word-level errors produced by the ASR model compared with the reference transcript. These errors include substitutions, deletions, and insertions. A lower WER indicates better word-level recognition performance. 
Equation 10. Word Error Rate 
Equation 10 measures word-level transcript error in the ASR model testing phase. 
In ReaDirect, WER was useful for evaluating word-level and sentence-level transcription because it showed how many words were incorrectly recognized, omitted, or inserted by the ASR model. This was important because the system included word reading, sentence reading, passage reading, and reassessment tasks where the accuracy of the recognized words affected transcript review and scoring support. 
Character Error Rate 
Character Error Rate, or CER, was used as a supporting metric for measuring transcript error at the character level. CER compares the predicted transcript with the reference transcript by counting character-level substitutions, deletions, and insertions. A lower CER indicates better character-level recognition performance. 
Equation 11. Character Error Rate 
Equation 11 measures character-level transcript error in the ASR model testing phase. 
CER was included because ReaDirect involved short speech responses such as alphabet letters and short words. In these cases, a small character difference may affect whether the output matches the expected response. For example, short utterances such as C, Q, U, and X may be recognized as spoken-word equivalents or phonetically similar outputs. CER helped provide a finer view of transcript differences that may not be fully described by word-level error alone. 
The actual Whisper validation and test WER and CER values, together with the isolated-letter classifier’s accuracy, per-class metrics, and confusion matrix, are reported in Chapter IV. Chapter III is limited to the testing procedure, evaluation sets, runtime configuration, and formulas used to measure the performance of each speech-processing path.
