Concept Paper 

A. Basic Information 

Project Title: 
ReaDirect: AI-Based Speech Recognition–Assisted Oral Reading Coach as a Learning Support Intervention for Grade 1 Learners Using the DepEd ARAL Program 

Topic: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), Oral-Reading Coach, DepEd ARAL Program, Progressive Web Appholy Deployment (PWA), WebRTC Voice Activity Detection (VAD), RNNoise, Wav2Vec2, Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC), Beam Search, Word Error Rate (WER), 
Correct Words Per Minute (WCPM), Pause Duration Detection, Rule-based tutoring, Template-based Natural Language Generation (NLG), Talk to Speech Tacotron2 (TTS), HiFi-GAN vocoder 

Proponents 
Jerick E. Mendez 
Nick Narry S. Mendoza Victor P. De Mesa Jr. 

B. Technical Description Literacy serves as the cornerstone of early education in the Philippine basic education system, particularly for Grade 1 learners who must develop foundational reading skills including phonological awareness, letter-sound recognition, word decoding, and proper pronunciation to succeed throughout Key Stage 1. The COVID-19 pandemic severely disrupted face-to-face learning, resulting in widespread reading deficiencies among early grade learners and increasing the number of non-readers and struggling readers in elementary schools, as evidenced by assessments like the Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment (CRLA) and Philippine Informal Reading Inventory (Phil-IRI). 
In response to these learning gaps, the Philippine government enacted Republic Act No. 12028, establishing the Academic Recovery and Accessible Learning (ARAL) Program, with DepEd Order No. 18, Series of 2025 providing implementing guidelines that emphasize targeted literacy interventions through structured modules progressing from basic to complex reading tasks. While ARAL modules offer essential instructional materials, teachers face significant challenges in delivering individualized reading instruction due to large class sizes and multiple responsibilities, limiting opportunities for repeated guided practice in pronunciation, phonological awareness, and oral reading fluency. 
Due to these persistent literacy challenges and limitations in traditional ARAL delivery, different studies have attempted to identify technology-based solutions to enhance reading interventions for early grade learners. These studies demonstrate how speech recognition and automated feedback systems can provide the individualized practice that classroom constraints often prevent. 
A related study by Perci et al. (2024) entitled “ICOMPREHEND: Exploring a Multimodal Approach for Enhancing Reading Comprehension Through Question Generation and Recognition Technologies” 

developed a web-based system that integrates AI-driven question generation and cognitive-affective state detection to support reading comprehension. The system utilized a T5 Transformer for generating comprehension questions and ResNet50V2 for detecting learners’ cognitive load and emotional states. Results showed that the best-performing model produced fluent, contextually relevant questions, achieving high scores in BLEU, ROUGE, and METEOR metrics, as well as strong human evaluation ratings in answerability, relevance, and clarity. 
The study highlights the effectiveness of combining automated question generation with adaptive learner feedback to improve engagement and comprehension. Although focused on higher-level reading, its findings are relevant to early literacy, emphasizing the importance of integrating comprehension checks and adaptive support. In relation to the proposed study, these concepts support the use of AI-driven feedback systems, where speech recognition can be used to assess oral reading while incorporating simple comprehension prompts, thereby enhancing both reading accuracy and understanding among Grade 1 learners. 
The study of Tomas, M. J. L., Villaros, E. T., & Galman, S. M. A. (2021) entitled "The perceived challenges in reading of learners: Basis for school reading programs" found that most Grade 1 to Grade 7 learners operated at the frustration reading level in both Filipino and English, far below grade expectations, with many remaining nonreaders particularly in lower grades due to weak phonological awareness, poor alphabet knowledge, phonics deficiencies, limited vocabulary, poor fluency, and low comprehension. The researchers identified three major causes including non-mastery of reading elements like sound blending and word recognition, presence of learners-at-risk such as nonreaders with health issues, special needs without support, low reading interest, absenteeism, and untrained teachers, alongside absence of reading culture marked by scarce materials, insufficient guidance, weak parent-teacher partnerships, and poor program monitoring. Because of these findings, the study proposed strengthening school reading support through Literacy Programs targeting the "Big Six" areas (oral language, phonological awareness, phonics, vocabulary, fluency, comprehension), Individual Reading Recovery via one-on-one tutoring and progress monitoring, and Enrichment Programs with reading centers, contextualized materials, readathons, daily reading time, teacher training, and intervention recognition, concluding that immediate structured programs based on actual learner needs are essential. 
Another study authored by Bai, Y., Hubers, F., Cucchiarini, C., & Strik, H. (2020) entitled "ASR-based evaluation and feedback for individualized reading practice" proves that Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) can effectively evaluate children's oral reading by detecting pronunciation errors, delivering individualized feedback, and driving improvements in accuracy and fluency, as shown with 39 Grade 1 pupils where repeated readings post-feedback significantly reduced errors and boosted speed. The research measured reading accuracy, speed, post-feedback performance, and error detection, revealing ASR's ability to pinpoint problematic words, log progress for teacher tracking, and enable independent practice that alleviates teacher workload in large classes. This establishes three validating concepts for the proposed system: automated evaluation through phoneme analysis is feasible for children's speech, immediate feedback enhances pronunciation and accuracy via repetition to mastery, and technology enables individualized practice for struggling readers, perfectly complementing ARAL modules. 
With the problems identified in terms of widespread reading deficiencies among Grade 1 learners and the limitations of teacher-led ARAL implementation, the proposed AI-based speech recognition–assisted oral reading coach directly addresses these gaps by providing scalable, interactive feedback through progressive phases from phonological awareness to fluent reading. The system integrates ARAL inputs to generate precise outputs like error-specific reports, supporting data-driven teaching and nationwide literacy recovery as outlined in DepEd guidelines. This positions the coach as a feasible supplementary intervention for non-readers, struggling readers, and Alternative Learning System participants needing foundational reading support. 
Statement of the Problem: 
Grade 1 learners in Philippine public elementary schools, particularly those under the DepEd ARAL Program, face significant reading deficiencies with many classified as non-readers and struggling readers due to weak phonological awareness, pronunciation errors, and poor oral reading fluency as shown by CRLA and Phil-IRI assessments (Cañete & Eswan, 2025; Avergonzado & Polinar, 2025). Although RA 12028 and DepEd Order No. 18 s. 2025 provide ARAL modules for literacy recovery, teachers cannot deliver individualized feedback due to large classes and time constraints (Creagh et al., 2023; Kitonga et al., 2024). With the help of AI speech recognition technology, this study will address the following problems: 
What is the profile of Grade 1 non-readers/struggling readers in phonological awareness and reading fluency? How can speech recognition be integrated with ARAL modules for a 3-phase reading coach system? What is the accuracy of AI speech analysis for detecting Filipino Grade 1 pronunciation errors? How does the AI coach improve reading proficiency vs. traditional ARAL instruction? What are teachers' and learners' perceptions of the AI reading coach's usability and effectiveness? Objectives: General and Specific General Objective: 
The study aims to design and develop an AI-based speech recognition–assisted oral reading coach as a learning support intervention for Grade 1 learners within the DepEd ARAL Program, addressing the literacy crisis of non-readers and struggling readers identified through CRLA and Phil-IRI assessments. 
Specifically, it aims to: 
To design a three-phase oral reading intervention module ("Knowing My Strength in Reading" for phonological awareness and letter-sound recognition, "Knowing My Ability in Reading" for phrase-level guided reading proficiency, and "Knowing Myself to Become the Best Learner" for sentence fluency and comprehension) precisely aligned with DepEd Order No. 18 s. 2025 ARAL Program modules and Grade 1 MATATAG Curriculum reading competencies. To develop and integrate an automatic speech recognition (ASR) component utilizing advanced phoneme analysis algorithms to capture, process, and evaluate learners' oral reading of ARAL materials (letters, CVC words, phrases, simple sentences) while detecting specific pronunciation errors, phonemic inaccuracies, and reading fluency patterns in real-time for Filipino child speech. To implement adaptive feedback mechanisms that deliver immediate corrective guidance, targeted repetition prompts until mastery, visual/audio pronunciation models, and progressive difficulty adjustment based on detected phonological and prosodic error patterns, enabling independent practice that compensates for teachers' classroom constraints. To evaluate the effectiveness of the AI-based oral reading coach through controlled pre-post assessments measuring statistically significant improvements in reading accuracy (word recognition), phonological awareness (sound blending/segmentation), and oral reading fluency (words correct per minute) across all three phases compared to traditional ARAL instruction. To determine teachers', reading coordinators', and school administrators' perceptions regarding the system's technical usability, pedagogical acceptability, scalability potential, and instructional value as a supplementary ARAL intervention through validated survey instruments and focus group validation. To assess the system's generalizability and potential to support diverse beneficiaries including Grade 1 learners, identified struggling readers/non-readers, out-of-school youth, adult literacy learners, and Alternative Learning System (ALS) participants requiring structured foundational reading remediation aligned with national literacy recovery priorities. How did others solve the problem? 
Reading Comprehension / Literacy Improvement 
The researchers describe the use of an Intelligent Tutoring System (ITS) to improve reading comprehension in primary and secondary education. A systematic literature review was used to analyze existing ITS-based reading-comprehension studies, and an experimental method was employed to evaluate the proposed system in contexts where learners study in their native language. Results showed that ITS-based instruction was more effective than traditional teaching approaches, especially for students with lower reading proficiency, indicating that AI-tutoring can support struggling readers and enhance reading-comprehension development. (Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITS) to Improve Reading Comprehension: A Systematic Review, H. Atun, 2020) The researchers investigated Problem-Based Learning (PBL) to improve reading comprehension of informational texts among elementary-level students. A Classroom Action Research (CAR) design based on the Kemmis and McTaggart model—comprising planning, action, observation, and reflection—was used to implement PBL in a grade-level classroom. Findings showed that student mastery increased from 47% in the pre-cycle to 68.42% in Cycle I and 94.7% in Cycle II, while the class average improved from 78.42 to 88.31, indicating that PBL significantly enhanced students’ understanding of informational texts, critical thinking, interactive learning, and collaborative problem solving. (Enhancing Elementary Students' Reading Comprehension of Informational Texts Through Problem-Based Learning, S. Juliati et al., 2025) The researchers aimed to evaluate the reading performance of Grade 1 learners and identify the causes of reading difficulty to develop a remedial reading program. A descriptive-survey design was used involving 70 Grade 1 learners, 7 parents, and 3 teachers; the Comprehensive Rapid Literacy Assessment (CRLA) and survey questionnaires were administered to assess reading performance. Results revealed that 84% of learners were “full refreshers” and needed major reading support, with key causes including inadequate reading materials, limited parental support at home, and insufficient time for reading practice. (Reading Performance of Grade 1 Learners: Basis for Remedial Program, S. Cañete & J. Eswan, 2025) The researchers investigated how early exposure to English (L2) affects phonological awareness in Chinese (L1) among young bilingual learners using behavioral tests and neuroimaging (fNIRS). The findings revealed that bilingual children showed enhanced phonological processing mechanisms, with evidence of L2-to-L1 transfer, where learning English contributed to improved phonological awareness in their mother tongue. Although behavioral differences between bilingual and monolingual learners were not yet significant at Grade 1, bilingual learners demonstrated greater brain activation in regions associated with phonological manipulation, indicating early cognitive advantages that support literacy development. These results suggest that learning a second language can positively influence and strengthen first language reading skills, particularly in phonological awareness. ( Neural substrates of L2-L1 transfer effects on phonological awareness in young Chinese-English bilingual children, Kou et al., 2024 ) The researchers examined the impact of mother tongue transfer on Chinese students’ English reading and writing by reviewing existing theories and empirical studies on language transfer. The findings revealed that first language skills significantly influence second language literacy, with strong phonological and syntactic abilities in the mother tongue positively contributing to English reading comprehension and word recognition. The study also found that similarities between the first and second language result in positive transfer, while differences may lead to errors in writing and grammar. Overall, the results indicate that the mother tongue serves as a foundation for second language development, where strong L1 proficiency enhances L2 literacy, while weak L1 skills may limit effective learning of English. ( The Impacts of Mother Tongue Transfer on Chinese Students’ English Reading and Writing, Xie, 2023 ) Advanced ASR / Model-Based 
The researchers systematically examined the effect of Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR)-based tools on ESL/EFL pronunciation through a meta-analysis of multiple experimental studies. Data were aggregated from various research projects testing ASR-supported pronunciation practice among second-language learners, and effect sizes were computed to compare ASR-based interventions against control conditions. Results showed that ASR-based tools significantly improved pronunciation accuracy and overall speaking ability, with particularly strong effects when feedback was targeted and specific. (The effectiveness of automatic speech recognition in ESL/EFL pronunciation: A meta-analysis, T. Ngo, H. Chen, & K. Lai, 2023) 
The researchers designed and tested an oral pronunciation correction system using speech recognition and AI technologies to enhance learners’ speaking and pronunciation skills. The study followed a system-design and experimental-testing approach, implementing speech-recognition and speech-synthesis modules, along with preprocessing, feature extraction, and template-matching algorithms to compare learner speech against standard pronunciation models. Experimental results indicated high recognition accuracy—up to 97.755% for males and 95.208% for females—under controlled conditions, and the system successfully delivered real-time corrective feedback, demonstrating its suitability as an automated pronunciation-training tool. (Speech recognition sensors and artificial intelligence automatic evaluation application in English oral correction system, W. Jing, 2024) The researchers developed a gamified mobile app for pronunciation training to support Computer-Assisted Language Learning (CALL) using AI and speech-recognition technology. The system was specifically designed for children, including those with Speech Sound Disorder, and for non-native learners of Swedish and Finnish. A speech-recognition-based engine was embedded in a mobile game, where learners produced utterances that the models evaluated and fed back to them in a playful context. The setup worked well as both a recognizer and evaluator within the game, providing accurate feedback while maintaining learner engagement through gamified elements. (Developing an AI-Assisted Low-Resource Spoken Language Learning App for Children, Y. Getman et al., 2023) The researchers explored the use of Google Translate’s ASR to provide corrective and confirmative feedback for second-language learners’ pronunciation. An experimental design was used, in which Quebec Francophone speakers produced English words beginning with “th,” “h,” and various vowels embedded in both predictable and unpredictable sentence contexts; their recordings were fed into Google Translate’s ASR to assess transcription accuracy and the system’s ability to distinguish correct from incorrect pronunciations. Results showed that ASR was most accurate for correct pronunciations in predictable contexts, enabling confirmative feedback, and performed well at detecting errors in less predictable ones, highlighting its potential as a pronunciation-assessment and feedback tool in language learning. (Exploring automatic speech recognition for corrective and confirmative pronunciation feedback, P. John, C. Johnson, & W. Cardoso, 2025) 
The researchers investigated an ASR-based language-learning system named “I Can Speak” to improve English pronunciation among learners. An experimental study was conducted in which participants used the system to practice spoken English, with their utterances analyzed by an ASR engine that provided targeted, immediate feedback. Results indicated that learners showed clear improvements in pronunciation accuracy and overall speaking ability after using the system, suggesting that ASR-driven platforms can serve as reliable tools for targeted pronunciation practice and language-skills development. (“I Can Speak”: improving English pronunciation through automatic speech recognition-based language learning systems, M. Bashori et al., 2024) The researchers examined the extent to which commercial ASR (IBM Watson) can be used to assess reading skills in primary-school children. The methodology involved 22 French-speaking and 26 Italian-speaking children who completed two reading-related tasks: word and pseudoword decoding, and initial phoneme deletion. The ASR system was adapted by uploading prompt and target lists into IBM Watson’s language model and fine-tuning parameters such as customization weight and background-noise suppression. Performance was analyzed using word edit distance and error categories; results showed that ASR could reliably classify correct vs. incorrect responses for words and pseudowords, although it had difficulty detecting fine-grained phoneme-level errors, leading the authors to recommend a two-pass approach with a specialized mispronunciation detector to reduce clinician workload. (Using Commercial ASR Solutions to Assess Reading Skills in Children: A Case Report, T. Piton et al., 2023) The researchers explored the adaptation of Whisper, a large-scale supervised Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) model, for child speech recognition, addressing the challenge of limited child speech datasets. An experimental research design was employed using multiple datasets (MyST, PFSTAR, CMU Kids, and LibriTTS), where Whisper models were evaluated in both zero-shot and fine-tuned settings and compared with self-supervised wav2vec2 models. Results showed that Whisper models without finetuning performed poorly on child speech, but finetuning significantly reduced Word Error Rate (WER), improving recognition accuracy across datasets. However, wav2vec2 models finetuned on child speech generally outperformed Whisper, especially on datasets with similar distributions, while Whisper demonstrated better generalization to unseen datasets. The study concludes that finetuning is essential for improving child speech recognition and that both supervised and self-supervised approaches have distinct advantages depending on the application context. ( Adaptation of Whisper Models to Child Speech Recognition, A. Barcovschi et al., 2023) The researchers investigated the effectiveness of wav2vec2, a self-supervised learning (SSL) model, in improving automatic speech recognition (ASR) for child speech, addressing the challenge of limited annotated child speech data. An experimental research design was employed using multiple configurations of pretraining and finetuning with combinations of adult and child speech datasets (e.g., MyST, PFSTAR, CMU Kids, LibriSpeech, and LibriLight). Results showed that models pretrained on large adult speech datasets and finetuned with even small amounts of child speech significantly improved performance, achieving Word Error Rates (WER) as low as 7.42 on MyST, 2.91 on PFSTAR, and 12.77 on CMU Kids. Findings also revealed that as little as 10 hours of child speech could outperform models trained with hundreds of hours of adult speech, and that combining multiple child datasets yielded the best results, highlighting the importance of domain-specific data in enhancing child speech recognition accuracy. ( A WAV2VEC2-Based Experimental Study on Self-Supervised Learning Methods to Improve Child Speech Recognition, A. Barcovschi et al., 2022 ) The researchers explored how self-supervised adult speech representations, particularly wav2vec 2.0, can be fine-tuned to improve automatic speech recognition (ASR) for children’s speech despite limited child-specific data. Using an experimental approach, they trained models on three child speech corpora (MyST, OGI, and TLT) and evaluated performance across native and non-native speech, data quantity, and cross-domain datasets. Results showed that fine-tuning significantly improves performance, with cross-domain training yielding the best results—achieving up to 46.08% relative improvement for native speech and 45.50% for non-native speech compared to adult baseline models. The study also found that as little as 5 hours of child speech can outperform models trained on 960 hours of adult speech, emphasizing the efficiency of self-supervised learning. However, performance varies depending on data distribution, age, and speech type, with younger children and spontaneous speech remaining more challenging. Overall, the findings highlight that leveraging adult pretrained models with targeted child data is an effective strategy for overcoming data scarcity in children’s speech recognition. ( Improving Children’s Speech Recognition by Fine-tuning Self-supervised Adult Speech Representations, Lu et al., 2022 ) The researchers proposed a two-stage approach to detect mispronunciations in children’s sentence reading by first segmenting speech into candidate word pronunciations and then classifying each as correct or incorrect. Using a dataset of European Portuguese children (ages 6–10), the system accounts for common reading disfluencies such as repetitions, false starts, intra-word pauses, and phonetic deviations. The segmentation stage uses a syllable-based decoding strategy that allows pauses within words, improving alignment accuracy compared to traditional word-based methods. The classification stage relies on multiple acoustic and phonetic features, with the most effective being a log-likelihood ratio comparing the expected pronunciation to a free phone-loop model, along with features like goodness of pronunciation (GOP) and Levenshtein distances between expected and recognized phoneme sequences. Results show that combining multiple features significantly improves detection performance, reducing miss rates by up to 18% at a fixed false alarm rate of 5%. The system achieves low word error rates (around 2–2.17%) and performs close to manual segmentation, demonstrating its effectiveness for applications such as reading assessment and automated tutoring. ( Mispronunciation Detection in Children's Reading of Sentences, J. Proença et al., 2018 ) Application-Based / Learning Tools 
The researchers investigated the use of the ELSA Speak application, an Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR)-based tool, to supplement and improve English pronunciation skills among university students. A Classroom Action Research (CAR) design was employed, involving cycles of planning, action, observation, and reflection, with data collected through pronunciation tests, interviews, questionnaires, and observations. The study was conducted with 18 English Education students, and results showed a steady improvement in pronunciation performance across cycles, with average scores increasing from 60 in the preliminary stage to 72.7 in Cycle 1, 75 in Cycle 2, and 80 in Cycle 3, as shown in the performance tables on pages 7–9. Findings also revealed that students were highly motivated and benefited from features such as instant feedback and error correction, which helped them improve accuracy, fluency, and comprehensibility. Overall, the study concludes that ELSA Speak is an effective and engaging tool for enhancing pronunciation skills and supporting autonomous learning in language education. ( Elsa Speak App: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) for Supplementing English Pronunciation Skills, A. Kholis, 2021 ) The researchers examined the integration of artificial intelligence (AI), including automatic speech recognition (ASR) and generative AI chatbots, in three iOS pronunciation applications, namely ELSA Speak, Loora, and Vocal Image, to evaluate their effectiveness in supporting pronunciation learning. Using a qualitative review approach, each application was tested over a week, analyzing features such as pronunciation feedback, chatbot interaction, and learning affordances. Findings revealed that while AI-enabled features such as color-coded Goodness of Pronunciation (GOP) scores and conversational role-play enhance learner engagement and provide opportunities for autonomous practice, the feedback is often inaccurate and largely limited to segmental features like consonants and vowels. The study also found that ASR systems frequently misinterpret connected speech, including reductions and linking, which may confuse learners, as illustrated by incorrect feedback examples in Figures 1 to 4 on pages 6 to 10. Additionally, generative AI chatbots support extended communicative practice but lack reliable assessment of pronunciation features such as intonation and rhythm. Overall, the study concludes that AI-powered pronunciation apps are useful as supplementary tools but should be used cautiously due to their limitations in accuracy and pedagogical depth. ( Artificial Intelligence Integration in Three iOS Pronunciation Apps, D. Kaiser, 2025 ) Teacher / Educational System Support 
The researchers explored how heavy teacher workloads interfere with the implementation of digital literacy programs in public primary schools. A descriptive-survey design was used, targeting headteachers, teachers, and Grade 1 pupils in Kitui West Subcounty, Kenya; data were collected through questionnaires, interviews, and focus-group discussions and analyzed using statistical tools such as two-sample t-tests and percentages. Findings indicated that high workloads significantly reduced teachers’ time for preparing and integrating ICT tools into instruction, thereby slowing down the adoption and effectiveness of digital literacy initiatives. (Teacher Workload and Implementation of Digital Literacy Programme in Public Primary Schools in Kitui West Subcounty, Kenya, M. Kitonga et al., 2024) The researchers conducted a systematic research synthesis on teacher workload, work intensification, and time poverty across school settings. Existing studies on workload and intensification were reviewed to identify common patterns and to understand how added duties and administrative tasks affect teachers’ capacity to perform core teaching roles and implement new educational programs. Results showed that intense workload pressures and time constraints reduced teachers’ attention to teaching-related activities and hindered the adoption of new strategies and technologies, underlining the negative impact of overload on innovation in classrooms. (Workload, work intensification and time poverty for teachers and school leaders: a systematic research synthesis, S. Creagh, G. Thompson, N. Mockler, M. Stacey, & A. Hogan, 2023) The researchers investigated the effectiveness of targeted early reading interventions in improving the reading performance of Grade 1 learners in the San Miguel District, Bohol, Philippines. A descriptive research design with a quantitative pretest-posttest approach was employed, involving 515 pupils and 27 teachers, using the Early Grade Reading Assessment (EGRA) tool and survey questionnaires. Results showed significant improvements across multiple reading components after the interventions, with orientation to print increasing from 58% to 94.5%, letter name knowledge from 12% to 76.2%, and reading comprehension from 1.6% to 44.3%, as shown in the table on page 1. Statistical analysis further confirmed that the differences between pretest and posttest scores were significant (p < 0.05), indicating that the interventions effectively enhanced reading performance. However, no significant relationship was found between pupil score gains and the frequency of intervention use, suggesting that effectiveness depends more on quality than frequency of implementation. The study concludes that diverse and well-implemented reading strategies significantly improve early literacy skills among young learners. ( Reading Performance of Grade I Learners through Targeted Early Reading Interventions, R. Avergonzado & C. Polinar, 2025 ) The researchers conducted a 5-year longitudinal study to examine the development of reading skills and their predictors among Italian-speaking children from Grade 1 to Grade 5. The findings revealed that reading skills, including fluency and accuracy, develop linearly over time in both typical readers and children with reading difficulties. The study identified phonological awareness and verbal short-term memory as significant predictors of reading development, with phonological awareness influencing both reading fluency and accuracy, while memory skills contributed to reading accuracy. Additionally, early reading performance at the end of Grade 1 was found to be a strong predictor of later reading outcomes, explaining a substantial portion of variance in reading proficiency. These results highlight the importance of early cognitive skills and early intervention in supporting long-term reading development. ( Development and predictors of reading skills in a 5-year Italian longitudinal study, Mascheretti et al., 2024 ) Patents 
The researchers invented a method and apparatus for reading education that presents written text on a screen and prompts the learner to read it aloud, using speech recognition to analyze the spoken response against a grammar tailored to the displayed passage. The system compares the recognized utterance with the original text to detect mispronunciations, skipped or repeated words, and computes multiple performance metrics (e.g., words read per minute, words read correctly, total reading time, word- or phrase-level confidence) that are aggregated into a fluency score. Stored feedback includes pause durations and highlighted problem words, enabling teachers to review progress and re-analyze the same audio under different grammars or settings, supporting fully automated assessment of reading fluency and accuracy. (Method and apparatus for reading education, W. J. Byrne et al., US8226416B2, 2012) The researchers designed a comprehensive literacy system that integrates speech recognition into a computer-based curriculum spanning phonology, semantics, syntax, and text-level reading. 
How do you intend to solve the problem? 
The proponent of the study plans to address the literacy challenges among Grade 1 learners under the ARAL Program by developing an AI-assisted oral reading coaching system capable of analyzing learner speech and providing automated pronunciation and reading feedback. The system captures learners’ oral responses through a device microphone while they read letters, words, or sentences from ARAL-aligned instructional materials. The captured audio is processed using modern speech recognition and phonological analysis algorithms to detect pronunciation accuracy, phoneme-level articulation errors, and reading fluency indicators. These detected patterns allow the system to deliver adaptive feedback that supplements teacher instruction while enabling individualized practice. The overall architecture of the proposed AI reading coach follows a multi-pipeline design consisting of diagnostic assessment pipelines, adaptive learning pipelines, and a reassessment pipeline that measures reading improvement. Each pipeline represents a structured sequence of algorithms that process learner speech input and convert it into interpretable literacy performance metrics. Figure 1 illustrates the first diagnostic pipeline used to assess phonological reading skills. 

Figure 1. Phoneme Reading Assessment Pipeline 
The phoneme reading assessment pipeline evaluates a learner’s ability to correctly pronounce letters, recognize rhyming words, and read words within short sentences. The assessment begins with Task 1, where the learner is asked to pronounce ten letters individually. Each correctly pronounced letter is assigned one point, giving a maximum possible score of ten points. The scoring procedure and task distribution rules for this stage are summarized in Table 1. If the learner scores between zero and six points, the learner proceeds to Task 2A and Task 2B for further phonological assessment. Task 2A requires the learner to produce rhyming words for ten prompts, while Task 2B requires the learner to read ten words embedded within a sentence context. Each correct response in these tasks is assigned one point. However, if the learner scores between seven and ten points in Task 1, Task 2A is automatically assigned a full score because the learner is assumed to already demonstrate adequate phonological awareness. The learner then proceeds directly to Task 2B. After completing the required tasks, the scores from Task 1, Task 2A, and Task 2B are combined, producing a maximum possible score of thirty points. The interpretation of the total score and the corresponding intervention level classification are shown in Table 2. 
The system delivers interactive exercises through a graphical interface, allowing learners to respond by typing or speaking, with spoken responses interpreted by speech recognition software and mapped to appropriate exercise answers. The curriculum adapts subsequent exercises based on stored performance data, creating a personalized learning path, and the multimodal interaction (visual, auditory, and spoken) supports sustained practice and engagement, particularly for learners with limited typing or pointing skills. (Literacy system, J.  Blank, US8016596B2, 2011) 

During pronunciation evaluation, the learner’s speech is captured through a device microphone and processed using WebRTC Voice Activity Detection to identify segments containing speech signals. The captured audio frames are then filtered using RNNoise neural noise reduction to remove background noise typically present in classroom environments. Following noise filtering, root-mean-square loudness normalization is applied to standardize audio amplitude and ensure consistent input quality for speech processing. The normalized audio is passed through a Wav2Vec2 transformer-based speech encoder that converts the waveform signal into contextualized speech embeddings. These embeddings are decoded through a Connectionist Temporal Classification phoneme decoder to predict the phoneme sequence spoken by the learner. The predicted phoneme sequence is then aligned with the expected phoneme output generated through a grapheme-to-phoneme conversion model, which converts the displayed letters or words into their phonological representation. Viterbi forced alignment synchronizes the predicted phonemes with the expected sequence in order to determine pronunciation accuracy. To quantify articulation quality, the system computes a Goodness of Pronunciation score and calculates phoneme differences using Levenshtein phoneme distance, which identifies substitutions, insertions, and deletions within the spoken response. These calculations allow the system to determine whether the learner produced the correct pronunciation or rhyme response and generate a structured phonological performance score. Figure 2 illustrates the second diagnostic pipeline. 

Figure 2. Reading Comprehension Assessment Pipeline 
The second diagnostic pipeline evaluates the learner’s ability to read short passages and demonstrate comprehension through question answering. The learner is first asked to read a passage containing fifty words within a one-minute time limit. The expected average reading time for the passage is forty-five seconds. If the learner exceeds the one-minute limit, any remaining words that were not read before the time limit are automatically counted as incorrect. The timing rules and passage parameters for the reading task are presented in Table 3. 
Table 3. Reading Passage Timing Rules 
Parameter 
Value 
Description 

Passage Length 
50 words 
Standardized reading passage 

Expected Reading Time 
45 seconds 
Average reading duration 

Maximum Allowed Time 
60 seconds 
Reading stops at this limit 

Unread Words 
Counted as incorrect 
Words not reached within 1 minute 

During reading, the learner’s speech is captured through the device microphone and processed using WebRTC Voice Activity Detection to isolate active speech segments. The audio signal is then filtered using RNNoise neural noise reduction and normalized using loudness normalization to ensure consistent signal quality. The processed audio is encoded using the Wav2Vec2 transformer encoder to extract speech features and decoded through a Connectionist Temporal Classification decoder to generate a predicted sequence of spoken words. A beam search language model is applied to refine the predicted word sequence by selecting the most probable transcription based on Grade 1 vocabulary probabilities. The predicted text is then compared with the expected passage using Levenshtein word alignment to detect reading miscues, including substitutions, omissions, and mispronunciations. These miscues, together with any words not reached within the one-minute limit, are counted as incorrect words as described in Table 4. 
Table 4. Miscue and Error Counting 
Error Type 
Description 
Count Rule 

Mispronunciation 
Word spoken incorrectly 
Count as 1 incorrect word 

Substitution 
Incorrect word spoken instead 
Count as 1 incorrect word 

Omission 
Word skipped 
Count as 1 incorrect word 

Unread Word 
Word not reached before 1 minute 
Count as 1 incorrect word 

Reading accuracy is then computed based on the total number of incorrect words in the passage. Since the passage contains fifty words, each word represents two percent of the total score. The formula used 
to calculate reading accuracy is shown in Table 5, where each incorrect word subtracts two percent from the total possible accuracy score of one hundred percent. After completing the reading task, the learner answers five comprehension questions related to the passage. The learner’s responses are processed through text normalization procedures and evaluated using Sentence-BERT semantic embeddings combined with cosine similarity to determine whether the learner’s answer is semantically similar to the expected answer. The final reading classification is determined by combining the learner’s reading accuracy within one minute and the number of comprehension questions answered correctly. The classification criteria for reader levels are summarized in Table 6. 

After completing the diagnostic assessments, the system determines the learner’s starting instructional level using a module placement decision pipeline. The results produced by the phoneme reading assessment and reading comprehension assessment are combined and analyzed using a rule-based decision tree that evaluates phonological awareness, word recognition performance, and comprehension ability which is shown in Table 7. Based on these combined results, the system assigns the learner to the most appropriate instructional module within the AI oral reading coach. This placement mechanism ensures that learners receive targeted instruction appropriate to their current reading ability, preventing both excessive difficulty and insufficient challenge during the learning process. Figure 3 shows the first learning module pipeline. 
Table 7. Module Placement Rules 

Figure 3. Letter and Sound Learning Pipeline 
The first learning module focuses on developing foundational phonological awareness by guiding learners to correctly articulate letters and their corresponding sounds. Learners are presented with letter prompts and asked to produce the corresponding sound. The learner’s speech is then processed through voice activity detection, RNNoise neural noise reduction, and loudness normalization to obtain a cleaner speech signal. The normalized audio is passed to the Wav2Vec2 encoder and then to the CTC phoneme decoder to generate predicted phoneme outputs. These predicted phonemes are aligned with the expected phoneme representation through Viterbi phoneme alignment. Pronunciation performance is then assessed using goodness of pronunciation and Levenshtein phoneme distance to identify possible articulation errors. Based on this evaluation, a rule-based phoneme tutor determines the appropriate corrective response. The feedback is then generated through template-based natural language generation, synthesized using Tacotron2 text-to-speech, and finally rendered as speech through the HiFi-GAN vocoder. Figure 4 illustrates the second learning module pipeline. 

Figure 4. Word Reading Learning Pipeline 
The second learning module focuses on improving word recognition and pronunciation accuracy. Learners are presented with individual word prompts and asked to read them aloud. The learner’s speech is processed through WebRTC voice activity detection, RNNoise neural noise reduction, and loudness normalization before being encoded by the Wav2Vec2 encoder and decoded by the CTC decoder. The decoded output is then refined through a beam search language model, and reading accuracy is measured using word error rate calculation and Levenshtein word distance. Pronunciation quality is further evaluated using goodness of pronunciation. Based on the results, a rule-based word tutor selects the appropriate corrective feedback. The feedback is then generated through template-based natural language generation and synthesized into speech using Tacotron2 TTS and HiFi-GAN. Figure 5 shows the third learning module pipeline. 

Figure 5. Sentence Reading and Fluency Learning Pipeline 
The third learning module focuses on developing sentence-level reading fluency. Learners are presented with short sentence prompts and asked to read them aloud. The learner’s speech is processed through WebRTC voice activity detection, RNNoise neural noise reduction, and loudness normalization before being encoded by the Wav2Vec2 encoder and decoded by the CTC decoder. The decoded output is then refined through a beam search language model, and reading accuracy is measured using word error rate calculation. Fluency is further assessed through words correct per minute and pause duration detection. Based on these results, a rule-based fluency tutor generates the appropriate feedback. The feedback is then produced through template-based natural language generation and synthesized into speech using Tacotron2 TTS and HiFi-GAN. Table 8 shows the module master progression and reassessment rules. 
Table 8. Module Progression and Reassessment Rules 


After completing the learning activities within each instructional module, learner performance metrics are evaluated through a module mastery progression engine to determine whether mastery has been achieved. The system collects module performance metrics including pronunciation accuracy, word reading accuracy, and sentence reading fluency. These metrics are aggregated to compute an overall module performance score representing the learner’s proficiency within the completed module. 
The computed score is then evaluated using a rule-based mastery decision model that references predefined thresholds stored in the dataset module_mastery_thresholds.csv. As summarized in Table 6, the decision rules determine whether the learner should progress to the next instructional module, repeat the current module for additional practice, or return to a previous module for remediation. For example, learners who achieve high mastery scores progress to the next module, while learners with moderate scores repeat the module to reinforce learning. Learners who score below the minimum threshold are redirected to earlier modules or assigned additional phoneme drills to strengthen foundational reading skills. 
Once the learner successfully completes all instructional modules, the system initiates a final reassessment loop. During this phase, the system reruns the Phoneme Reading Assessment Pipeline (Pipeline 1) and the Reading Comprehension Assessment Pipeline (Pipeline 2) to evaluate improvements in pronunciation accuracy, phonological awareness, reading fluency, and comprehension ability. The results of these reassessments are then compared with Grade 1 literacy benchmarks to verify whether the learner has achieved the target reading outcomes, specifically Grade Ready phonological proficiency and Reading at Grade Level comprehension performance. Figure 6 illustrates the conceptual framework of our study 
The conceptual framework of the study illustrates the overall structure and interaction of the components within the proposed AI-assisted oral reading coaching system. As shown in the framework, the input consists of ARAL literacy materials, Grade 1 reading prompts such as letters, words, and short sentences, and learner speech responses captured through the device microphone. These inputs are processed through a series of system components that analyze learner reading performance. The process stage begins with diagnostic assessment pipelines, which include the phoneme reading assessment and reading 

Reading comprehension assessment. These pipelines analyze learner speech using speech processing, phoneme recognition, and semantic analysis techniques to determine pronunciation accuracy, word reading performance, and comprehension ability. Based on the results of these assessments, the module placement engine assigns the learner to the most appropriate instructional module. The learner then proceeds through a sequence of adaptive learning modules, including letter and sound learning, word reading practice, and sentence reading and fluency training. Learner performance within each module is evaluated through a module mastery progression engine that applies rule-based thresholds to determine whether the learner should advance, repeat the module, or return to earlier lessons for remediation. After completing the instructional modules, the system initiates a final reassessment by rerunning the diagnostic assessment pipelines to measure improvements in reading performance. The output of the system consists of computed literacy performance metrics such as pronunciation accuracy, reading fluency, comprehension scores, and final reader level classification indicating whether the learner has achieved Grade Ready phonological proficiency and Reading at Grade Level comprehension ability. Through this framework, the system demonstrates how AI-based speech analysis and adaptive instruction can support individualized reading development among Grade 1 learners. 
Target Users/Beneficiaries 
Grade 1 Learners (Struggling Readers and Non-Readers) Primary beneficiaries identified through CRLA/Phil-IRI assessments as struggling readers or non-readers. The AI coach provides individualized pronunciation practice for ARAL materials, guiding them through three phases from basic phonological awareness (sounds/letters) to oral reading fluency (sentences) with immediate feedback that addresses classroom teacher constraints. 
Out-of-school Youth Dropouts and working youth needing literacy recovery. PWA deployment enables flexible, self-paced access on personal smartphones without internet dependency, supporting RA 12028 dropout recovery goals through ARAL content delivered in short, achievable practice sessions. 
Adults with Basic Literacy Needs Functional illiterate adults requiring foundational reading skills for employment or community participation. The system accommodates mature learners through simplified interface, self-paced progression, and workplace-appropriate practice times while maintaining ARAL pedagogical structure. 
Alternative Learning System (ALS) Participants ALS learners balancing work/studies who need structured reading intervention. The coach provides systematic phonics instruction and progress monitoring typically unavailable in flexible ALS sessions, ensuring alignment with DepEd literacy recovery standards while accommodating non-traditional schedules. 
requirements. PWA deployment eliminates device costs while generating compliance documentation for regional validation and funding allocation. 
To Out-of-school Youth 
Extends ARAL intervention through flexible PWA access on personal smartphones, supporting RA 12028 dropout recovery goals with self-paced modules accommodating working learners' schedules while maintaining pedagogical rigor. 
To Adults with Basic Literacy Needs 
Provides workplace-accessible reading remediation for functional illiterate adults, supporting poverty alleviation through literacy while maintaining ARAL curriculum alignment for mature learners. 
To Alternative Learning System (ALS) Participants 
Delivers structured phonics instruction and progress monitoring typically unavailable in flexible ALS sessions, ensuring DepEd literacy recovery standards alignment while accommodating non-traditional schedules. 
To AI Education Technology Research 
Demonstrates successful Filipino child ASR adaptation (Bai et al., 2020) within Philippine infrastructure constraints. PWA + offline ASR establishes feasibility for low-resource contexts, contributing implementation data for global child speech recognition research. 
To National Literacy Policy 
Provides controlled evidence of AI impact on pandemic-induced reading loss, supporting DepEd 
technology integration roadmap and validating RA 12028 intervention framework through scalable delivery model.
